By the 15th century, the Italian Renaissance had spread to other European countries, leading to the rise of the Northern Renaissance. - Key Figures of the Northern Renaissance - Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536) - The central figure of the Northern Renaissance. - A renowned scholar-publicist, known as the “Prince among Humanists.” - Combined literary and scholarly pursuits with deep religious devotion. - Notable Work: - The Praise of Folly – a satirical critique of different social classes, exposing their corruption and immorality while emphasizing the simple teachings of the Gospels. - Sir Thomas More (1478–1535) - Close friend of Erasmus, second only to him in humanist distinction. - Notable Work: - Utopia – described an ideal society on an imaginary island, highlighting the abuses and injustices of his time. - Architecture in Northern Europe - The Gothic style dominated Northern European architecture. - Characterized by: - Pointed arches - External buttresses - Large windows - Notable Structures: - Reims Cathedral, France - Louvre Museum, France - Renaissance Music - Music, along with painting and sculpture, became a brilliant aspect of Renaissance creativity during the 15th and 16th centuries. - Musical Innovations - Development of polyphony – a technique that harmonized multiple individual melodies. - Introduction of new musical instruments, such as: - Violin - Double bass - Lute - Harpsichord - Role of Music in Society - Paid musicians and singers became an integral part of churches and court life. - Folk songs and music entered mainstream European culture. - The Northern Renaissance not only embraced humanism and artistic achievements but also made remarkable contributions to architecture and music, shaping European culture for centuries. - Scientific Inventions of the Renaissance - The Renaissance not only revolutionized art, literature, and philosophy but also stimulated scientific knowledge, leading to groundbreaking discoveries that transformed the Western world. - Printing Press - The most significant invention of the period was the printing technology. - Origins: - 12th century – Arabs introduced movable printing techniques from China (developed in the 11th century). - 14th century – Johannes Gutenberg (Germany) improved the technique. - First Printed Works: - Papal letters of indulgence (satisfaction/fulfillment/state of being) - A version of the Bible - Impact of Printing: - Books became cheaper than handwritten manuscripts. - Reduced copying errors. - Allowed mass production of books, leading to the spread of knowledge. - Advances in Medicine - The invention of printing enabled the wide publication of medical books with anatomical illustrations, advancing medical skills. - Dissection and Anatomy - Partial lifting of the 13th-century ban on human dissection led to direct observation of the human body. - Universities of Padua and Ferrara became renowned for medical studies. - Key Figures: - Andreas Vesalius – published On the Structure of the Human Body, based on human dissection. - Gabriello Fallopio – conducted early studies on the human fetus and described the fallopian tube. - Pharmacology and Surgery - Paracelsus (Swiss physician): - Challenged Galen’s theory that disease was caused by imbalances in bodily fluids. - Proposed chemical remedies for specific diseases. - Ambroise Paré (French surgeon): - Revolutionized surgery by developing stitches to seal blood vessels, replacing the painful hot iron cauterization method. - Astronomy - The Renaissance era brought major breakthroughs in the understanding of the cosmos. - Copernican Revolution - 1543 – Nicolas Copernicus (Poland) published Six Books Concerning the Revolutions of the Heavenly Spheres. - Challenged the geocentric (Earth-centered) model of the universe proposed by Ptolemy. - Proposed the heliocentric (Sun-centered) model, stating: - The Earth revolves around the Sun, rather than the other way around. - This contradicted Church teachings and the traditional cosmic order. - - - Further Developments in Astronomy - Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) – Compiled the most accurate astronomical observations. - Johannes Kepler (1571–1630) – Used Brahe’s data to: - Support the heliocentric model. - Prove that planetary orbits are elliptical, not circular (On the Motion of Mars, 1609). - Galileo Galilei (1564–1642) – Developed the telescope, enabling him to: - Discover mountains on the Moon and sunspots. - Observe Jupiter’s moons, proving celestial bodies orbit objects other than Earth. - His findings contradicted Church teachings, leading to his condemnation and house arrest (Dialogues on the Two Chief Systems of the World, 1632). - - Isaac Newton and the Law of Gravitation - Isaac Newton (1642–1727) expanded on Galileo’s work and solved key planetary motion problems. - 1687 – Published The Mathematical Principles of Natural Philosophy, introducing: - The Law of Gravitation – all celestial bodies move due to mutual attraction. - Combined mathematics and physics in astronomy. - Similar principles were explored earlier by Varahamihira and Aryabhatta in 5th–6th century India. - Calendar Reform - 16th century – Increasing astronomical knowledge led to the reform of the Julian Calendar. - 1582 – Pope Gregory XIII introduced the Gregorian Calendar: - Adjusted the solar year by moving the calendar back by 10 days. - Leap years were revised – only centenary years divisible by 400 were considered leap years. - This Gregorian Calendar was widely adopted worldwide and is still in use today. - The Renaissance scientific revolution laid the foundation for modern science through printing innovations, medical discoveries, astronomical breakthroughs, and calendar reforms, marking a new era in human knowledge and progress. - The Rise of Protestantism - Awareness of Corruption in the Catholic Church - The European people, with a renewed spirit of inquiry, recognized the corruption within the Catholic Church. - The term Protestant was first used for Martin Luther’s followers in 1529 when they protested against restrictions on religious reforms. - Over time, Protestant came to describe all Christians who rejected Papal supremacy. - The major branches of Protestantism in the 16th century were Lutheranism, Calvinism, and Anglicanism. - Martin Luther and the Birth of Lutheranism - Martin Luther was born in 1485 in Germany to a peasant family. - His father sent him to study law at the University of Erfurt, but he abandoned it to become a monk in the Order of the Hermits (a person living in religious solitude) of St. Augustine. - In 1512, he earned his doctorate in theology at Wittenberg. - Luther’s Criticism of the Church - A visit to Rome exposed him to the corruption of the Catholic Church. - He believed that salvation came not from sacraments (religious ceremonies in christian church) or “good works” but through faith in God’s grace (smoothness/elegance). - He strongly opposed the sale of indulgences, which allowed people to buy forgiveness for sins. - The Ninety-Five Theses (1517) - On 31 October 1517, Luther nailed his Ninety-Five Theses to the Church door in Wittenberg. - He also printed and distributed them, gaining support from common people, merchants, and princes. - The Elector of Saxony protected him against charges of heresy. - Key Teachings of Martin Luther - The civil government is superior to the Church in political matters. - Priesthood is unnecessary, as every Christian can connect with God directly. - The Bible should be accessible to all, leading him to translate it into German. - He officially broke away from the Catholic Church and organized his followers into a new Church, founding Lutheranism. - Spread of Protestantism - Zwingli and Calvin in Switzerland - Ulrich Zwingli and John Calvin led the Protestant movement in Switzerland. - Calvin’s Swiss cities became a refuge for persecuted Protestants from Western Europe. - Calvin founded an academy to train Protestant missionaries. - He wrote The Institutes of the Christian Religion, a concise explanation of Protestant beliefs. - The Protestant Reformation in England - Led by King Henry VIII and Queen Elizabeth I. - Unlike Luther and Calvin, their reforms were driven by political interests rather than religious or social ideology. - - Henry VIII’s Break with the Catholic Church - Henry VIII wanted to divorce Catherine of Aragon to marry Anne Boleyn. - The Pope refused, as Henry had received a Papal dispensation (exemption from a rule / distribution / supply) allowing his first marriage. - In response, Henry declared himself the head of the Church of England and married Anne Boleyn. - Their daughter, Elizabeth I, later became Queen of England. - Formation of the Anglican Church - In 1529, England’s Parliament passed laws ending Papal authority over the English Church. - The English Church was renamed the Anglican Church, with the King as its head. - Side information on history of England's parliamentary system 1. Magna Carta (1215) - Signed by King John under pressure from barons. - Limited the powers of the king and laid the groundwork for a council to advise the monarch—an early form of Parliament. 2. Model Parliament (1295) - King Edward I summoned what is considered the first representative Parliament. - Included commoners (knights and burgesses) along with clergy and nobles. 3. Development of the Bicameral System (1300s) - By the 14th century, Parliament had divided into two houses: - House of Lords (nobles and clergy) - House of Commons (elected representatives) 4. English Civil War and Execution of Charles I (1642–1649) - Power struggle between the monarchy and Parliament. - Resulted in temporary abolition of monarchy and rise of Oliver Cromwell. 5. Glorious Revolution (1688) - Parliament invited William III and Mary II to rule, forcing them to accept the Bill of Rights (1689). - This event firmly established Parliament’s supremacy over the monarchy, marking a shift toward constitutional monarchy. 6. Development of the Prime Minister Role (1700s) - Sir Robert Walpole (1721–1742) is considered the first de facto Prime Minister. - Gradually, the executive (government) became responsible to Parliament, especially the House of Commons. Final Transition to Parliamentary Democracy - By the 19th and 20th centuries, especially after the Reform Acts (1832, 1867, 1884) and full adult suffrage (by 1928), the UK had evolved into a modern parliamentary democracy. - Conclusion - The Protestant Reformation was not a single event but a series of movements across Europe. - Lutheranism, Calvinism, and Anglicanism emerged as the dominant Protestant sects. - It reshaped the religious and political landscape of Europe, reducing the power of the Catholic Church. - The Counter-Reformation - The Protestant Reformation, led by Luther, Zwingli, and Calvin, severely weakened the authority of the Roman Catholic Church. - To counter the damage, the Catholic Church initiated a series of reforms known as the Counter-Reformation. - Key Measures of the Counter-Reformation - The Council of Trent (1545) - Summoned by Pope Paul III to combat Protestantism. - Focused on three main objectives: - Resolving doctrinal disputes between Catholics and Protestants. - Eliminating corruption and administrative abuses within the Church. - Launching a new crusade against Muslims. - The Index of Prohibited Books was created, barring Catholics from reading certain texts. - The Formation of the Jesuits - A new order of missionaries, the Jesuits, was founded. - Led by Ignatius Loyola, a Spanish soldier who experienced a religious transformation. - The Jesuits were dedicated to spreading the message of Christ worldwide. - Revival of the Inquisition - The Inquisition (church court) was revived to suppress heresy (belief or opinion contradictory to orthodox religious). - What was the Inquisition? - It was essentially a religious court established in the 13th century. - Operated under Papal authority. - It investigated, interrogated, and punished individuals accused of heresy. - Operated across Europe and the Americas to enforce Catholic orthodoxy. - Impact of the Counter-Reformation - The Catholic Church failed to regain full control over Europe. - However, it successfully checked the spread of Protestantism in many regions. - While much of Europe remained Protestant, new lands overseas were converted to Catholicism. - Geographical Explorations and Discoveries - The Renaissance sparked a spirit of inquiry and adventure, leading to increased interest in geographical explorations. - European merchants and rulers financed voyages to discover new lands, sea routes, and trade opportunities. - Factors Facilitating Exploration - The invention of the mariner’s compass helped in navigation on the high seas. - The astrolabe allowed sailors to determine latitude. - Improved maps and guidebooks provided better knowledge of routes. - European Powers Leading the Explorations - Spain and Portugal were the first European nations to finance voyages. - Portugal gained wealth from African trade in gold, ivory, and slaves. - Interest in gold expanded to the spice trade in India, where pepper and cloves were in high demand. - Key Voyages and Discoveries - Bartholomew Diaz (1487) - Sailed down the west coast of Africa. - Rounded its southern tip, later named the Cape of Good Hope. - Vasco da Gama (1498) - Followed Diaz’s route and landed in Calicut, India. - Established a direct sea route from Europe to India. - Christopher Columbus (1492-1504) - An Italian explorer, funded by Spain, sought a westward route to India. - Discovered the “New World” but believed he had reached India. - Named the native people “Indians,” leading to the term “Red Indians” for South Americans. - Remained unaware that he had discovered a new continent. - Christopher Columbus never realized during his lifetime that he had discovered a new continent—what we now call the Americas. - He died in 1506 still believing that he had reached the outskirts of Asia, specifically islands near India or China. - Ferdinand Magellan (1519-1522) - A Portuguese explorer, seeking a westward route to the spice islands of Asia. - Crossed the Pacific Ocean via South America and reached the Philippines. - Killed in a battle with a local chief, his crew continued the journey under Juan Sebastián Elcano. - His expedition circumnavigated the earth, proving conclusively that the world is round. - - Rise of Colonialism - European Explorations and the Race for Colonization - During the 15th and 16th centuries, seafarers from Western European nations crossed vast oceans and discovered new civilizations. - These discoveries transformed world history, leading to intense competition among European countries to control land and resources in Asia, Africa, and America. - The colonization of these three continents was closely linked to the commercial revolution in Europe, which was shaped by capitalism and mercantilism. - Capitalism and Mercantilism - Capitalism: - A system where private owners invest accumulated wealth for profit, encouraging commercial expansion from local to global levels. - Mercantilism: - A theory that viewed a state’s power as dependent on its wealth, especially in gold and silver. It promoted self-sufficiency, maximizing exports while minimizing imports. - Mercantilism led to the establishment of overseas colonies, which supplied raw materials and precious metals. - European governments and entrepreneurs worked together to expand global commerce, particularly in the 17th and 18th centuries. France and England became the most successful mercantilist states in early modern Europe. - Portugal and Spain: Pioneers of Colonialism - The first overseas discoveries were financed by Portugal and Spain in the 15th century. - In 1494, the Treaty of Tordesillas divided the overseas territories between Spain and Portugal. Lands to the east of a designated line went to Portugal, while lands to the west went to Spain. - Four key principles guided European colonialism: - The mother country dominated trade, providing manufactured goods while the colonies supplied raw materials. - Other European nations were excluded from trade with a colony. - Armed forces were used to control natives and protect trade routes. - Christianity was spread forcefully or through missionary activities, particularly in the Americas, Africa, and parts of Southeast Asia. - Colonization of the Americas - Following the discovery of America, Spain established control over vast territories, including Mexico, Peru, Bolivia, Chile, Guatemala, and the southwestern coast of America. - The Portuguese controlled Brazil. - The English colonized North America and the Caribbean, establishing their first permanent colony at Jamestown, Virginia, in 1607. - By the mid-1600s, English settlers had founded over twenty autonomous settlements in the New World. - The Dutch colony of New Amsterdam was annexed by the English in 1664 and renamed New York. - The French established colonies in the St. Lawrence Basin of North America. - The Swedish and Dutch had minor colonies in America. - The process of colonization led to the destruction of the Aztec and Inca civilizations. Their kings were killed, and native populations were largely eliminated or replaced by European settlers. - The remaining indigenous people were forcibly converted to Christianity. - Colonization of Africa - Unlike the Americas, Africa was not an unknown land to Europeans. - North Africa had been part of the Mediterranean trade network since the Roman period, while East Africa was linked to Arab traders. - In the 15th century, Europeans initially bypassed Africa, considering it poor by their standards. - Central and southern Africa was inhabited by tribal clans, whose cultures Europeans made little effort to understand. - The Slave Trade and African Exploitation - Europeans were mainly interested in the slave trade and commodities like gold, ivory, and tropical goods. - The Portuguese, Dutch, English, and French set up trading stations along Africa’s coastal belt. The interior remained mostly unexplored, except by slavers and their native accomplices. - The cultivation of sugar and tobacco in the Americas depended on African slave labor. - During the 18th century, an estimated 75,000–90,000 Africans were shipped across the Atlantic every year. - Over the entire period of the transatlantic slave trade, about nine million Africans were transported, with six million in the 18th century alone. - 35% of the slaves were sent to English and French plantations in the Caribbean. - 5% went to North America. - The rest were taken to Portuguese Brazil and Spanish colonies in South America. - As the demand for slaves increased, governments abolished state monopolies over the trade, allowing private entrepreneurs to participate. - - Colonization of Asia In Asia, India, China, Japan, and Southeast Asia became major centers of European trade in the 16th century. - Over time, Europeans used their military strength and divide-and-rule tactics to govern these regions. - Portuguese, Dutch, British, and French Expansion - The Portuguese were the first to establish settlements on the west coast of India and in China. - They were soon joined by the Dutch, British, and French. - In China, the Portuguese maintained control of Macao, while the Dutch established a post in Taiwan. - In Japan, European traders initially secured privileges, but strong resistance led Japan to restrict foreign trade. - The Dutch East India Company (1602) became a dominant force in Asia, controlling Sumatra, Borneo, the Spice Islands, and parts of the Malay Peninsula. - They also drove out Portuguese traders from many areas. - The Advent of Europeans in India - Early European Contact - Europeans had known about India since Alexander’s invasion in ancient times. - During the Roman Empire, India was in direct contact with Europe. - Throughout the Middle Ages, Arab traders linked India and Europe, transmitting Indian knowledge of mathematics, astronomy, medicine, and literature. - However, Europeans were more interested in commercial trade than cultural exchanges. - The Struggle for Indian Trade - The four European powers competing for control over Indian trade were: - Portuguese - Dutch - English - French - The Portuguese were the first to establish a trading company in India. - Their main goals were to defeat Arab traders and spread Christianity. - They fought the Malabar kings, who protected Arab merchants, leading to conflicts with Egypt, Turkey, and Gujarat. - Despite resistance, the Portuguese defeated a combined fleet of these powers, securing naval supremacy in the Indian Ocean. - They conquered Goa from the Sultan of Bijapur, making it a Portuguese colony. - In the 16th century, the Portuguese were challenged by the Dutch, English, and French. - Eventually, the British East India Company emerged victorious, laying the foundation for the British Empire in India in the 18th century. - The Rise of Major European Powers - During the 17th and 18th centuries, five major states dominated Europe: Great Britain, France, Austria, Prussia, and Russia. - These states expanded their influence globally through military strength, economic growth, and colonial expansion. - Their dominance came at the expense of weaker European states. - Two Models of Political Consolidation - The English Model - Developed a limited monarchy with a strong Parliament by the late 17th century. - Maintained a balance of power between the monarchy and elected representatives. - Influenced governance in British colonies, especially in North America. - The French Model - Followed an absolute monarchy, where the king held unchecked power. - Relied on a large standing army and high taxation to sustain military expenses. - Established a bureaucracy to efficiently collect taxes and manage state affairs. - These two models shaped European politics and influenced global colonial expansion. - The Glorious Revolution of England - Turbulence in England (1603-1714) - England experienced a century of crisis with conflicts between the Puritans, the Stuart Kings, and Parliament. - Puritanism in 17th-century England included a range of religious groups, from moderate Anglicans to radical sects. - Despite three foreign rulers and a period without a king (1649-1660), England emerged with a model of limited monarchy, parliamentary government, and religious tolerance. - James I (1603-1625) - First foreign king of England as James VI of Scotland. - Inherited a divided church, royal debt, and tensions with Parliament. - His attempts to raise revenue independently led to conflict with Parliament. - Religious tensions increased due to his pro-Spanish foreign policy. - Best remembered for the translation of the Bible into English (King James Version). - Charles I (1625-1649) - Son of James I, continued his father’s conflicts with Parliament. - Imposed new taxes without consulting Parliament, leading to its dissolution in 1629. - Recalled Parliament in 1640 due to a war with Scotland. - Long Parliament (1640-1653) abolished his new taxes and made Parliament dissolution illegal without its consent. - Charles I saw a division in Parliament and invaded it with soldiers in 1642. - Parliament, led by Oliver Cromwell, formed the New Model Army to fight against him. - Executed on 30 January 1649 after a trial, leading to the abolition of monarchy, the House of Lords, and the Anglican Church. - - The Puritan Republic (1649-1660) - England became a Puritan republic under Oliver Cromwell. - His army conquered Ireland and Scotland, forming Great Britain. - In 1653, Cromwell dissolved Parliament and ruled as Lord Protector. - His military dictatorship was unpopular, leading to a desire to restore monarchy. - The Restoration of Monarchy (1660) - In 1660, Charles II (1660-1685), son of Charles I, was restored to the throne. - Marked the return of a hereditary monarchy and the supremacy of the Anglican Church. - Succeeded by his brother, James II (1685-1688), who dissolved Parliament and promoted Catholicism. - His Catholic wife gave birth to a son in 1688, ensuring a Catholic heir, which triggered a political crisis. - The Glorious Revolution (1688) - Whigs and Tories united against James II and invited William III of Orange and Mary to invade England. - William III arrived in November 1688 and was welcomed without opposition. - James II fled to France, and Parliament declared the throne vacant. - William III and Mary (1689-1702) were crowned as joint monarchs. - They signed the Bill of Rights (1689), which: - Limited the monarchy’s power. - Ensured Parliament’s supremacy. - Guaranteed civil liberties to the privileged classes. - Prohibited Catholics from occupying the English throne. - The Glorious Revolution established constitutional monarchy in England, where monarchs ruled with the consent of Parliament and under the law. - American War of Independence - Introduction - The American War of Independence marked a turning point in European colonialism. It set the stage for future liberation movements in Africa, Asia, and Latin America. - The war led to the secession of thirteen American colonies from Britain and became a laboratory for new political ideas and institutions. - British Colonization in America - Establishment of Colonies - Britain established its first colony in Jamestown, Virginia, in 1607. - Within a century, a series of English colonies were established in North America. - By the early 18th century, the British Crown directly governed these colonies. - Administration of Colonies - The Crown appointed Royal Governors for administration. - Each colony had an elected legislative assembly, a council, and militias. - Economic Structure - The economy was primarily agricultural, based on plantations managed by enslaved people. - Major crops: tobacco, indigo, rice, and sugar. - Principal port cities: Boston, Newport, New York, Philadelphia, and Baltimore. - Growing Resentment (bitterness) Against Britain - Political Awareness and Influence - Until the 1760s, American political values were similar to England’s. - Prominent American political theorists included Benjamin Franklin, Thomas Jefferson, James Wilson, and John Adams. - Their ideas were influenced by John Locke and Montesquieu, advocating self-government. - Demand for Self-Government - Initially, American leaders sought self-governance within the British Empire, not independence. - British leaders, however, viewed the Empire as indivisible. - By 1750, few expected independence to happen in the 18th century. - Causes of the American Revolution - Economic Strains After the Seven Years’ War - Britain sought to recover war expenses by taxing American colonies. - The colonies, having benefited from British victories, were expected to share the financial burden. - The Stamp Act (1765) - Imposed taxes on legal documents and newspapers. - British justified it as legal, but colonists opposed it, asserting “No taxation without representation.” - Colonial Protests and the Stamp Act Congress - In 1765, the Stamp Act Congress in America drafted a protest memorandum. - Groups like the ‘Sons of Liberty’ led widespread resistance. - Britain repealed the Stamp Act in 1766 but passed the Declaratory Act, affirming British authority over the colonies. - British Political Writings and American Resistance - Radical British political writings, ignored in Britain, gained traction in America. - These texts reinforced ideas of liberty and republican governance. - Key Events Leading to Independence - The Tea Act and the Boston Tea Party (1773) - British Parliament allowed the East India Company to sell tea directly to the colonies. - Though the Act lowered tea prices, it retained a tax without colonial consent. - In response, colonists in Boston threw a shipment of tea into the harbor on 16 December 1773, an event known as the ‘Boston Tea Party.’ - - The Intolerable Acts (1774) - British Parliament, under Lord North, passed laws to punish the colonies: - Closed the port of Boston. - Reorganized the Massachusetts government. - Allowed British troops to be housed in private homes. - The Path to War - Formation of the First Continental Congress (1774) - Colonists formed Committees of Correspondence to coordinate resistance. - The First Continental Congress met in Philadelphia, demanding self-government. - The Second Continental Congress and Rebellion (1775) - In May 1775, the Second Continental Congress began governing the colonies. - King George III declared the colonies in rebellion. - Thomas Paine’s pamphlet Common Sense (1776) galvanized support for independence. - Declaration of Independence and the War - Declaration of Independence (4 July 1776) - The Continental Congress formally declared independence from Britain. - The war continued until 1781. - Foreign Support and Victory - Spain and France supported the American colonies against Britain. - In 1781, George Washington’s forces defeated Lord Cornwallis at Yorktown. - The Treaty of Paris (1783) officially ended the war, recognizing American independence. - - Formation of the United States - Post-Independence Perspective - Initially, Americans saw themselves as protecting English liberties from a tyrannical monarchy and Parliament. - Over time, they developed a new sense of liberty. - The U.S. Constitution (1787) - A Constitutional Convention was held in Philadelphia under George Washington’s chairmanship. - Proposed a federal system, dividing power between central and state governments. - The American colonies officially united as the United States of America. - The American colonies gained independence from Britain in just about 12 years after serious tensions began - The reasons that differentiate why America took only 12 years while India took approx 200 years - Nature of Colonialism: Settler vs. Exploitative - British were more oppressive toward Indians (considered inferiors) as compared to American colonies. - Unity - American colonies got united very quickly due to homogeneous nature (language, religion, roots) while India was diversified into multiple religions, languages, etc making divide and rule easy - Indians were fighting amongst themselves and would often support British among their rivalries. - Geopolitical Distraction - Britain was already busy with ongoing European rivalries and lacked resources for proper control during that time - Military Power - Americans rebels were using same Military knowledge as the British while Indians were using old inferior ways of fighting. - Leadership and Strategy - American revolution had coordinated leadership while Indian freedom struggle was fragmented and more on personal benefits of participating individuals. - Communication and Technology - India was way behind in technology against European powers. - Lack of education - Most of the Indians were uneducated and could not counter the intelligent strategies of British like Divide and conquer, strategic disarmament, etc - Timing - Americans were the first to decolonize themselves from the British crown, but during for India, british had learned from the experience and had counter strategies in place. - The French Revolution - Causes of the French Revolution - The French Revolution was one of the most dramatic and complex upheavals (disruption / sudden change) in Western civilization. It not only disrupted France but also had a profound impact on the rest of Europe. - The Revolution was rooted in: - The social and political conditions of 18th-century France. - The ideas of the Enlightenment, which were widespread throughout Europe. - Historians debate which of these factors played a more significant role: - Some argue that the abuses of the Ancien Régime (Old Regime) made the revolution inevitable. - Others believe the Revolution was mainly driven by philosophers influenced by Enlightenment ideas, who exposed the weaknesses of the Ancien Régime. - The Ancien Régime - The term Ancien Régime refers to the political, social, and economic structures that existed in France before 1789. It was characterized by: - Political system: - Absolute monarchy supported by bureaucracies and armies. - Economic system: - Agriculture-based, scarcity of goods, slow transport, weak financial institutions, and competitive overseas trade. - Social structure: - Aristocratic elites with inherited privileges, powerful churches, organized labor in guilds, and a heavily taxed peasantry. - Few people outside the government called for reforms, which were promoted under the Enlightenment Movement. - Enlightenment thinkers exposed contemporary abuses and argued for political and social change. - The Role of Enlightenment Thinkers - By the mid-18th century, Enlightenment philosophers brought their ideas to the public through writings and debates. They: - Exposed political oppression and religious restrictions. - Suggested practical reforms to improve society. - Became highly respected figures, influencing revolutionary thought. - Key figures included: - Voltaire - John Locke - Montesquieu - Rousseau - These thinkers inspired the Revolution by challenging oppressive governance, unfair social structures, and conflicts between the monarchy and the aristocracy. - Financial Crisis and the Monarchy’s Failure - The French monarchy failed to manage its finances, leading to a severe economic crisis. Major causes included: - The costly Seven Years’ War (1756–1762). - Financial aid to the American War of Independence, deepening debt. - Inability to tax the wealthy aristocracy and clergy, while the overburdened peasants had no means to pay. - King Louis XVI and Marie Antoinette - Louis XVI (ruled 1774–1792) was honest but indecisive, preferring hunting and court politics over governance. - Marie Antoinette, his Austrian wife, was seen as extravagant (no restraint on spending) and disconnected from common people’s struggles. - - The Estates-General and the Rise of the Third Estate - To address the financial crisis, Louis XVI appointed various ministers (1786–1788) to persuade the nobility and clergy to pay taxes, but all failed. - In July 1788, under pressure, the king agreed to convene the Estates-General in May 1789 (the first meeting since 1614). - The Estates-General consisted of three estates: - Clergy - Nobility - Bourgeoisie (middle class), also known as the Third Estate - The Third Estate demanded: - All three Estates sit together and vote individually. - The number of Third Estate representatives be doubled. - When these demands were ignored, the Third Estate declared itself the National Assembly, taking the Tennis Court Oath (20 June 1789), vowing to draft a new constitution for France. - Key Events of the Revolution (1789) - The Fall of the Bastille (14 July 1789) - Parisian mobs stormed the Bastille, a fortress and political prison, symbolizing the hated Ancien Régime. - The governor ordered troops to fire on civilians, killing 98 people. - In response, the crowd stormed and captured the Bastille, marking the Revolution’s violent beginning. - The Women’s March on Versailles (October 1789) - A scarcity of bread (due to drought and crop failures) led to protests. - Rumors spread that Marie Antoinette had dismissively said, “Let them eat cake.” - Thousands of women marched to Versailles, demanding the King return to Paris, forcing Louis XVI to comply. - The National Constituent Assembly and the Declaration of Rights - The Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen (27 August 1789) - Before drafting a new constitution, the Assembly issued this document, declaring: - All men are born free and equal. - Natural rights include liberty, property, security, and resistance to oppression. - Sovereignty rests with the people. - The Constitution of 1791 - France became a constitutional monarchy with limited powers. - Voting rights were granted only to those who paid taxes or owned property. - The National Legislative Assembly was created, with the power to declare war and peace. - Radicalization and the Execution of Louis XVI - Rise of Political Factions - Jacobins (radicals) - Girondists (moderates) - Sans-Culottes (working-class revolutionaries) - The Trial and Execution of Louis XVI (January 1793) - Louis XVI was tried for treason and executed by guillotine in January 1793. - His execution marked the official end of the monarchy and radicalized the Revolution. - The Reign of Terror (1793–1794) - Led by Robespierre and the Jacobins, aiming to eliminate “enemies of the Revolution.” - More than 15,000 people were guillotined, including Queen Marie Antoinette. - - By 1794, the Terror ended, and the Jacobins lost power. - The Directory (1795–1799) - A new Republican Constitution established the Directory, a five-member executive government. - Napoleon Bonaparte, a rising military leader, gained prominence. - Napoleon Bonaparte (1799–1815) - - Rise to Power - 1799: Napoleon led a coup against the Directory, becoming First Consul with dictatorial powers. - A plebiscite (public vote) confirmed his authority, with 99.9% approval. - Achievements (1799–1804) - Restored stability after the Revolution. - Defeated France’s enemies through military campaigns. - Introduced economic and administrative reforms, including: - Establishing the Bank of France. - Reforming the tax system. - - Unification of Italy - The period from 1848 to 1875 witnessed significant political changes in Europe, driven by the ideals of Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity—representing individual political liberty, economic equality, and national unity. - - Italy Before Unification - At this time, Italy was merely a geographical expression, divided into several independent states. - Italian nationalists aspired to unite these small, absolutist principalities into a single nation, but there was no consensus on the approach: - Religious-minded patriots supported a federation under the Pope. - Moderate nationalists favored a constitutional monarchy. - Leaders of the Unification Movement - Three key figures emerged as leaders in the Italian unification struggle: - Count Camillo Benso di Cavour – The royal minister of Piedmont (also known as the Kingdom of Sardinia). - Giuseppe Mazzini – Advocated for a “free, independent, and republican Italy,” inspiring the youth and setting an example for other European nations. - - Giuseppe Garibaldi – A revolutionary military leader who played a crucial role in securing southern Italy. - Cavour, supported by King Victor Emmanuel II, worked towards unifying Italy as a democratic republic and implemented key reforms in Piedmont to gain international support. - The Struggle for Unification - Early Attempts and Failures - The first attempt at expelling Austria failed in 1848-1849, as Sardinia was defeated. - Cavour’s Strategy - In 1852, Victor Emmanuel II appointed Cavour as his minister. - Cavour aimed to prove that Italians were efficient and economically progressive to gain international recognition. - He promoted free trade, railway construction, economic expansion, and agricultural development. - He established the Nationalist Society and a printing press to advocate unification across Italian states. - Believing that Italy needed French support, he allied with France and Britain in the Crimean War to bring the Italian issue to the global stage. - The War Against Austria (1859) - In June 1859, war broke out between Sardinia and Austria. - Austria was defeated and driven out of most of northern Italy. - However, France betrayed Cavour by indirectly supporting Austria. - Despite this, Sardinia gained control of Lombardy, and the regions of Parma, Modena, Tuscany, and Romagna voted to unite with Sardinia. - Garibaldi’s Campaign in Southern Italy (1860) - Garibaldi’s republican forces successfully conquered Sicily, Palermo, and Naples. - Meanwhile, Cavour’s troops seized the Papal States, except for the area around Rome, which remained under Papal control. - Garibaldi, despite favoring republicanism, reluctantly accepted Sardinia’s leadership. - The Formation of Unified Italy - By the late 1860s, the southern Italian states joined the northern union led by Sardinia. - In March 1861, Victor Emmanuel II was declared the King of Unified Italy. - The new state adopted the conservative constitution of 1848, originally issued by Charles Albert. - Challenges After Unification - Despite unification, Italy faced numerous difficulties: - It lacked the natural resources of France and Germany. - It did not have the strong overseas trade network that Britain enjoyed. - These economic weaknesses continued to affect Italy well into the 20th century. - Unification of Germany - - Before its unification, Germany consisted of thirty-nine independent states, which had replaced the old Holy Roman Empire. - These states were loosely grouped together in an organization called the German Confederation. - The success of Italian unification intensified nationalist aspirations in Germany, and its unification process followed a similar pattern. - Otto von Bismarck: The Architect of German Unification - The key figure behind Germany’s unification was Otto von Bismarck (1815-1898), who shaped European history for the next thirty years. - Bismarck began his career as a staunch defender of royal privilege during the turbulent year of 1848. - As a reward, King Wilhelm I appointed him as Prussia’s representative to the Frankfurt Diet of the German Confederation. - He later served as Prussia’s ambassador to St. Petersburg and Paris. - Bismarck’s Rise to Power - Meanwhile, King Wilhelm I faced opposition from the liberal-dominated Prussian Parliament, which refused to approve military reforms demanded by the army generals. - On the verge of abdicating, Wilhelm I appointed Bismarck as Chancellor to resolve the crisis. - Bismarck made it clear that the great questions of the day would be solved not by parliamentary debates but by “blood and iron”. - His policies were strongly supported by the army and the bureaucracy. - Wars Leading to Unification - Bismarck understood that uniting Germany under Prussian leadership required shifting public focus from internal issues to foreign affairs. - War Against Denmark (1864) - Bismarck declared war on Denmark over the disputed territories of Schleswig and Holstein. - Austria supported Prussia in the war, and together they defeated Denmark. - After victory, Schleswig and Holstein were placed under joint Austro-Prussian administration, but tensions between the two powers grew. - Austro-Prussian War (1866) - Bismarck formed strategic alliances with France and Italy to ensure that Austria would be isolated in the event of war. - In 1866, war broke out between Prussia and Austria over the administration of Schleswig and Holstein. - Prussia won a decisive victory, and the Treaty of Prague excluded Austria from German affairs. - Prussia now emerged as the dominant power among the German states. - Formation of the North German Confederation (1867) - Prussia incorporated all German states north of the River Main, deposing their ruling dynasties. - These states, along with Prussia, formed the North German Confederation, with Prussia as the leader. - The Confederation’s Constitution (later adopted in 1871 for the German Empire) introduced a legislative house (Reichstag) elected by universal male suffrage. - However, the Reichstag had little real power, as the army always remained loyal to the King and his ministers. - Franco-Prussian War and the Final Unification (1870-1871) - Bismarck now sought to incorporate the southern German states into the Confederation. - The opportunity arose when France opposed the possible succession of a Prussian prince to the Spanish throne. - Bismarck manipulated a diplomatic dispute (the Ems Dispatch) to make it appear as though King Wilhelm I had insulted the French ambassador. - Enraged, France declared war on Prussia in July 1870. - The southern German states sided with Prussia, completing Germany’s national unity. - The Fall of France - On September 1, 1870, at the Battle of Sedan, the Germans defeated the French army and captured Napoleon III. - By late September, Paris was under siege. - On January 28, 1871, Paris surrendered. - Ten days earlier, on January 18, 1871, in the Hall of Mirrors at the Palace of Versailles, the unified German Empire was proclaimed. - Wilhelm I was crowned Emperor (Kaiser) of Germany, while the rulers of southern German states retained their positions within the new Federation. - Impact of German Unification - The unification of Germany in the 19th century established a strong, centralized state in the heart of Europe. - The new German Empire had some liberal characteristics, but real power remained with the monarchy and the military. - Bismarck’s “blood and iron” policy captured popular imagination, but it also fostered militarism and authoritarianism, shaping Germany’s future trajectory. - The world from 1900 to 1919 - Political Landscape at the Turn of the 19th Century - The world did not change significantly from previous decades. Europe remained dominant in world affairs. - Great Britain maintained naval supremacy. - It ruled over a vast colonial empire. - However, it avoided continental commitments. - Two key reasons for this were: - Queen Victoria’s accession (1837) severed ties with Hanoverian Germany. - Britain’s domestic and imperial politics led to its isolation from European issues. - Meanwhile, Germany was the leading land power. Italy, Japan, and the U.S. had yet to emerge as global forces. - Major Powers and Their Influence - Germany, Austria-Hungary, Russia, and Turkey dominated continental Europe (east of the Rhine). - Britain, Russia, and China held power in Asia. - Africa was undergoing colonial partition, as in the 19th century. - Despite minor changes, the European order remained intact. - This order was preserved more by tradition and sentiment than by practical adjustments. - The Rise of Imperialism - The principles of liberalism and nationalism grew more competitive. - European states expanded their colonial ambitions. - They eyed distant lands and ancient civilizations. - This new political obsession brought complex challenges. - One key driver of this outlook was scientific and technological progress. - These advances boosted industries. - They also enhanced military weaponry. - This period came to be known as the “Era of Armed Peace”. - The extension of European control and intensified rivalries defined this phase. - The Changing Balance of Power - The European order was stable only in appearance. - The Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy) controlled one side. - The Dual Alliance (France, Russia) balanced the other. - A major shift began in 1890. Kaiser Wilhelm II dismissed Otto von Bismarck as Imperial Chancellor. - This led to: - A change in German foreign policy. - Abandonment of Bismarck’s cautious approach. - Increased German interference in the Near East. - A German naval build-up, challenging British naval dominance. - Wilhelm II and his generation sought new triumphs (a greater victory or achievement). No single leader controlled Germany’s foreign policy. - Instead, it became a compromise between: - The Kaiser - The Imperial Chancellor - The Foreign Office - The Concert of Europe and Its Failure - Despite rivalries, major powers acted jointly in the Concert of Europe. - This alliance (Austria-Hungary, France, Germany, Britain, Russia, Italy) prevented small conflicts from escalating. - However, its members often manipulated one another. This internal struggle eventually led to its collapse. Its failure paved the way for World War I. - Developments Leading to World War I - At the start of the 20th century, Britain and the Dutch clashed over South Africa. This struggle began in October 1899. - The conflict arose because: - The Dutch republic of Transvaal refused to grant political rights to Anglo-Saxon immigrants. - Other Boer republics also denied these rights. - The Boer republics were independent, self-governing republics formed by Dutch-speaking inhabitants of the Cape Colony and their descendants. - In Britain, public opinion saw this as a just war. The Boers (Dutch speaking settlers) were denying lawful rights to British citizens. - However, European and global opinion viewed it as British imperialism. - The Boer War - At the start of the 20th century, Britain and the Boers clashed over South Africa. - The Boers, having previously defeated Britain, were aware of its military weaknesses in the region. - They believed that before the war ended, some European power would intervene on their behalf. - British Underestimation - The British underestimated: - The tenacity and tactical skill of Boer fighters. - The advantage of fighting on home ground. - The Dutch-class irregulars were strong fighters but suffered from: - Lax discipline - Excessive caution - Lack of unified strategy - Had they overcome these weaknesses, the war could have lasted longer in their favor. - Course of the War - Several small battles took place. - Boer forces used thin, extended defensive lines, making them difficult to penetrate. - The British gradually gained successes. - The war spanned thousands of square miles and involved 450,000 British soldiers. It ended on 31 May 1902, after a two-and-a-half-year campaign. - The British secured victory by: - Splitting the war zone into smaller resistance areas. - Using mounted columns to strike at Boer mobility. - Impact of the War - Inside Britain: - The Liberals came to power in 1905. - The new Secretary of State for War, R.B. Haldane, took key steps: - Maintained British naval supremacy. - Reorganized the army, drawing lessons from the Boer War. - Outside Britain: - The war showcased British imperial unity. - Britain’s military reputation suffered due to prolonged Boer resistance. - However, Britain’s naval power prevented European rivals from aiding the Boers. - Politics of Alliances - - At the beginning of the 20th century, Britain’s long-standing policy of splendid isolation was becoming impractical. - Maintaining naval supremacy alone was no longer sufficient, and Britain began seeking an ally on the European mainland. - Germany as a Potential Ally - Britain initially considered Germany due to: - Its military strength, as the most formidable (intimidating) power in Europe. - Its rising population and tradition of aggressive diplomacy. - Its potential as a counterweight to Russia, Britain’s key rival in the Middle and Far East. - Its historical ties with Britain: - The Kaiser of Germany was Queen Victoria’s grandson. - Germany had helped Wellington at Waterloo. - British Efforts to Secure an Alliance - During the Boer War, British Colonial Secretary Joseph Chamberlain sought closer ties with Germany. - He proposed a secret agreement over Morocco, where Germany’s influence was growing. - German Resistance - Baron von Holstein, a German Foreign Ministry official, advised the Kaiser against an alliance. - He argued that Britain always used others for its own benefit. - He encouraged Germany to demand a higher price for its friendship. - Holstein believed a British-French alliance was unlikely, making Britain dependent on Germany. - Shift Towards France - Britain, frustrated by Germany’s reluctance (unwillingness), began reconsidering its alliances. - Factors Leading to a French Alliance - The British press urged an understanding with Russia, even at the cost of concessions (compromise) in China and the Persian Gulf. - Growing concerns over Germany’s expanding naval power pushed Britain to seek alternatives. - France, though a traditional enemy, became a potential partner. - Challenges in a British-French Alliance - Britain was aristocratic, whereas France was the only acknowledged republic in Europe. - France had suffered British indifference when defeated by Germany thirty years earlier. - The two nations had nearly gone to war in 1898 over Fashoda (later Kodok) in Sudan. - France’s internal instability: - It was seen as divided, with a weak government. - The appointment of Alexander Millerand, a Socialist, to the French Cabinet in 1899 worried Britain’s Conservative government. - The Anglo-French Entente (agreement / understanding / arrangement) - Despite obstacles, Anglo-French relations improved at the start of the 20th century. - The Role of King Edward VII - May 1903: King Edward VII visited Paris, breaking the diplomatic deadlock. - His visit paved the way for negotiations between: - Lansdowne (British Foreign Minister) - Delcassé (French Foreign Minister) - Formation of the Entente (April 1904) - The negotiations resulted in a series of agreements known as the Anglo-French Entente, the most significant diplomatic event of the early 20th century. It remained in effect until France fell in World War II. - Key Agreements - First Agreement (Egypt & Morocco) - Britain agreed not to alter Egypt’s political status. - Egypt remained a semi-independent Turkish province but was under British occupation. - France would not interfere in British affairs there. - France agreed not to alter Morocco’s political status. - Morocco was under French influence. - Britain would not interfere in French affairs there. - Additional terms: - Trade freedom in Egypt and Morocco for 30 years. - No fortifications on the Moorish coast opposite Gibraltar. - France would negotiate separately with Spain on Morocco’s status. - The Egyptian government could manage its finances, provided it paid its debt interest. - Suez Canal matters were settled as per French wishes. - Second Agreement (Newfoundland & West Africa) - France gave up its fishing rights off Newfoundland’s coast. - In return, France gained uninterrupted access between its territories on: - The Niger River. - Lake Chad (West Africa). - Third Agreement (Southeast Asia & Madagascar) - Both nations agreed to: - No military intervention or special privileges in the Menam Basin (Thailand). - France recognized British influence: - West of the neutral zone (Siamese territory). - Gulf of Siam, Malaya Peninsula, and adjacent islands. - Britain recognized French influence: - East and southeast of the neutral zone. - Britain withdrew objections to: - French tariffs in Madagascar (occupied by France in 1896). - 1906: Britain and France established a joint rule (condominium) over the New Hebrides islands. - Impact of the Anglo-French Entente - The agreement resolved many outstanding disputes between Britain and France. - It strengthened their diplomatic ties, paving the way for future cooperation. - However, it also deepened European divisions, leading to the formation of two opposing military blocs. - Lord Rosebery (British ex-Premier) noted: - Germany, Europe’s strongest military power, was excluded from the negotiations. - The neglect of Germany’s interests in Morocco contributed to growing tensions. - Ultimately, this pushed Britain towards war against Germany. - The Franco-Russian Alliance - The Anglo-French Entente influenced Russo-British relations, which had been hostile for most of the 19th century. - Russia’s Shift in Focus - After bitter experiences in Bulgaria, the Russian Tsars turned their attention towards the Far East. - The collapsing Chinese Empire presented new expansion opportunities for Russia. - To secure Western support, Russia sought an alliance with France. - Strengthening Franco-Russian Ties - Since the late 1880s, relations between France and Russia improved due to: - Financial transactions. - Military exchanges. - 1891: A political agreement was reached to coordinate foreign policy. - 1895: A military convention (a way in which something is usually done) was officially announced. - The alliance aimed to counter any attack by the Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy). - Reaction from Other Powers - The Franco-Russian Alliance was met with suspicion and disapproval in Berlin and London. - However, the alliance did not mask the military and political weaknesses of both partners. - Historians note: - Both France and Russia were weaker than they appeared. - Over the next ten years, their weaknesses became evident. - Only when Britain later joined the alliance did it become a true counterbalance to the Triple Alliance. - The Status of the Triple Alliance - The Triple Alliance consisted of Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy, but internal tensions and conflicting interests weakened its unity. - Austria-Hungary: A Fragile Partner - Apart from Germany, the most significant power in the alliance was Austria-Hungary. - As a multiethnic empire, it faced challenges from rising nationalism. - Emperor Franz Josef followed a moderate policy: - He refused to impose military dictatorship in Bohemia. - However, Germany and Hungary formed an unholy alliance to undermine the empire’s balance. - For Germany, Austria-Hungary was merely: - A recruitment ground for its army. - A buffer state against Russian expansion. - Austria-Hungary adopted anti-Slav policies to align with German interests, despite many Slavs being loyal subjects. - This approach also suited Hungary, which had its own record of mistreating minorities. - Italy: A Reluctant Ally - Italy joined the Triple Alliance despite its historical enmity with Austria-Hungary. - Its decision was driven by hostility towards France due to: - France’s imperialist policies in Africa. - Mediterranean territorial disputes. - Italy resented French control over: - Savoy - Nice - Corsica - Though a formal member, Italy’s commitment to the alliance remained weak, as its national interests often clashed with those of its allies. - The Status of the Ottoman Empire - The Ottoman Empire sprawled across the Balkans, the Near East, and North Africa. Despite its declining power, it remained a force capable of maintaining its vast, multiethnic dominions. - The “Sick Man of Europe” - Tsar Nicholas I famously referred to the Ottoman Empire as the “Sick Man of Europe”, highlighting its weakening position. - However, the empire still managed to hold its diverse territories together, despite internal and external challenges. - Military Strength and Resilience - In the late 19th century, the Ottomans proved their resilience by: - Crushing an Armenian revolt. - Defeating Greece decisively in battle. - These victories demonstrated that, although in decline, the Ottoman Empire remained a significant military power in the region. - The Moroccan Question - The Anglo-French Entente focused on resolving Mediterranean disputes, including control over territories such as Morocco, Bosnia, Herzegovina, Tripoli, and the Balkan Peninsula. - France’s Blunder and Germany’s Reaction - The Entente granted France a free hand in Morocco, but French Foreign Minister Delcassé failed to consult Germany on the matter. - German Chancellor Bernhard von Bülow took advantage of this to assert that Morocco was an international issue, not just a French concern. - Germany also used the dispute to test the strength of the Anglo-French Entente. - Kaiser Wilhelm II’s Intervention (March 1895) - Kaiser Wilhelm II publicly challenged France’s role in Morocco, delivering a speech to the German colony in Tangier: - He emphasized Morocco’s independence and called for an open-door policy for international trade, opposing monopolies or annexation. - In April 1895, Bülow escalated tensions by demanding a conference of the Treaty of Madrid (1880) signatories to discuss Morocco. - The Algeciras Conference (1906) - Sultan Abdul Aziz IV of Morocco invited the concerned parties to a conference. - French Foreign Minister Delcassé resisted but eventually conceded under pressure from U.S. President Theodore Roosevelt, marking the first major U.S. intervention in European diplomacy. - The conference was held in Algeciras (January–April 1906). - Outcomes of the Conference - France and Spain jointly gained control over the Moroccan police. - Germany ensured that the Moroccan issue remained a multilateral concern. - The conference had broader geopolitical effects: - Strengthened ties between Britain, France, and Russia. - Marked the rise of the U.S. as a world power. - Continued Anglo-French military consultations until 1914, despite changes in the British government. - The Anglo-Russian Convention (1907) - The Anglo-Russian Convention, signed on 31 August 1907, marked the end of longstanding rivalries between Britain and Russia, bringing them to an agreement on key territorial and political issues in Persia, Afghanistan, and Tibet. - Persia: Spheres of Influence - Persia was divided into three zones: - A large Russian sphere in the north. - A small British sphere in the southeast. - A neutral zone in between, where both powers were placed on equal footing. - Both countries agreed to maintain the status quo in the Persian Gulf. - Afghanistan: British Supremacy Recognized - Britain assured Russia that it would not take any action threatening Russian interests in Afghanistan. - Russia, in turn, agreed to conduct all political relations with Kabul only through the British Foreign Office. - Tibet: Non-Interference Policy - Both nations agreed to respect Tibet’s territorial integrity. - They also pledged not to interfere in Tibet’s internal administration. - The Anglo-Russian Convention marked a significant step in the formation of the Triple Entente, aligning Britain, France, and Russia against the Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy) in the lead-up to World War I. - The Awakening of the Far East - At the turn of the 19th century, the Far East gained global significance, with the balance of power shifting in favor of Japan. - Japan pursued regional supremacy, challenging European interests, particularly in China and Korea. - The Boxer Rebellion and Its Aftermath (1900-1901) - The rise of ultra-nationalist Boxers, supported by the Dowager Empress Cixi (1835-1908), threatened foreigners in China. - European powers, setting aside their rivalries, sent an international force to suppress the rebellion in 1900. - The Boxer settlement (September 1901) imposed severe penalties on China: - Emperor Guangxu apologized to Berlin and Tokyo for the murder of German and Japanese diplomats. - China was forced to pay an indemnity of 450,000,000 Taels. - Harsh punishments were imposed: - Two Imperial princes were sentenced to death. - Three high-ranking officials were ordered to commit suicide. - Three leading Mandarins were beheaded. - The Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1902) - In January 1902, Britain and Japan signed an agreement to safeguard their special interests in China and Korea. - The treaty allowed Britain to shift its naval focus from the Pacific to the North Sea and Atlantic, where Germany’s growing naval power posed a challenge. - Japan’s international standing soared, reaching its peak in 1905 when it defeated Russia decisively in the Russo-Japanese War. - This period marked Japan’s emergence as a dominant power in Asia, while European influence in the Far East began to wane. - The Russo-Japanese War and the Treaty of Portsmouth (1904-1905) - By the turn of the 20th century, Russian influence was expanding in northern China. This expansion, particularly in Manchuria and Korea, alarmed Japan and led to rising tensions between the two powers. - Rising Tensions Between Russia and Japan - Russian Expansion in Manchuria and Korea - Russia deployed troops into Manchuria and constructed railways linking Vladivostok and Port Arthur under permission from the Chinese government. - In winter 1902-03, Russia increased its activities in North Korea, moving troops towards the Yalu River under the pretext of protecting Russian nationals cutting timber under a Chinese concession. - Failed Diplomatic Negotiations - Negotiations between Russia and Japan led to an agreement in January 1904: - Japan agreed to stay out of Manchuria. - Russia agreed to stay out of Korea. - However, Russia failed to respond within the stipulated three weeks, leading Japan to sever diplomatic relations. - The Russo-Japanese War (1904-1905) - Contrary to European expectations, Japan was well-prepared: - Its army was expanded, reorganized, and re-equipped. - Its navy was highly efficient and well-organized. - Japan had a geographical advantage, whereas Russia struggled to reinforce its Far Eastern troops. - Economic Backing - Japan received financial support from London and New York. - Russia relied on France. - Major Battles and Japanese Victories - Land Battles: Japan defeated Russian forces in the battles of Liaoyang, Shaho, and Mukden (20 February – 10 March 1905). - Naval Battles: - Admiral Togo led the destruction of Russian sea power in Port Arthur. - He inflicted a crushing defeat on Russia’s Baltic Fleet in the Battle of Tsushima (27-28 May 1905). - The Treaty of Portsmouth (5 September 1905) - By summer 1905, Japan had achieved its war objectives, leading to a peace settlement brokered by the United States. - The Treaty of Portsmouth (signed 5 September 1905) included: - Russian Recognition of Japanese Dominance - Russia recognized Japan’s paramount interests in Korea. - Russia transferred the Liao-Tung Peninsula lease and the southern Chinese Eastern Railway (Port Arthur to Changchun) to Japan, subject to Chinese approval. - Russia ceded (give up) the southern half of Sakhalin Island to Japan. - Withdrawal from Manchuria - Both sides agreed to withdraw their forces from Manchuria and not use railways there for strategic purposes. - Consequences of the War - Impact on Russia - The defeat led to domestic rebellion, planting the seeds of the Bolshevik Revolution (1917). - Impact on European Power Balance - Germany’s military strategists saw war on two fronts (as in 1914) as more feasible after Russia’s defeat. - Impact on Nationalist Movements - Japan’s victory over a European power inspired nationalist movements in India, Turkey, Persia, and beyond. - The Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1905) - Britain recognized Japan’s dominance in Korea. - Japan acknowledged Britain’s interests in India’s frontier regions. - The Russo-Japanese War marked the first time an Asian power decisively defeated a European one, shifting the global balance of power and reinforcing Japan’s role as a dominant force in East Asia. - The Manchurian Crisis - Japanese Expansion in Korea - Agreement with Korea (17 November 1905) - Japan signed an agreement with Korea. - Prince Ito was appointed as the Resident-General at Seoul. - Assassination of Prince Ito (1909) - Killed by a Korean nationalist. - Rising hostility due to Japanese overbearing and exploitative conduct. - Annexation of Korea (22 August 1910) - Japan formally annexed Korea. - Tacit approval from Russia and Great Britain. - Japanese Expansion in Manchuria - Impact of the Russo-Japanese War - Japan’s victory over Russia strengthened its position in Manchuria. - China was forced to accept the terms of the Portsmouth Treaty. - Sino-Japanese Agreement (22 December 1905) - China agreed to open sixteen towns in Manchuria to foreign trade and residence. - Japan was allowed to reconstruct railways connecting Antung (Korean frontier) with Mukden. - Friction and Delays - Reconstruction was completed only in 1911 due to tensions. - China tried to involve Britain and the US by granting railway concessions to British-American companies. - US Intervention and Its Failure - US Proposal (1909) - Suggested an international loan to China to buy foreign-controlled Manchurian railways. - Aimed at ensuring railway neutralization. - Rejection by Russia and Japan - Russia and Japan jointly rejected the US proposal. - Showed that the US was still far from becoming a world power. - Growing Japan-US Tensions - Speculation of Conflict - Rising tensions over immigration issues between Japan and the US. - Third Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1911) - Strengthened Japan’s position in the Far East. - China’s Political Collapse - Government Breakdown - Administrative failure across all provinces. - Fall of the Manchu Dynasty (1912) - Yuan Shih-Kai took power. - Established a new regime, ending imperial rule in China. - Crisis in the Near East - The Aftermath of the Treaty of Berlin (1878) - The Treaty of Berlin settled Near Eastern affairs, with little change except for Egypt coming under British rule. - The Ottoman Empire, though in decline, failed to capitalize on the internal divisions within the Balkans. - Britain’s acquisition of Cyprus and dominance over Egypt reduced Ottoman Turkey’s strategic importance. - Russia, after the Treaty of Portsmouth (1905), shifted focus from the Near East to the Middle East, creating a power vacuum that Germany sought to exploit. - The Armenian Massacres and German Influence - During the late 19th century, Kaiser Wilhelm II’s interest in the region became evident, particularly during the Armenian massacres. - The massacres at Urfa (September 1895) and Constantinople (August 1896) were orchestrated under Sultan Abdul Hamid II, with Turkish soldiers and fanatics carrying out the atrocities. - The British public reacted strongly, but Salisbury’s Conservative government refrained from intervention to avoid a European war. - The crisis strengthened German influence in Constantinople while weakening Britain’s position, setting the stage for World War I. - The Macedonian Crisis (1903-1908) - Turkish misrule in Macedonia led to tensions, prompting Austria and Russia to agree on the Murzsteg reforms in 1903, dividing Macedonia into administrative zones. - Austria’s 1908 proposal to build a railway through the Sanjak of Novibazar raised Russian suspicions, ending their cooperation in the Balkans. - In July 1908, the Young Turk Revolution, led by Enver Pasha, overthrew Sultan Abdul Hamid II and established a parliamentary system. - The new Parliament’s inclusion of representatives from Bosnia and Herzegovina alarmed Vienna, as Austria-Hungary formally occupied these provinces. - The Annexation of Bosnia and Herzegovina (1908) - Serbia, under King Peter I, sought the unification of Southern Slavs, but Austria-Hungary swiftly moved to annex Bosnia and Herzegovina. - A secret agreement between Austria and Russia allowed the annexation in exchange for Russian access to the Bosphorus and the Dardanelles. - On 5 October 1908, Bulgaria declared independence, and the next day, Austria-Hungary annexed Bosnia and Herzegovina. - Serbia, diplomatically isolated, had to accept the annexation in March 1909. - German Chancellor Bülow hailed (call out to attract attention) this as proof of the Austro-German alliance’s strength in continental politics. - - The Moroccan Crisis (1908-1912) - In 1908, Sultan Abdul Aziz IV of Morocco was overthrown by his brother Mulai Hafid, but his rule was unstable. - The Riff tribes rebelled against Spain in 1909, forcing Spain to deploy 50,000 troops. - In 1911, revolts around Fez led France to intervene, occupying the city while Spain seized Larache. - Germany, angered by these developments, sent the warship Panther to Agadir, challenging the Treaty of Algeciras. - Britain reacted sharply, leading to negotiations where Germany backed down. - In 1912, France established a protectorate over Morocco, ceding parts of the French Congo to Germany in return. - The crisis left lasting tensions between Britain, France, and Germany. - The Italo-Turkish War (1911-1912) - Italy, fearing French expansion in North Africa, declared war on Turkey in September 1911. - Using Turkish interference in trade as a pretext, Italy seized Tripoli. - Britain denied Turkey permission to send reinforcements through Egypt, allowing Italy an easy victory. - The Treaty of Ouchy (October 1912) resulted in Turkey ceding Tripoli to Italy. - Italy’s shift from the Triple Alliance to the Triple Entente was a crucial development for World War I. - Formation of the Balkan League (1912) - Bulgaria’s King Ferdinand and Prime Minister Gueshoff sought cooperation with Serbia to realize Bulgaria’s territorial ambitions. - With Russian support, Serbia and Bulgaria signed a treaty in March 1912, joined by Greece in May and Montenegro in August. - A secret annex in the treaty provided for joint military action against Turkey under Russian approval. - The First Balkan War (1912-1913) - Turkish promises of reform in Albania dissatisfied Serbia and Bulgaria. - Montenegro mobilized, and by 3 November 1912, Balkan Christian states were at war with Turkey. - The Bulgarians advanced toward Constantinople, forcing Turkey to seek European intervention. - A conference in London led to a peace treaty in December 1912, but Enver Pasha rejected it. - Fighting resumed, with Adrianople falling to Bulgarian and Serbian forces while Greece captured Jannina. - The Treaty of May 1913: - Greece gained Salonika, southern Macedonia, and Crete. - Serbia secured northern Macedonia. - Bulgaria received Thrace and the Aegean coast but ceded Silistria to Romania. - Turkey retained Constantinople and eastern Thrace. - The Second Balkan War (1913) - Within a month, Bulgaria attacked Serbian forces in Macedonia and attempted to seize Salonika. - Serbia and Greece resisted, while Romania invaded Bulgaria. - Turkey exploited the situation to retake Adrianople. - The Treaty of Bucharest (August 1913): - Bulgaria lost access to the Aegean Sea and territory in Macedonia. - Romania gained land with 340,000 people. - Turkey retained Adrianople. - Albania was recognized as independent. - Prelude to World War I (1913-1914) - The Balkan Wars proved that Britain and Germany could cooperate to prevent war. - However, military buildup intensified across Europe, with growing tensions in Germany and France. - French Ambassador to Vienna noted the rising belief that war was inevitable. - The Assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand (28 June 1914) - The heir to the Habsburg throne was assassinated in Sarajevo, triggering a chain of events leading to World War I. - Austria issued an ultimatum to Serbia on 23 July 1914, demanding compliance within 48 hours. - On 31 July, Germany gave Russia an ultimatum to halt mobilization within 12 hours. - By 1 August, Germany and Russia were at war, followed by Germany’s declaration of war on France on 3 August. - Germany’s invasion of Belgium provoked Britain, which declared war on 4-5 August. - Bismarck’s 1891 prediction that a world war would start in the East proved correct. - World War I - Causes of World War I - The immediate provocation for World War I was the assassination of Archduke Francis Ferdinand. - However, several underlying causes had been brewing for over a generation, leading to the outbreak of the war: - Extreme Nationalism - Inflamed racial pride among people of different nations. - Increased rivalries between states. - Led to disputes over colonial possessions in Asia, Africa, and the Balkans. - Nationalist aspirations in the Balkans turned the region into a volatile "tinderbox" that ultimately ignited the war. - Formation of Alliances - Nations grouped into mutually suspicious alliances, increasing tensions. - Militarism, along with secret treaties, intensified rivalries, setting the stage for war. - Imperialism and Economic Conflicts - Competition for markets, raw materials, and investment opportunities. - Germany’s unfulfilled imperialist ambitions played a major role in the war’s outbreak in 1914. - Character and Progress of World War I - When war broke out, it became unprecedented in scale and destruction: - Nearly all known nation-states participated, and battles were fought worldwide. - Lethal weaponry, including submarines, Zeppelins, poison gas, tanks, and armored vehicles, led to massive casualties. - Like many European wars before, it was fought for the "Balance of Power." - Two opposing sides emerged: - Central Powers: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Bulgaria, and Turkey. - Allied Powers: France, England, Japan, and Italy. - Key Developments: - For the first three and a half years, Germany held the upper hand. - In 1917, Russia withdrew from the war after the Bolsheviks overthrew the Tsarist regime, further strengthening Germany. - Germany's large-scale submarine warfare (U-boat menace) threatened Allied control of the seas. - The Lusitania, a British ocean liner, was sunk by a German U-boat on May 7, 1915, and over 100 American passengers died. - This factor along with Germany's unrestricted submarine warfare and it's incitation of Mexico against U.S. led U.S. to enter war and join allied forces. - Germany surrendered in November 1918 due to combined Allied pressure. - The Allies quickly defeated Turkey, secured Bulgaria's surrender, and witnessed Austria's collapse. - End of the War: - President Woodrow Wilson of the USA proposed the "Fourteen Points" as the basis for a peace settlement. - A naval mutiny in Germany led to the Kaiser fleeing to Holland. - A republic was established in Germany, which signed the armistice on 11 November 1918, ending the war. - The Paris Peace Conference of 1919 - Held in Paris in 1919, attended by the victorious Allied powers. - The venue selection reflected a spirit of vengeance. - The conference had to consider various secret treaties made during the war regarding territorial distributions. - French Prime Minister Clemenceau and British Premier Lloyd George pressured Wilson into compromising on his Fourteen Points. - Resulted in a series of five treaties concluded in and around Paris. - The Treaty of Versailles, 1919 - Signed between the Allies and Germany on 28 June 1919, the Treaty of Versailles imposed harsh conditions on Germany: - Territorial Losses: - Alsace-Lorraine was returned to France. - Saar Valley’s coal fields were given to France for 15 years. - Eupen and Malmedy were ceded to Belgium after a plebiscite. - Schleswig was returned to Denmark following a plebiscite. - West Prussia was used to create a Polish corridor to the Baltic. - Memel was given to Lithuania, and Prussian Poland was annexed to Poland. - Danzig was internationalized under League of Nations control. - Colonial and Overseas Losses: - Germany lost all African colonies and privileges in China, which were placed under the League of Nations. - Military Restrictions: - German army reduced to 100,000 men; conscription (compulsory enlistment for state service) abolished (formally put to an end). - Banned the production and trade of war materials. - German navy was limited and prohibited from having submarines. - The Rhineland was demilitarized. - War Guilt and Reparations: - Germany accepted full responsibility for the war. - Had to pay huge financial reparations (compensation). - Allied forces occupied the left bank of the Rhine until treaty terms were met. - The Treaty of Versailles was a "dictated peace" as German representatives had no say in its terms. - - Other Treaties - Treaty of St. Germain (1919): Signed with Austria-Hungary, dismembering the empire: - Bohemia and Moravia formed Czechoslovakia. - Bosnia and Herzegovina merged with Serbia to form Yugoslavia. - Italy received South Tyrol, Trentino, and parts of the Adriatic coast. - Poland received Galicia, and Hungary became independent. - Treaty of Neuilly (1919): Bulgaria lost: - Western Thrace to Greece. - Part of Macedonia to Yugoslavia. - Dobruja to Romania. - Treaty of Trianon (1920): Hungary ceded: - Transylvania to Romania. - Slovakia to Czechoslovakia. - Croatia to Yugoslavia, losing all sea access. - Treaty of Sèvres (1920): Signed with Turkey: - Lost all rights in Africa and the Middle East. - Greece occupied Eastern Thrace, Smyrna, and parts of Asia Minor. - Armenia was to become independent. - Dardanelles and Bosphorus became neutral zones. - Turkish nationalist leader Mustafa Kemal rejected the treaty, leading to its replacement by the Treaty of Lausanne. -
