Results of the War - Collapse of four major empires: German, Austro-Hungarian, Ottoman, and Russian. - Rise of new republics and smaller nation-states. - Spread of democratic ideals, but also rise of totalitarian (authoritarian) ideologies (Bolshevism, Fascism, and Nazism). - Formation of the League of Nations to resolve future conflicts. - Advances in labor rights, women's suffrage, medicine, aviation, and military technology. - - Impact of World War I on India - India actively supported Britain: - 300,000 Indian soldiers fought in West Asia, Africa, and Europe. - 800,000 more were later recruited. - Supplied vast amounts of food, materials, and funds. - Contributed £100 million to Britain’s war costs. - Britain’s Response: - Promised self-governance (Montagu Declaration, 20 August 1917). - Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (Government of India Act, 1919) introduced dyarchy. - Failed to satisfy Indian aspirations, especially after oppressive measures: - Rowlatt Act: Allowed arrests without trial. - Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (1919): British troops killed peaceful protestors. - Khilafat Movement: Discontent over British treatment of Turkey. - Rise of Indian nationalism led by Mahatma Gandhi, ultimately culminating in India's independence on 15 August 1947. - The Russian Revolution, 1917 - Introduction - During World War I, two significant events occurred in 1917: - The entry of the United States into the war, which ultimately influenced its outcome. - The Russian Revolution, which marked the end of the Tsarist regime and introduced the “dictatorship of the proletariat”. - The Russian Revolution was the most significant social upheaval (disruption) since the French Revolution. - Circumstances Leading to the Russian Revolution - The revolution, epitomized by the Bolshevik seizure of power in Petrograd on 7 November 1917, had long been in the making due to: - The Russo-Japanese War (1905), which weakened confidence in the Tsarist regime. - Russian military defeats in World War I, leading to further distrust. - Widespread discontent with the inefficient and autocratic rule of the Tsar. - The First Revolution (March 1917) - Initially, Russia entered the war with enthusiasm and saw some early successes. However, by 1915: - Military defeats exposed the regime’s weaknesses. - Munitions (ammunition) and supplies were critically low. - Tsar Nicholas II was weak and influenced by corrupt ministers. - Rumors of a separate peace with Germany fueled unrest. - Rasputin’s influence over the royal family worsened the crisis, leading to his assassination. - Key Events: - Riots erupted among peasants and workers. - Desertions (abandonment) increased within the army. - Food shortages intensified, particularly a shortage of bread. - The Petrograd Soviet (workers’ and soldiers’ council) was formed. - The Duma (parliament) established a Provisional Government, forcing Tsar Nicholas II to abdicate. - The Provisional (interim, temporary) Government - The Provisional Government, led by moderate republicans (Kadets), implemented: - Liberal reforms (freedom of press, speech, and religion). - Plans for a National Constituent Assembly. - Commitment to continuing the war, hoping to maintain patriotic support. - However, the masses prioritized: - Peace - Land reforms - Food security - Growing Discontent: - The revolution shifted from a political movement to a socialist one. - Local Soviets emerged across Russia, mobilizing workers, peasants, and soldiers. - Workers demanded better wages and working conditions. - Peasants seized (take hold forcefully) noble lands. - Soldiers mutinied, and national minorities (Finns, Poles) sought independence. - The Second Revolution (November 1917) - - The Rise of the Bolsheviks: - The failure of the Provisional Government to end the war led to dissatisfaction. - Mensheviks, under Alexander Kerensky, came to power but failed to bring peace. - The Bolsheviks, led by Lenin and Trotsky, opposed the war and advocated immediate socialist reforms. - The Bolshevik Coup (seizure of power): - Germans advanced towards Petrograd, creating instability. - Bolshevik influence grew within the Petrograd Soviet. - In November 1917, the Bolsheviks overthrew the Provisional Government. - Kerensky fled, and Bolsheviks seized power. - Consolidation of Bolshevik Rule - Immediate (action of making something more solid or stronger) Actions: - Lenin negotiated the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk (1918), ending Russia’s involvement in World War I. - Territorial losses were accepted as a necessary price for revolution. - Key Reforms: - Abolition of private property. - Peasants received land to be cultivated for the state. - Factories were handed over to workers. - Compulsory labor was introduced. - Public debts were repudiated (rejected). - The Orthodox Church was stripped of power. - Repression (action of subduing someone): - The Cheka (secret police) executed opposition figures, including Tsar Nicholas II and his family (July 1918). - The Red Army, led by Trotsky, crushed anti-Bolshevik forces, ensuring Bolshevik rule for over 70 years. - Causes of the Russian Revolution - The revolution resulted from long-term dissatisfaction across Russian society: - Peasants (agricultural worker) - Suffered economic insecurity and social oppression. - Small land holdings vs. large aristocratic estates led to resentment (bitterness / anger and disappointment). - Formed the bulk of the Social Revolutionary Party. - Workers - Low wages and harsh working conditions led to discontent. - Many joined the Workmen’s Social Democratic Party. - Middle Class (Bourgeoisie) - Wanted constitutional government to limit autocratic rule. - Intellectuals - Advocated radical political and social reform. - Minority Nationalities (Jews, Poles, Finns, etc.) - Resisted Tsarist policies of Russification (making russian) and sought independence. - Influence of Ideologies - Moderate republicanism and Marxian socialism fueled revolutionary movements. - These diverse elements ultimately overthrew the Tsarist regime, leading to the establishment of a socialist state under the Bolsheviks. - The world from 1919 to 1939 - The Inter-War Period (1919–1939): Prelude to World War II - Aftermath of World War I - World War I, or the Great War, was called the ‘war to end all wars.’ - It was the most devastating conflict in human history up to that time. - Over 37 million combatants lost their lives. - None of the original parties to the war achieved anything substantial. - Despite the devastation, the war ended with a sense of hope. - Post-War Optimism - Many expected that humanity would learn from the horrors of the war. - It was hoped that future territorial disputes would be resolved through structured dispute-settlement mechanisms. - There was optimism that democracy would be safeguarded. - Hopes of equity and progress for all were widespread. - Reality: Continued Chaos and Disillusionment - Contrary to expectations, Europe quickly fell into further chaos. - The United States and Europe remained the political and economic centers of global affairs. - Instead of peace and stability, disillusionment spread across the world. - Persistence of Pre-War Institutions - Colonialism remained intact, keeping over half of the world’s population in subjugation. - Aristocracy, feudalism, supra-nationalism, and militarism continued to exist. - Rather than fading away, these forces shaped the political landscape of the post-war world. - Rise of New Political Forces - Fascism (authoritarian right wing) and Communism (state ownership / collectivism) emerged as dominant ideologies. - These forces played a crucial (decisive) role in destabilizing Europe. - The continent, and consequently the world, moved closer to another catastrophic war. - The ‘Inter-War’ Period (1919–1939) - The period between World War I and World War II is known as the ‘inter-war’ period. - During this time, major world powers and their allies influenced global politics. - Their actions collectively contributed to the conditions that led to World War II. - This chapter analyzes their roles in the unfolding global crisis. - The League of Nations: Mankind’s First Global Peacekeeping Attempt - Introduction - Any discussion on the inter-war period must begin with the League of Nations. - It was humanity’s first attempt at creating a global institution to prevent armed conflict. - The League aimed to serve as a neutral arbiter in international disputes while preserving national sovereignty. - Origin and Vision - The League of Nations was originally the vision of U.S. President Woodrow Wilson (1913–21). - He proposed its formation in his Fourteen Points on 18 January 1918. - The victorious powers incorporated the Covenant (agreement / contract) of the League of Nations into the peace treaties that concluded World War I. - It was designed as a forum where all states, regardless of size, could cooperate to maintain peace and limit military aggression. - Formation and Structure - The Covenant of the League of Nations outlined its principles and operational framework. - An international committee, including leaders like Lord Robert Cecil (Britain), Jan Smuts (South Africa), and Léon Bourgeois (France), drafted the Covenant. - President Wilson ensured that the Covenant was included in every peace treaty, securing its establishment. - Headquarters: Geneva, Switzerland. - Main Organs: - General Assembly – Included all member states. - Council – Had four permanent members: Britain, France, Italy, and Japan. - Permanent Court of International Justice – Based in The Hague, Netherlands. - Secretariat – Managed administrative affairs. - Various Committees – Focused on mandates, dispute resolution, and disarmament. - Peacekeeping Arm – Responsible for conflict resolution. - International Labour Organisation (ILO) – Created under the League’s mandate. - Refugee and Health Organisations – Precursors to modern United Nations (UN) agencies. - Fundamental Weaknesses and Contradictions - The League was flawed from the beginning. - It did not include a clear stance against colonialism. - Instead, it introduced a “mandate” system, where colonies were placed under the control of major powers under the pretext of guiding them toward independence. - Lack of enforcement mechanisms made this provision (providing / supplying) ineffective. - To persuade Japan to join, it allowed Japan to take over former German concessions (allowance / something that is provided in exchange) in China. - To appease France, Germany and Russia were excluded from membership. - The United States refused to join, further weakening the League. - The reasons include internal senate opposition between political parties (Democrats vs Republicans), fear of loss of sovereignty, to sentiment to remain isolated from European conflicts. - As a result, three of the world’s most powerful nations (USA, Germany, and Russia) had no role in the League, significantly diminishing its impact. - Failures of the League - The League repeatedly failed to act in major international crises: - 1920 - Polish Seizure of Vilna: - France-backed Poland seized the Vilna region from Lithuania, and the League did not intervene. - 1923 - Corfu Incident: - Three Italian officials were murdered on Greek territory. - Italian dictator Benito Mussolini blamed Greece and demanded compensation. - Greece refused, leading to an Italian bombing campaign on Corfu. - Instead of League intervention, Britain and France acted as mediators. - 1931 – Japanese Invasion of Manchuria: - Japan seized Manchuria from China in violation of international agreements. - 1936 – Italian Invasion of Ethiopia: - Italy conquered Ethiopia, and the League did nothing. - 1930s – Rise of Adolf Hitler: - By the time Hitler emerged as a European threat, secret treaties and power-balancing tactics had returned, rendering the League obsolete. - Legacy of the League of Nations - The League provided a model for the United Nations (UN), which was established after World War II. - However, during its existence, it failed to prevent major conflicts and proved ineffective in maintaining peace. - With the League’s decline, global politics reverted to military alliances, secret treaties, and power struggles, setting the stage for World War II. - Germany - The Impact of the Treaty of Versailles on Germany - The Treaty’s Effect on German Pride - The Treaty of Versailles left a deep scar on German pride. - Many Germans believed they had been “stabbed in the back” by their own generals and communists at the end of World War I. - This resentment (bitterness) fueled the desire for revenge, contributing to the conditions leading to World War II. - A Historical Perspective on Post-War Punishments - Retaliatory actions after conflicts were not uncommon in European history. - The Congress of Vienna (1815) had imposed heavy penalties on France for the Napoleonic Wars. - Germany had treated France harshly in 1871 after the Franco-Prussian War. - The Treaty of Brest-Litovsk (1918) had imposed severe territorial and economic losses on Russia by Germany. - Compared to Brest-Litovsk, Versailles was lenient—Germany remained geographically and economically intact and preserved its political unity. - The Weimar Republic: Struggles and Challenges - Formation of the Weimar Republic - Germany’s Chancellor Philip Scheidemann called the Treaty an “imprisonment of the German people” but had no choice but to accept it. - The Social Democrats and the Catholic Centre Party formed a new government, signing the Treaty under pressure from the victorious Allies. - The new government, known as the Weimar Republic (1919–1933), was stigmatized (condemned / regarded as unworthy) for accepting Versailles under threat of military occupation. - The Weimar Constitution - A liberal document promising civil liberties and universal adult suffrage. - However, it had structural flaws: - Proportional representation made it easy for small parties to gain seats, destabilizing governance. - The President had the power to appoint or remove the Chancellor, who led the cabinet. - The President could rule by decree (an official order) in national emergencies, enabling dictatorship. - Political Instability and Extremist Threats - Left-Wing and Right-Wing Uprisings - The Republic faced constant threats from both left-wing communists and right-wing conservatives. - In 1919, the Spartacist uprising, led by Rosa Luxemburg and Karl Liebknecht, attempted a communist revolution but was crushed by the Army. Both leaders were executed. - In 1920, the right-wing Kapp Putsch tried to restore Prussian aristocratic dominance, causing government officials to flee Berlin temporarily. - Subsequent strikes and uprisings required military intervention. - Assassinations of key Republican leaders further destabilized Germany. - Economic Crises and Their Political Impact - The Burden of War Reparations - In 1921, the Allies presented Germany with a war reparations (compensation) bill of over $33 billion, forcing the Weimar government to accept under threat of invasion. - The French invaded the Ruhr in 1923 to enforce payments, prompting passive resistance—German workers went on strike while the government subsidized them. - Hyperinflation and Unemployment - Printing excessive currency led to hyperinflation. - Unemployment reached 6 million in the mid-1920s. - Salaries could not keep up with rising prices. - Those with fixed incomes suffered the most. - The Great Depression’s Impact (1929) - Germany’s economic revival had relied on American loans and cheap exports under the Dawes Plan (1924) and the Young Plan (1929). - The Wall Street Crash (1929) caused an economic collapse, further weakening the Republic. - Agricultural prices collapsed, angering farmers. - University students faced disillusionment due to unemployment. - Weimar Republic’s Brief Stability and Stresemann’s Efforts - Gustav Stresemann’s Leadership (1923) - Briefly served as Chancellor before becoming Foreign Minister. - Stabilized the economy by appointing economist Hjalmar Schacht, who introduced a new currency (Rentenmark). - Negotiated new reparation payment methods, leading to the withdrawal of French forces from the Ruhr. - Foreign Policy Achievements - Pursued reconciliation with Britain and France. - Germany joined the League of Nations. - Negotiated the Treaty of Locarno (1925), which secured Germany’s western borders but left eastern borders ambiguous—favoring future territorial revision. - However, nationalist parties, including Hitler’s Nazi Party, opposed Locarno, seeing it as an extension of Versailles. - The End of Weimar and the Rise of Totalitarianism - Despite Stresemann’s efforts, the Great Depression provided an opportunity for radical political forces. - Economic desperation led to a shift towards totalitarian ideologies. - Hitler and the Nazi Party exploited public discontent to rise to power in 1933, marking the end of the Weimar Republic. - The Rise of Nazism - Adolf Hitler’s Early Life and Entry into Politics - Adolf Hitler first appeared on the German political scene in 1923. His rise to power was unprecedented (never known or seen before). - William L. Shirer, the American journalist-historian, described him as an unlikely figure to succeed the legacy of Bismarck, the Hohenzollern emperors, and President Hindenburg. - Born in 1889 as the son of an Austrian customs official, Hitler had an incomplete education and lived as a vagabond (a person who wanders from place to place without a home or job) in Vienna. - During World War I, he fought in the German Army with exceptional valor (bravery / courage / fearlessness), earning high military honors. - After the war, he lived a purposeless life but was deeply affected by Germany’s humiliation, fueling his desire for revenge and driving him into politics. - Formation of the Nazi Party - In 1919, as an intelligence agent for the German Army, Hitler attended a meeting of a small nationalist and anti-Semitic group and later joined it out of curiosity. - By 1920, the group was renamed the National Socialist German Workers Party (Nazi Party). - The party’s Twenty-Five Points demanded: - Repudiation (rejection) of the Treaty of Versailles. - Unification of Austria and Germany. - Exclusion of Jews from German citizenship. - Agrarian reforms and nationalization of industries. - The Nazis conducted their activities with military fanfare, organizing paramilitary groups known as Sturm Abteilung (SA) or Brownshirts. - The 1923 Beer Hall Putsch and Its Aftermath - The French occupation of the Ruhr Valley in 1923 helped the Nazis stir nationalist passions. - On November 9, 1923, Hitler led a failed coup in Munich during a Bavarian legislature meeting at a Beer Hall. - The coup was crushed, and Hitler was sentenced to five years in prison, but he served less than a year before being paroled (permanent or temporary release of a prisoner before the expiry of sentence on the promise of good behaviour). - While in jail, he wrote Mein Kampf (My Struggle) and realized that legal means were necessary to capture power. - The Nazis and the Economic Crisis of 1929 - After Hitler’s release, extreme nationalism lost appeal under the Weimar Republic’s reformist phase. - The Great Depression of 1929 devastated Germany’s economy, increasing unemployment and political instability. - The centrist coalition government collapsed in March 1930 over economic disagreements. - President Paul von Hindenburg ruled by decree and appointed Heinrich Brüning as Chancellor, but the crisis persisted (carried on). - Nazi Electoral Success and Hitler’s Rise to Power - In 1932, von Hindenburg replaced Brüning with Franz von Papen, who sought Nazi support. - The Nazi Party gained 30.1% of votes in 1930, rising from 12 Reichstag seats in 1928 to 107 seats. - To counter opposition, von Papen lifted the ban on Nazi rallies, leading to further electoral gains. - In the July 1932 elections, the Nazis won 230 seats (37.2% of the vote). - Hitler demanded the Chancellorship, but Hindenburg refused. - After political deadlock and von Papen’s resignation, Kurt von Schleicher was appointed Chancellor but dismissed soon after. - On January 30, 1933, Hitler was appointed Chancellor of Germany. - Hitler’s Consolidation of Power - Eliminating Political Opposition - Within a month of taking office, Hitler used the Reichstag Fire incident to suppress communists. - The fire was later revealed to be set by Nazi agents, but they blamed the communists, stirring fears of a Soviet-style revolution. - Hitler secured emergency powers and jailed or executed communist leaders. - In March 1933, the Enabling Act was passed, allowing Hitler to rule by decree. - Establishing a One-Party Dictatorship - Hitler outlawed all political parties by July 1933, making the Nazi Party the only legal party. - Provincial governments were reorganized to suit Nazi interests. - Opposition politicians were imprisoned, sent to concentration camps, or murdered. - Nazi spies monitored civilians, turning Germany into a police state. - Germans were forced to swear personal loyalty to Hitler, addressed as Der Führer (The Leader). - The Suppression of Jews - May 1933: Jewish intellectuals’ books were burned in public bonfires. - 1935 Nuremberg Laws: German Jews lost their citizenship and were expelled from jobs. - November 1938 Kristallnacht (Night of Broken Glass): - Jewish homes, shops, and synagogues were attacked. - Thousands of Jews were arrested. - The Night of the Long Knives (June 30, 1934) - The SA (Brownshirts), led by Ernst Röhm, had grown to one million members. - The German Army feared it would become a rival military force. - To secure the Army’s loyalty, Hitler ordered Röhm’s execution along with key SA leaders. - Former Chancellor von Schleicher was also killed. - A month later, President von Hindenburg died, and Hitler merged the offices of President and Chancellor, becoming the supreme dictator. - Hitler’s Militarization and Foreign Policy - Hitler rearmed Germany, violating the Treaty of Versailles. - In 1936, he remilitarized the Rhineland, but France failed to intervene. - He promoted Lebensraum (“living space”), justifying territorial expansion in Eastern Europe. - Conclusion: Germany’s Path to War - Within two decades of World War I, Germany emerged as a threat to European peace. - The Allied powers, preoccupied with domestic problems, did not resist Hitler’s militarization. - Hitler instilled (gradually but firmly establish) racist ideologies, portraying Germans as Aryans, superior to Jews and Slavs. - This ideology set Germany on the path to another global war. - Meanwhile, a similar authoritarian movement was rising in Italy under Fascism, which we shall now examine. - Italy and the Rise of Fascism - Origins and Ideology of Fascism - Fascism was a political ideology antithetical (directly opposed or contrasted) to democracy - Fascism opposed: - Democracy - Parliamentarism - Individual liberty - Freedom of expression - State control over businesses (though it clashed with communism) - Derived from the Italian word fasces, meaning the authority of the state over all. - Fascists believed: - Democratic politics weakened nations by prioritizing partisan interests over national unity. - Single-party rule was necessary to maintain order and strength. - Benito Mussolini: - First fascist dictator, came to power in 1922. - Declared in 1931: - “The Fascist conception of the state is all-embracing, and outside of the state, no human or spiritual values can exist.” - Fascist governments in Italy, Germany, and Spain were characterized by: - Single-party rule - Use of terror - Regimentation (organized according to a strict system) of society - Widespread police surveillance - Mussolini’s Early Life and Political Rise - Born in 1883, son of a blacksmith. - Entered politics in 1912 as a socialist. - In 1914: - Left socialism. - Joined the Italian Army (Italy fought on the Allied side in WWI). - Was wounded in action. - After WWI (1919): - Founded Fasci di Combattimento (Band of Combatants) in Milan. - Nationalist sentiment grew in Italy due to: - A feeling of betrayal, as the country was denied territorial gains despite being on the victorious side. - Dysfunctional parliamentary system. - Widespread internal turmoil, strikes, and unrest. - Fear of a communist takeover. - The Rise of the Black Shirts and Seizure of Power - Mussolini organized the Black Shirts to: - Terrorize Socialists (the most powerful party at the time). - Attack strikers and farm workers. - Gained support from: - Conservatives - Industrialists - 1921: - Mussolini and 34 of his followers elected to the Chamber of Deputies. - October 1922: - Organized the March on Rome (hundreds of thousands of Black Shirts participated). - King Victor Emmanuel III, alarmed, sent a telegram on 29 October asking Mussolini to become Prime Minister. - Mussolini’s Establishment of Dictatorship - Initially, Mussolini’s Fascists were a minority in the Chamber of Deputies. - He demanded dictatorial powers from the King: - The King, pressured by big business, agreed on 23 November 1922. - Given dictatorial powers for one year to ensure good governance. - Mussolini’s actions to consolidate power: - Removed elected representatives from lower government levels. - Replaced them with Fascists. - 1924: - Changed election laws: - The party with the largest popular vote would automatically receive two-thirds of Parliament seats. - Coalition governments became obsolete. - 1925 elections: - Fascists gained complete control over the Chamber of Deputies. - 1925–1926: - Passed laws granting Mussolini: - Power to rule by decree. - Authority to dissolve all other political parties. - Mussolini’s Policies and Downfall - Ruthless against opponents. - Gained popularity among the masses through: - Large public projects. - Social security for workers. - Subsidized vacations. - 1929: - Won over the Catholic Church: - Gave the Pope sovereignty over the Vatican. - Made Roman Catholicism the state religion. - Late 1920s: - Strengthened Italy’s economy. - 1930s: - Great Depression weakened Italy’s economy. - To maintain power, Mussolini initiated military adventures abroad. - Italy’s aggressive policies contributed to Europe’s instability, leading to World War II. - Spain and the Spanish Civil War - Spain Before the Civil War - 1 Constitutional Monarchy (1885–1923) - Spain was a constitutional monarchy under King Alfonso XIII (ruled since 1885). - 2 Rule of General Primo de Rivera (1923–1930) - 1923: General Primo de Rivera staged a coup and ruled with the King’s approval. - Though a dictator, he was: - Not a fascist. - Progressive in implementing public works and industrialization. - Successfully resolved Spain’s problems in Morocco (1925). - Spain remained stable until the Great Depression affected its economy. - The economic crisis led to: - Financial ruin. - Rising unemployment. - Loss of Army support for de Rivera. - 1930: De Rivera resigned, and King Alfonso XIII abdicated (resigned / give up throne). - A Republic was proclaimed. - Problems Faced by the New Republic - Political and Regional Conflicts: - Catalonia and the Basque provinces demanded separation. - The Roman Catholic Church opposed the Republic and wanted the King’s return. - Economic Crisis: - Wine and olive export markets collapsed. - Iron and steel production declined. - Unemployment rose. - Public discontent against the new government. - Right-Wing Reforms and Resistance: - Conservative Right-Wing parties controlled the Cortes (Parliament). - They were supported by socialists and middle-class radicals. - Attempted reforms: - Allowed Catalonia some autonomy. - Increased workers’ wages. - Nationalized large estates. - Separated the Church from the State. - These reforms caused internal conflicts among the Right-Wing factions. - Pro-Church CEDA party defended: - The Church. - The rich landlords. - Rise of Fascism and the Road to Civil War - 1932: Army officers attempted a coup against Prime Minister Manuel Azaña, but it failed. - Left-Wing (Communist-inspired) parties launched agitations to weaken the Republic. - 1933 elections: - Azaña’s government was defeated. - A Right-Wing government, dominated by the CEDA, was formed. - The government failed to restore peace. - Formation of the Fascist Party (Falange, 1933): - Founded by José Antonio de Rivera, son of the former dictator. - Supported by the Army. - 1936: The Army incited rebellion in Spanish Morocco, sparking the Spanish Civil War. - The Spanish Civil War (1936–1939) - Brutal conflict with atrocities on both sides. - Became a proxy war for world powers. - Key participants: - Fascists (led by General Francisco Franco): - Received arms, troops, food, and ammunition from Hitler (Germany) and Mussolini (Italy). - Republicans: - Direct support from the Soviet Union (Russia). - Unofficial support from Britain and France (sent “volunteers” to fight). - March 1939: Franco captured Madrid and declared himself Dictator. - Ruled Spain until 1975. - Significance of the Spanish Civil War - Though an internal conflict, it is often called a “Little World War” due to international involvement. - Spain remained neutral in World War II under Franco’s rule. - Britain and France in the Inter-War Period - Britain in the Inter-War Years - 1 Economic Decline Post-World War I - Britain emerged victorious from World War I but lost its position as the world’s foremost industrial power to the United States. - The country suffered from economic stagnation, like the rest of Europe. - To revive the economy, the government implemented: - Wage reductions to make British goods more competitive. - This backfired, leading to strikes and industrial unrest. - Political impact: - The failure of economic policies ended Conservative supremacy. - The Labour Party came to power in 1924 and 1929. - The Great Depression (1929) worsened economic conditions. - 1931: A National Government was formed with representatives from the Conservative, Liberal, and Labour parties. - 2 Social and Economic Challenges - Women’s Suffrage (1918): - Representation of the People’s Act granted women the right to vote, but: - The voting age for women was 30, while for men, it was 21. - Economic Problems: - 1920: A slump in British overseas trade led to massive unemployment. - Labour Party demanded: - Reforming the industrial system along socialist lines. - Strikes in railways and coal industries shook the country. - 3 The Irish Problem (1918–1922) - 1918 elections: - The old Irish Nationalist Party was defeated. - Sinn Féin emerged as the dominant party. - Sinn Féin leaders Michael Collins and Arthur Griffith: - Advocated Republicanism. - Set up a Republican government in Ireland. - Rejected British authority. - British response: - Offered to establish two separate governments in Dublin and Belfast. - Brutally suppressed Irish resistance. - Led to severe public resentment (bitterness) in both Ireland and Britain. - 1921: Under pressure from the United States, the David Lloyd George government negotiated a settlement: - Irish Free State was created with British dominion status. - Accepted by Griffith and Collins, but rejected by Eamon de Valera. - 1922: Irish Civil War erupted between: - Republicans. - Those who accepted the 1921 treaty. - 4 Britain’s Industrial and Economic Struggles - Britain’s economic power, built in the 19th century, declined significantly. - Before World War I, Britain dominated: - Textiles, heavy engineering, and manufactured goods. - Post-war competition: - United States and Germany captured British markets. - Asian and African colonies started producing their own goods. - Tariff barriers in America and Europe made British exports uncompetitive. - Great Depression impact (1929): - Britain faced a severe financial crisis. - The James Ramsay MacDonald government had to: - Raise taxes (a politically risky move). - Establish a National Government in 1931, including all major parties. - Recovery Measures: - Increased taxes and wage cuts (unpopular but necessary). - Trade agreements with Canada and Australia boosted exports. - By 1934, the economy showed signs of recovery. - France in the Inter-War Years - 1 Economic Challenges and Political Struggles - France faced severe economic problems, worsened by: - The threat of Fascist and Communist movements. - The Bolshevik takeover in Russia (1917), which deprived France of an anti-German ally. - French Military Interventions: - Sent troops to support anti-communist forces in Russia (White Russians). - 1920: Helped Poland defeat Russia in the Polish-Soviet War. - Rising international tensions: - 1935: Britain signed the Anglo-German Naval Agreement, allowing Germany to build submarines (violating the Treaty of Versailles). - France felt betrayed and was unable to counter Hitler’s remilitarization of the Rhineland. - 2 Economic Development Amidst Class Struggles - France’s economic condition improved during the inter-war years. - However, class conflicts emerged, hindering national progress. - Governments of Raymond Poincaré (1922–1924) and Édouard Herriot (1924–1926): - Industries prospered, but at the cost of labor rights. - Rise of the United States - The United States as an Economic Power - By the late 19th century, the U.S. had already become a global economic power. - After World War I, its vast industrial capacity and large domestic market reinforced its dominance in global trade. - During World War I, the U.S. loaned the Allies over $12 billion at a 5% interest rate. - In the 1920s, the U.S. government encouraged American corporations to expand profits in Europe, Canada, and Central and South America. - The Republican Party (1921–1933) followed an isolationist policy, keeping the U.S. out of European politics. - The U.S. did not join the League of Nations but could not fully disengage due to its global economic interests. - The American public demanded repayment of loans given to Britain and France. - Domestic Policies and Social Inequality - Successive U.S. Presidents in the inter-war years ignored social reforms. - Government policies prioritized big businesses over workers’ rights. - When individual states passed progressive laws, the Supreme Court often struck them down. - The economic boom benefited private industries but widened the gap between the rich and the poor. - Labour rights were weak, and wages did not grow in proportion to economic expansion. - U.S. Intervention in Germany’s Financial Crisis - The U.S. played a key role in stabilizing Germany’s economy during its financial crisis in 1923. - Under the Dawes Plan (1924) and Young Plan (1929), ‘German bonds’ were sold to American investors. - This financial aid helped Germany pay reparations to Britain and France. - Britain and France, in turn, used that money to repay their debts to the U.S. - Economic Boom and the Great Depression - The U.S. economy boomed in the 1920s due to strong industrial growth and financial capacity. - American consumers created high demand for domestic goods. - European products struggled to compete due to high tariff barriers protecting U.S. industries. - By 1927, consumer demand peaked and then started to decline. - The stock market became overextended, with inflated share prices and risky margin trading. - In October 1929, the Wall Street Crash triggered a financial collapse. - The Great Depression began, shrinking the market to one-tenth of its previous value. - American investments in German bonds stopped, worsening the global financial crisis. - Banks collapsed, industries shut down, and unemployment soared. - By 1933, 14 million people were jobless, and wages had drastically fallen. - Great depression - The Great Depression: The Worst Economic Crisis - The Great Depression was the worst economic crisis in history. - It began in the United States and spread across the industrialized world. - The primary cause was the extreme inequality in wealth distribution during the 1920s. - Causes - Wealth Inequality and Its Impact - Between 1923 and 1929, the U.S. national income grew from $74.3 billion to $89 billion. - However, this wealth was not evenly shared. - The top 0.1% of Americans had the same income as the bottom 42%. - This elite group controlled 34% of all savings, while 80% of Americans had no savings at all. - The Wage-Consumption Imbalance - Industrial productivity increased, but wages did not grow proportionally. - Business owners made large profits by keeping wages low. - A healthy economy requires a balance between supply and demand. - By the late 1920s, an oversupply of goods existed, but consumers lacked purchasing power. - The Rise of Consumer Credit - With limited incomes, Americans turned to credit to buy goods. - The “buy-now-pay-later” trend became widespread. - By the late 1920s: - 60% of cars and 80% of radios were bought on installment plans. - Outstanding installment credit doubled from $1.38 billion in 1925 to over $3 billion in 1929. - People spent most of their income repaying past loans, reducing their ability to make new purchases. - The Fragile Economic Foundation - The U.S. economy depended on luxury spending and investments by the wealthy. - This worked only as long as the rich had confidence in the economy. - The economic structure was weak: - 200 corporations controlled over half of corporate wealth. - The automobile sector thrived, but agriculture suffered. - Many industries relied on car and radio production (steel, glass, rubber, petrol, construction). - Demand for cars and radios was limited, while food was a necessity—but agriculture was in ruins by 1929. - International Economic Instability - America’s economic boom in the 1920s contrasted with Europe’s struggles after World War I. - During the war, the U.S. loaned European allies $7 billion and another $3.3 billion in the 1920s. - Under the 1924 Dawes Plan, the U.S. lent money to Germany. - Over 90% of these loans were used to buy American goods. - However, the U.S. imposed high tariffs on European products, preventing European recovery and repayment of debts. - The Culture of Stock Market Speculation - Many Americans turned to stock market investments to pay off debts. - In the absence of wage growth, people borrowed money to buy shares. - Brokers allowed investors to buy shares with loans: - A person could buy a $75 share with just $10 of their own money, borrowing the remaining $65. - As stock prices surged, investors could sell at a higher price, repay the broker, and keep profits. - However, the stock boom was not based on real economic growth—many companies with soaring stock prices were financially weak. - The 1929 Stock Market Crash - By mid-1929, brokers’ outstanding loans totaled over $7 billion, rising to $8.5 billion in just three months. - Share prices suddenly began to fall. - Panic selling followed, and on “Black Tuesday” (October 29, 1929), stock prices collapsed. - Buyers disappeared, and share values plummeted. - The Economic Collapse and Global Impact - The stock market crash destroyed the confidence of wealthy Americans. - Luxury spending and investments stopped. - The middle and lower classes could no longer pay off loans or afford new purchases. - This led to a decline in industrial demand and production. - Mass layoffs followed, and unemployment skyrocketed. - The crisis spread to Europe and other nations reliant on U.S. investment. - Unemployment in the U.S. rose from 5 million in 1930 to 13 million in 1932. - The country spiraled into economic disaster—this was the Great Depression. - The Impact of the Depression and Roosevelt’s New Deal - End of Republican Governance - The Great Depression ended 11 years of Republican rule. - Republican Presidents: - Warren Harding (1921-23) - Calvin Coolidge (1923-29) - Herbert Hoover (1929-33) - Franklin D. Roosevelt (Democrat) introduced the New Deal to revive the economy. - Franklin D. Roosevelt: A Dynamic Leader - Suffered from polio, leaving his legs completely paralyzed. - Displayed great determination but could never walk unaided. - Excellent communicator, gained popularity through radio addresses. - First and only US President to serve four terms. - Later, a law was passed limiting Presidents to two terms. - Objectives of the New Deal - Relief – Direct aid to the poor, homeless, and unemployed. - Recovery – Reduce unemployment and boost economic demand. - Reform – Prevent another Wall Street crash. - Key Policies of the New Deal - Banking & Financial Reforms - Government temporarily took over banks and financial institutions. - Securities Exchange Commission (1934) regulated the stock market. - Investors buying shares on credit had to pay at least 50% upfront. - Agricultural Support - Farmer’s Relief Act (1933) paid farmers to reduce production, raising prices. - By 1937, farmers’ incomes doubled but left many farm laborers jobless. - Employment & Infrastructure Development - Civilian Conservation Corps created 2.5 million jobs by 1940. - Workers earned $30 per month, with $25 sent directly to families. - Provided free food, clothing, and necessities. - National Industrial Recovery Act (1933) - Created Public Works Administration for large infrastructure projects. - Built dams, roads, bridges, hospitals, schools, and airports. - Inspired by John Maynard Keynes (Keynesian economics). - Labour Reforms - National Recovery Administration - Abolished child labor. - Introduced an eight-hour workday. - Encouraged, but did not force, businesses to comply. - Wagner Act (1935) - Recognized labor unions. - Established National Labour Relations Board for worker grievances. - Fair Labour Standards Act (1938) - Maximum 45-hour workweek. - Minimum wage introduced. - Social Security Act (1935) - Old-age pensions. - Unemployment insurance. - Impact of the New Deal - Millions of jobs created. - Unemployment fell to 8% by 1937. - Restored public confidence in the government. - Prevented a communist revolution. - Tennessee Valley Authority and other projects provided lasting infrastructure. - First step towards making the US a welfare state. - Debate: The New Deal vs. World War II - Historians agree Roosevelt saved democracy from fascism and communism. - Debate over whether the New Deal or World War II caused lasting economic recovery. - Arguments Against the New Deal - Did not fully end the Depression. - World War II played a bigger role in economic recovery. - World War II’s Economic Impact - US supplied tanks, aircraft, and weapons to Europe from 1939 onward. - Between June 1940 and December 1941, supplied Britain with 23,000 warplanes. - After Pearl Harbor (December 1941), war production surged. - By 1942, 100% employment was achieved in the US. - Between 1939 and 1945, GNP almost doubled. - World War II solidified US global economic dominance. - Thus, while the New Deal stabilized the country, World War II ensured long-term economic prosperity. - Communism, totalitarianism and the road to world war II - Reassessment of World War II’s Causes - Traditional View on World War II’s Origins - Previously, Adolf Hitler’s ambitions were seen as the main cause of the war. - Governments worldwide discouraged scholars from exploring other factors. - Post-Cold War Revelations - Collapse of the USSR led to access to suppressed Soviet documents. - Confessions (a former statement admitting one is guilty) from former Soviet leaders revealed new perspectives. - It became clear that communism, like fascism, contributed to World War II. - Hitler’s Responsibility Remains Undisputed - These revelations do not diminish Hitler’s role in starting the war. - His crimes against humanity and warmongering (encouragement or advocacy of aggression towards others) remain undeniable. - Stalin’s Role in World War II - Contemporary scholars now view Joseph Stalin as equally responsible for the war. - Hitler and Stalin share the blame for the conflict’s outbreak. - The Rise of Communism and Vladimir Ilyich Lenin’s Rule - - Socialism vs. Communism in 19th-Century Europe - Political debates in Europe’s capitals focused on Marxian socialism. - Differing views: - Reject socialism, - Accept it, or - Blend it with capitalism for a softer state approach. - Communism and socialism became major global ideologies post-1917. - Birth of Communism: The Bolshevik Revolution (1917) - Vladimir Lenin’s Bolsheviks seized power from Kerensky’s Social Democratic government in October-November 1917. - Led to a brutal civil war between: - ‘White’ Russians (Tsar supporters) and - ‘Red’ Bolsheviks (Lenin’s followers). - Lenin emerged victorious. - Lenin’s Key Policies - Abolition of Capitalism - Marxist ideology saw private capital as a source of inequality and exploitation. - Lenin’s priority was eliminating capitalism entirely. - Rapid Industrialization - The economy was organized like an army, with state-appointed managers controlling all sectors. - Lenin anticipated future wars to defend communism. - Western nations (US, Japan, France, Britain) supported White Russians during the civil war. - August 1918 assassination attempt on Lenin led to the first ‘Red Terror’ purge—thousands of socialist opponents were executed. - New Economic Policy (NEP) (1921) - Introduced to revive the economy after the civil war. - Key features: - Peasants could keep surplus produce after taxation. - Limited private trade was permitted. - Opposition from hardliners (e.g., Kamenev, Zinoviev), who feared the kulaks (rich peasants) as potential threats to communism. - Lenin saw NEP as temporary, aiming for full state control over agriculture through collective farms. - Outcome: Economic recovery and improved living conditions. - Lenin’s Political Repression - March 1921: Lenin banned ‘factionalism’ within the Communist Party. - Consequences: - Expulsion or execution for those who opposed party policies. - Late 1921: One-third of party members purged via the Cheka (secret police). - Lenin’s Death and Its Aftermath - Died in January 1924 at age 53. - Established the world’s first communist state, but global communist uprisings did not occur. - USSR remained isolated in world politics. - Lenin’s early death led to a power struggle and the worst phase of Red Terror was yet to come. - Joseph Stalin’s Rise and Policies - Power Struggle After Lenin’s Death - Leon Trotsky was expected to succeed Lenin, but after a five-year power struggle, Joseph Stalin emerged victorious. - Stalin’s real name: Joseph Djugashvili; adopted the name Stalin (meaning “man of steel”) after joining the Bolsheviks. - Challenges Faced by the Soviet Economy - The Soviet Union faced economic and political problems: - Weak economy and industrial underdevelopment. - Fear of foreign invasion. - Stalin introduced radical measures to address these issues: - Five-Year Plans for industrial growth. - Collectivization of agriculture. - Establishment of a totalitarian regime. - Industrialization Through Five-Year Plans - Industrialization was conducted through a planned process: - First Five-Year Plan (1928-1932): Focused on heavy industries (coal, iron, steel, oil, heavy engineering). - Second and Third Plans: Continued emphasis on heavy industries but also included consumer goods. - Achievements by 1940: - USSR overtook Britain in iron and steel production. - USSR nearly matched Germany’s coal output. - Thousands of factories built in remote towns (safe from invasion). - Key industrial projects: - Iron and steel works at Magnitogorsk. - Tractor factories at Kharkov and Gorki. - Oil refineries in the Caucasus Mountains. - Harsh Conditions for Workers - Despite industrial growth, the Soviet Union was not a worker’s paradise: - Strict discipline enforced on workers. - Productivity rewards were given, but failure to meet targets led to severe punishment. - Workers accused of ‘sabotaging the revolution’ were executed or sent to forced labor camps. - Living conditions: - Workers lived in primitive housing. - Party officials enjoyed luxury, with residences in holiday resorts. - Historian Richard Freeborn: “The First Five-Year Plan was a declaration of war by the state against workers and peasants, subjecting them to greater exploitation than under capitalism.” - Brutal Collectivization of Agriculture - Stalin opposed Lenin’s New Economic Policy (NEP), especially its leniency towards kulaks (wealthy peasants). - 1929: Stalin launched a policy to merge small farms into large collective farms (kolkhoz). - Massive opposition from peasants: - Thousands deported or executed for resisting. - Millions died from hunger and repression. - Food security worsened: - Total grain production in 1934 was lower than in 1928. - Famine of 1932-33 led to five million peasant deaths. - Despite famine, USSR exported two million tonnes of grain to claim program success. - Revelations (surprising and previously unknown facts) after the Soviet collapse (1991): - Previously suppressed archives exposed the scale of atrocities (act of barbarity / act of brutality). - The Black Book of Communism (1999) documented: - Over two million deportations. - Six million deaths from hunger. - Hundreds of thousands died due to deportation. - Some historians claim Stalin welcomed the famine as a way to crush peasant resistance. - Stalin’s Totalitarian Rule and the Great Purge (cleanse / clear / free someone from) - Stalin’s Quest for Absolute Power - Stalin believed that all political activities must be controlled under his totalitarian regime. - He sought complete, unchallenged power and was intolerant of criticism. - His first major purge began in December 1934, following the murder of Sergei Kirov, one of his supporters. - Recent research suggests that Stalin himself ordered Kirov’s murder to justify eliminating his perceived enemies. - Even Nikita Khrushchev, Stalin’s successor, admitted this more than two decades later. - By this time, Stalin’s main opponent, Trotsky, had already fled into exile. - In 1940, Stalin’s agent murdered Trotsky in Mexico. - The Great Purge (1934-1938) - Over the next four years, hundreds of Communist Party officials were arrested and put through show trials with pre-determined sentences. - Key victims included many of the Bolsheviks who had led the 1917 revolution: - Zinoviev, Kamenev, Bukharin, and Radek. - Most of the top military officers of the Red Army were also executed. - Between December 1934 and February 1935: - More than 6,500 people were executed. - Thousands more were deported to Siberia or the Central Asian Republics. - The Great Terror (1936-1938) – Ezhovshchina - Known as Ezhovshchina, the Great Terror was one of the largest state-sponsored purges in human history. - The full extent of Stalin’s repression remains uncertain, but Khrushchev publicly admitted the scale of the crimes at the 22nd Party Congress of the CPSU in October 1961. - Estimated casualties: - At least 6 million arrested, jailed, or deported. - 3 million executions. - 2 million deaths in labor camps. - Revisionist historians claim the figures are exaggerated, but similar denials exist regarding the Nazi Holocaust. - Stalin’s 1936 Constitution – A False Democracy - Stalin introduced a New Constitution in 1936, creating an illusion of democracy: - Secret ballot voting allowed citizens to choose members of the Supreme Soviet. - However, the Supreme Soviet met for only two weeks a year and merely rubber-stamped Stalin’s decisions. - Real power lay with a small Presidium and the Union of Soviet Commissars, where Stalin served as Secretary. - Stalin’s Control Over Culture and Religion - Stalin demanded unquestioning loyalty from writers, artists, and musicians: - Maxim Gorky visited the Solovetsky prison camp (one of the worst Stalinist labor camps) and later wrote a book praising it. - Dmitri Shostakovich, a composer, was condemned (sentenced to a particular punished, especially death) because his opera “Lady Macbeth of Mtsensk” failed to please Stalin. - Religious repression: - Despite harsh measures against the Russian Orthodox Church, including the murder of priests, the people’s faith remained strong. - In the Central Asian Republics, Stalin suppressed the largely Islamic population: - Mosques were burned down. - Centuries-old Islamic heritage was destroyed. - Yet, the faith of the people did not waver. - Hitler’s Fascist Regime and the Nazi Atrocities - Hitler’s Brand of Fascism - Hitler’s fascism was similar to Mussolini’s in Italy but more extreme in its totalitarianism and brutality. - Unlike communists, the Nazis did not even pretend to uphold democracy. - Dissent (holding different opinion from formal ones) was forcibly suppressed, with strict censorship of the press and discouragement of foreign journalists. - State propaganda was widely used: - Books deemed ‘subversive’ were burned. - A secret police force monitored every citizen. - Anti-Semitism (anti-jewinesh) as State Policy - Nazism institutionalized anti-Semitism, with Hitler’s SS forces leading the elimination of Jews in occupied territories. - Jewish persecution (oppression / victimization) had deep historical roots in Europe: - Christian dogma had long treated Jews as an ‘undesirable race’, believing they had betrayed Jesus Christ. - The Catholic Church historically supported state-sponsored persecution of Jews. - Hitler’s own hatred for Jews developed during his struggling years in Vienna, influenced by anti-Semitic propaganda. - However, the Nazis did not promote Christianity: - Though Hitler was nominally Catholic, he prohibited the Church from interfering in state matters. - Thousands of Catholic priests and nuns were imprisoned or killed. - Yet, Hitler managed to get the Pope to remain silent on the Jewish genocide (systematic killing of humans in large numbers). - Racial Superiority and the ‘Master Race’ Doctrine - Hitler promoted the theory of Aryan racial superiority, believing Germans were the ‘master race’ (Aryans) with a divine right to rule over ‘inferior’ people. - He classified East Europeans and non-white races as inferior but modified this stance to accommodate Japan as an ally during World War II. - The glorification of war, conquest, and authoritarian rule was not Hitler’s invention: - The British had used similar racial theories to justify their colonial rule over Asia and Africa. - The Persecution of Jews – From Discrimination to Genocide - Hitler’s anti-Jewish policies began early in his rule: - Jews were banned from employment and business. - They were forced to wear armbands with the Star of David. - Public humiliation, exclusion from sports and the arts, and other restrictions reduced them to ‘sub-humans’. - Kristallnacht (Night of Broken Glass) – 9 November 1938: - Organized by the Nazi Party, this marked the beginning of systematic savagery against Jews. - It culminated in the Holocaust, which continued until the end of World War II. - The Final Solution (1942) and the Holocaust: - Over 6 million Jews and hundreds of thousands of other ‘sub-human’ races were systematically murdered. - Mass killings were conducted using advanced technology, making Nazi genocide the most industrialized slaughter in history. - While more people were killed under communist regimes, the Nazis used science and technology purely for mass extermination. - The World’s Indifference to Nazi Atrocities - The true extent of Nazi brutality was not widely known outside Germany before World War II. - Nations continued trading and maintaining diplomatic ties with Nazi Germany until the war broke out in September 1939. - Reactions to Nazi anti-Semitism: - Countries like the USA imposed strictures against Germany due to discrimination against Jews. - The 1936 Berlin Olympics became a political tool: - Several nations, including the USA, considered boycotting the event. - To avoid controversy, Hitler temporarily softened his anti-Jewish stance during the Games but resumed persecution afterward. - Failure to assist Jewish refugees: - International Jewish organizations pleaded with Western countries to allow Jewish emigration—most requests were ignored. - U.S. President Franklin D. Roosevelt ordered American embassies to slow down visa processing for Jewish refugees. - Even after intelligence confirmed mass killings in concentration camps, the USA refused to conduct commando operations or air raids to disrupt the extermination process. - Japan’s Aggression and Its Alliance with Nazi Germany - Japan’s Expansionist Policies in Asia - Japan pursued a policy of aggression, aiming to expand its influence in Asia. - Invasion of Manchuria (1931): - Manchuria, a Chinese province with 30 million people, was invaded by Japan. - The League of Nations failed to take any action. - Japan as a Hegemonic Power: - Since the late 19th century, Japan had built a powerful navy, dominating eastern Asia. - Impact of the 1929 Great Depression: - Japan’s exports declined, and the price of raw silk (its chief product) fell to less than a fifth of the 1923 level. - This led to widespread poverty and resentment (bitterness). - Conflict with China: - Japanese traders and businessmen in Manchuria were being pushed out, leading to tensions. - Fascist groups within the Japanese Army launched the invasion without government approval. - When Prime Minister Inukai criticized the invasion, he was assassinated. - After this, the Japanese Army gained dominance over the government. - Full-scale Invasion of China (1937): - Encouraged by its success in Manchuria, Japan invaded China, dragging the country into World War II. - The Germany-Japan Alliance Against Communism - Fear of Communism as a Unifying Factor: - Both Germany and Japan feared the spread of communism, particularly through the Comintern (Communist International), which had been founded by Lenin in 1919 to promote global communist revolutions. - Stalin’s Five-Year Plans were showing success, increasing concerns that the USSR would expand its influence. - Formation of the Anti-Comintern Pact (25 November 1936): - Germany and Japan signed the pact, officially presenting it as a propaganda (information of misleading nature) move to exploit global anti-communist sentiments (point of view). - Context of the Anti-Comintern Pact: - In 1935, Britain, France, and Italy had formed the Stresa Front to condemn Hitler’s rearmament. - France also strengthened ties with the USSR, fearing Germany’s resurgence. - These developments alarmed Hitler, pushing him to portray himself as a global fighter against communism. - Secret Protocol in the Anti-Comintern Pact: - If the USSR attacked Germany or Japan, both nations would consult on countermeasures to protect their shared interests. - They agreed not to assist the USSR in any way. - Neither country would sign separate treaties with the USSR without mutual consent. - Appeasement and the Road to World War II - Duplicity in International Relations - The inter-war period was marked by political duplicity, where Hitler benefited from the “conspiracy of silence” practiced by the Allied powers. - The Stresa Front (1935), formed by Britain, France, and Italy to counter German rearmament, collapsed quickly when: - Britain, without consulting France or Italy, signed the Anglo-German Naval Agreement (June 1935). - Inspired by the aggressive expansion of Germany and Japan, Mussolini invaded Abyssinia (October 1935). - Communism as the Greater Threat - In the 1930s, Western powers viewed communism, not Fascism or Japan, as the greatest threat. - Hitler exploited this fear, shifting focus onto the USSR’s international communist agenda. - Conservative politicians in London, Paris, and Washington saw Hitler as a bulwark against Soviet expansion, leading them to adopt a policy of appeasement. - Britain and France’s Policy of Appeasement - Neville Chamberlain (British PM, May 1937) took appeasement to new levels. - Historian Norman Lowe noted that Chamberlain sought to “find out what Hitler wanted” and negotiate rather than use force. - Consequences of Appeasement: - German Rearmament: No action was taken to check Hitler’s military buildup. - Anglo-German Naval Agreement (1935): Signed without consulting France, shaking French confidence in Britain. - Reoccupation of the Rhineland (1936): - The French could have easily expelled German forces, but they hesitated. - When Britain offered no support, France remained passive, despite this being a clear violation of the Versailles Treaty. - Spanish Civil War (1936-39): - Nazi Germany backed General Franco, but neither Britain nor France intervened. - Anschluss (March 1938): - Hitler annexed Austria, a move many in Britain saw as a “natural union”. - Munich Agreement (September 1938): - Chamberlain encouraged Hitler to take Czechoslovakia instead of resisting. - Czechoslovakia had one of Europe’s strongest armies, and an Anglo-French-Czech alliance could have deterred Hitler. - Yet, Chamberlain yielded, returning to London declaring “peace for our time”. - The Final Steps Toward War - After seizing all of Czechoslovakia (March 1939), Hitler turned to Poland. - Germany’s grievances with Poland (1919 Treaty of Versailles): - Poland was granted the port of Danzig, which Germany wanted back. - Hitler demanded a road-rail corridor linking East Prussia to Germany. - Poland refused, trusting British support. - Chamberlain’s Diplomatic Failure: - Britain needed Russian support to counter Germany in Poland. - A British delegation was sent to Moscow (11 August 1939), but no agreement was reached. - The Nazi-Soviet Pact and the Outbreak of War - On 23 August 1939, the world was stunned when Germany and the USSR signed a Non-Aggression Pact. - Two ideological enemies—Nazis and Communists—had joined hands. - The pact guaranteed Soviet neutrality when Germany attacked Poland. - Secret Clauses (revealed in 1989): - Germany and the USSR agreed to divide Eastern Europe: - Western Poland → Germany - Eastern Poland → USSR - Lithuania → Germany - Estonia, Latvia, Finland, Bessarabia, Ukraine, and Belarus → USSR - The Start of World War II - 1 September 1939: - Germany invaded Poland. - 3 September 1939: - Britain and France gave Germany two days to withdraw. - When Hitler refused, they declared war on Germany. - World War II had begun. - Developments in Asia, Africa and South Africa - Reassessing World History Beyond Europe - Eurocentrism in Historical Perspective - There is a common tendency to equate European history with world history. - In the 20th century, many scholars viewed global developments through a Euro-American lens. - While the 19th and 20th centuries were undeniably shaped by European political and social movements, it would be a mistake to overlook the reverse influence. - Asia, Africa, and South America not only responded to European influences but also impacted political relations within Europe itself. - Additionally, local histories in these regions shaped the lives of more than three-fifths of humanity, a perspective often neglected in traditional historiography. - The Role of Non-European Regions in Global Politics - During the late 19th and 20th centuries, European and American strategic and colonial interests dictated the fate of many countries in these continents. - However, the people in these regions were also evolving their own political and social consciousness. - By the early 20th century, these internal transformations led to significant domestic upheavals, which in turn: - Influenced European affairs. - Drew America deeper into global politics. - Examining Global Interactions - This chapter will explore the complex relationship between European, American, and non-European historical developments. - A key example is Japan, an ancient civilization that stood alone against the combined power of Europe and America during World War II. - Understanding Japan’s role offers crucial insights into the broader interplay of global forces beyond the traditional Eurocentric narrative. - The Rise of Japan: From Industrial Power to Militarist Expansion - Japan’s Strength and Early Expansion - Despite its small size, high population density, and lack of natural resources, Japan emerged as a major power in the late 19th century. - In 1854, the United States, under Commodore Mathew C. Perry, signed a treaty with Japan, opening trade with America, which accelerated industrial growth. - This economic expansion fueled imperial ambitions, leading to: - The Sino-Japanese War (1894-95), where Japan defeated China and gained control over Formosa (Taiwan). - The Russo-Japanese War (1904-05) over the Sakhalin Islands, where Japan’s victory shattered the myth of European military superiority over Asia. - The annexation of Korea in 1910, further solidifying Japan’s position as a dominant power in the region. - Japan in World War I and Its Economic Boom - Japan had been a British ally since the Anglo-Japanese Alliance of 1902. - During World War I, Japan supported the Allies, using its industrial base to supply war materials to Britain and America. - Key benefits from the war included: - Expanding its commercial influence in Asia, particularly in China. - Strengthening its Navy by training with the British and American fleets. - Gaining Germany’s former territories, including Pacific islands and Shandong province in China. - By 1918, Japan was thriving economically and politically, with growing calls for liberal reforms and democracy. - Rise of Militarism and the Economic Downturn - In 1925, Japan granted voting rights to all adult males, signaling a shift towards democracy. - However, military influence was also rising, fueled by: - Corruption in the government, which weakened democratic institutions. - The Wall Street Crash of 1929, which devastated Japan’s economy, particularly silk exports to the U.S. - Widespread rural poverty, leading many peasants to join the Army, giving military leaders greater influence. - The Army, backed by conservative politicians, saw expansionism as the solution to Japan’s economic struggles. - The Manchurian Crisis and Military Takeover - In 1931, Japan invaded Manchuria, a Chinese province with 30 million people and significant Japanese business interests. - The invasion, carried out without consulting the Prime Minister, led to: - The establishment of a puppet government, Manchukuo. - The assassination of Prime Minister Inukai, allowing the Army to seize control of the government. - From this point, Japan transitioned into a military dictatorship, mirroring the authoritarian regimes of Germany and Italy. - Japan’s Aggressive Foreign Policy and World War II - Under military rule, Japan pursued ultranationalist policies, eliminating all opposition: - Dissenters (holding different opinions from official) were labeled ‘communists’ and executed. - Freedom of speech and education were suppressed. - Japan’s imperialist ambitions led to: - The invasion of China in 1937, marked by the Nanjing Massacre, one of the most horrific atrocities in history. - Emperor Hirohito’s passive stance, which, according to historian Richard Storry, allowed the military to act without restraint. - This unchecked militarism ultimately led Japan into World War II, resulting in its near-total destruction. - The Path to Japan’s Post-War Revival - Japan’s self-destructive war policies culminated in its defeat in 1945. - The country’s post-war recovery and transformation into a peaceful industrial power will be explored in a later chapter. - - China’s Transformation: From Feudalism to Revolution - China’s Decline Under Foreign Domination - A century ago, China was a poor and feudal society, despite its vast potential and rich history. - For centuries, it remained unified under the Manchu (Ching) dynasty, but foreign interference led to its decline. - Key events of foreign exploitation: - Opium Wars (1839-42): Britain defeated China and took Hong Kong, opening the country to European and American intervention. - Defeat to Japan (1894-95): China lost Formosa (modern Taiwan), worsening national humiliation. - The Boxer Uprising (1898-1900): A rebellion against foreign influence, but it was crushed by an international army, forcing China to pay a heavy indemnity. - The Fall of the Manchu Dynasty and Political Chaos - The last effective Emperor of the Manchu dynasty died in 1911, leaving the throne to a five-year-old child, Pu Yi. - General Yuan Shihkai ruled in the child Emperor’s name, but China fell into anarchy (a state of disorder), with provincial (regional / state) warlords seizing control. - In 1915, Yuan declared himself King, losing military support and forcing his abdication (act of renouncing the throne). - The Warlord Era (1916-1928) and Fragmentation - After Yuan’s fall, China descended into chaos, with feudal lords and generals dividing the country among themselves. - This period, known as the Warlord Era, resembled modern Afghanistan under the Taliban, where central authority was weak, and tyranny prevailed. - The central government was powerless, failing to restore order. - The Rise of Nationalism and the May Fourth Movement (1919) - In 1919, the Treaty of Versailles handed Japan control of former German territories in Shandong, sparking public outrage. - The May Fourth Movement emerged, led by the Kuomintang (Nationalist Party) under Sun Yat-sen and Chiang Kai-shek, aiming to: - End warlord rule. - Resist foreign domination. - Establish a modern Chinese democracy. - Sun Yat-sen and the Kuomintang’s Vision - - Sun Yat-sen is regarded alongside Mahatma Gandhi (India) and Mustafa Kemal Atatürk (Turkey) as a reformist leader who sought to modernize his nation. - A US-trained doctor, he founded the Kuomintang in Canton province with three goals: - End foreign domination. - Establish democracy. - Implement land reforms to free peasants from feudal rule. - The Chinese Communist Party (CCP), founded in 1921, initially collaborated with the Kuomintang in these efforts. - The Kuomintang-Communist Rift and the Long March - After Sun Yat-sen’s death in 1925, Chiang Kai-shek took over the Kuomintang and turned against the Communists, leading to violent persecution. - This forced Mao Zedong and his Communist followers to embark on the 6,000-mile ‘Long March’, establishing a new power base in northern China. - This division set the stage for the later Communist-Nationalist struggle that would shape modern China. - Chiang Kai-shek’s Leadership and Anti-Communist Actions - - Soviet Support and Kuomintang’s Strengthening - After Sun Yat-sen’s death, Chiang Kai-shek took over the Kuomintang (Nationalist Party) and sought help from the Soviet Union. - The USSR supported Chiang, believing he might embrace communism. They helped build up the Kuomintang army, which Chiang used not only against warlords but also against Chinese communists. - Anti-Communist Purge and Repression (1927) - Chiang was deeply suspicious of the communists’ rising influence in China. - In 1927, he launched a ‘purification’ movement, purging suspected communists from the Kuomintang. - At least 250,000 people were executed under his orders during this period. - Failure to Implement Sun Yat-sen’s Vision - Despite consolidating Kuomintang rule, Chiang failed to realize Sun Yat-sen’s vision of a strong and equitable China. - Instead of land reforms or democracy, he empowered a new class of landlords, worsening social and economic inequality. - Mao - Mao’s Early Life and Rise to Power - Mao, born in 1893 in Hunan province, is regarded as one of the most charismatic leaders in modern history. - He worked as a librarian at Beijing University, a hub for communist activities. - Returning to Hunan, Mao built a strong trade union and peasant movement to fight against warlords. - The Communist Purge and the Long March - In 1930, Mao, Chairman of the Central Executive Committee, hid during the Communist purge. - He built the Red Army, and in 1934, after a crushing defeat by the Kuomintang’s Soviet-backed Army, Mao initiated the Long March. - 100,000 communists embarked on a 6,000-mile journey, crossing 18 mountain ranges, 24 rivers, and passing through 12 provinces. - Only 20,000 communists survived to reach the northern provinces of Shensi and Kansu. - Chiang Kai-shek and the Nationalist Struggle - Under Chiang Kai-shek, China’s situation worsened. He focused on fighting communists instead of the Japanese. - In 1937, when Japan invaded, Chiang’s generals pressured him to focus on Japan. - This led to a temporary alliance with Zhou Enlai, forming a united front against Japan. - Communist Advantage and the Japanese Occupation - The alliance benefited the communists, who retained control over Shensi. - The Kuomintang struggled, while the communists capitalized on their strong base in northern China. - The communists were seen as the true nationalists, and many Kuomintang soldiers defected to their side. - By the end of World War II, Mao had over 100 million followers. - The United States’ Role and the Post-War Situation - The U.S. sent aid to the Kuomintang, but Chiang was unwilling to fight the Japanese. - Mao’s army had strong grassroots support, and people saw him as a successor to Sun Yat-sen. - After Japan’s defeat, the USSR allowed the communists to take over Manchuria. - This led to confusion over who controlled China, with the Allies recognizing the Kuomintang. - The Civil War and the Kuomintang’s Retreat - With U.S. help, the Kuomintang retook eastern cities, but a civil war broke out. - The war continued until 1949, when Mao’s forces forced Chiang and his followers to flee to Hong Kong. - Chiang later established a Kuomintang republic on Formosa (Taiwan). - Turkey - Turkey’s Transformation: From Empire to Republic - Turkey’s shift from an empire-building nation to a modern republic serves as an example to the developing world, especially Islamic states. - After World War I, Turkey, which sided with the Central Powers, faced harsh treatment at Versailles. - Mustafa Kemal Ataturk (1881-1938), a key figure in Turkey’s transformation, is regarded as one of history’s major nation-builders. - Mustafa Kemal’s Leadership - Mustafa Kemal, later honored as ‘Ataturk’ (Father of the Turks), became a national hero. - He led a small, ill-equipped army to defeat the forces of the Sultan and the Western armies enforcing the Treaty of Versailles. - In 1922, his forces took over Istanbul, and in 1923, Turkey was proclaimed a Republic. - The Allies, impressed by the new regime’s popular support, signed the Treaty of Lausanne, allowing Turkey to retain lands reconquered under Mustafa Kemal. - Territorial Changes and Kemal’s Rule - The new Turkey included Anatolia, Armenia, and eastern Thrace but excluded Mesopotamia, Arabia, Palestine, and Syria. - Kemal ruled as a benevolent dictator until his death in 1938, officially as President, though he didn’t allow opposition parties. - He maintained his regime as ‘temporary’ or ‘transitional,’ believing the people weren’t ready for self-government. - Western nations tolerated his undemocratic rule because he kept Turkey out of international conflicts. - Ataturk’s Reforms - Ataturk’s reforms transformed Turkey into a modern state and captured global attention. - He established a national public education system and freed women from Islamic shariat laws. - The Caliphate (the area ruled by Khalifa or Caliph) and its institutions, like religious courts and legal codes, were abolished. - Madrasas were closed, and modern schools were established. Turkish was changed from Arabic to Roman script, with Ataturk personally teaching people how to use it. - Women’s Rights and Social Reforms - Women saw Ataturk as their true liberator. - He banned the veil, encouraged Western clothing, and opened modern schools for girls. - Women were granted the right to vote and entered professions previously reserved for men. - Turkey became the first country to have a female chief of a national supreme court. - Economic Reforms and Agriculture - Ataturk’s economic reforms guided other developing countries. - Despite abundant mineral resources, most of Turkey’s population relied on agriculture. - Ataturk established agricultural colleges, model farms, and banks to support farmers. - He promoted land reform, freed peasants from oppressive landlords, and encouraged smallholdings. - Post-Ataturk Era and Modernization - After Ataturk’s death in 1938, his successors, Ismet Inonu and Celal Bayar, continued modernizing Turkey. - Turkey remained neutral during World War II. - Africa - Africa’s Transformation (1880-1945) - Africa underwent significant changes between 1880 and 1945. - Sub-Saharan Africa, North Africa, and Egypt were connected to Ottoman Empire and European politics. - Central, eastern, and most parts of the continent were under colonial rule. - This era was marked by bloodshed, exploitation, and human suffering. - Colonial Struggles Before 1850 - Before 1850, struggles for colonies occurred mainly in South Africa and Algeria. - In South Africa, Boers (Dutch settlers) clashed with Bantu tribes. - Algeria was invaded by France in 1830, leading to conflict between European settlers and native resistance. - European governments and corporations dreamed of Africa’s untapped resources. - Christian missionaries sought to convert the local population. - European desire for markets and competition for power spurred further colonization. - European Colonial Powers - The British and French led the colonial expansion into Africa. - Britain had taken over most of South Africa from the Dutch and established a protectorate (a state that is controlled and protected by other) in Egypt in 1882. - Britain expanded its holdings into western Africa but preferred indirect involvement. - The French were more involved, with trading posts in Senegal and West Africa. - France captured Algeria in 1847, after 17 years of struggle, and took Tunisia and Ivory Coast as protectorates in the 1880s. - The French annexed Dahomey and established French Equatorial Africa. - - Other Colonial Powers - King Leopold II of Belgium and Bismarck of Germany also laid claims in southern, central, and eastern Africa. - Leopold financed commercial stations in the lower Congo, which became colonies. - Portugal, with ancient claims in Africa, had its territories recognized by Britain. - Bismarck authorized the colonization of South-West Africa, Cameroon, and Togo in 1884. - The Berlin Conference (1884-85) - The Berlin Conference recognized European claims in Africa: - Belgium’s claim to the Congo. - French rights in Equatorial Africa. - Free trade within the continent. - The conference laid out that European claims to African territories must be more substantial than informal dominance. - Bismarck declared a protectorate over the mainland opposite Zanzibar, where German companies had trading rights. - Britain partitioned Zanzibar in 1886, with modern Kenya given to Britain and mainland Tanzania to Germany. - Partition of Africa and Resistance - By the time World War I broke out, European powers had partitioned almost the entire continent, except for Liberia and Ethiopia. - Liberia and Ethiopia defended themselves with modern weapons. - Many large and remote areas remained outside European control. - European colonial rule in Africa is a dark chapter in history, marked by atrocities and racism. - The white minority exercised disproportionate power over native populations. - The legacy of apartheid (a system of discrimination based on race) in South Africa persisted (carried on) until 1990. - The Scramble for Africa - The last two decades of the 19th century saw rapid European partitioning of Africa. - Europeans used military might and technology to impose control. - By the time World War I broke out, small states had been created in Africa. -
