These states evolved to specialize in producing for the world market. - They developed political and social systems to survive as nations by the mid-20th century. - Resistance to Colonial Rule - Despite lacking diplomacy, technology, and economic power, native Africans resisted colonial rule. - In modern Uganda, King Mutesa resisted European takeover in the 1870s. - Ethiopians successfully repelled an Italian invasion. - The Hehe people fought a valiant but unsuccessful war against the Germans, who responded with brutal force. - The British committed genocide in South Africa, killing hundreds of thousands in concentration camps. - Widespread grievances led to local revolts by stateless people and small chiefdoms, though these lacked the organization to challenge European control. - Latin AMERICA - South American Independence and Economic Devastation - By the mid-1820s, Spanish and Portuguese colonies in South America had gained independence. - The wars for independence primarily aimed to end colonial trade monopolies and maintain existing social structures. - However, these wars devastated the continent’s economic infrastructure: - Mines were flooded. - Workforce was depleted due to fighting. - Livestock was lost. - South American countries, which once relied on Spain for markets and credit, came under the influence of the United States and Britain. - Unique Characteristics of Latin America - Latin America’s official languages are primarily European, though many people speak native American languages. - The predominant religion is Roman Catholicism. - Nations in the region have adopted constitutional traditions from Europe and the U.S. - Many elites were educated in Europe and the U.S. - Despite these similarities, Latin America’s economic and political development has been distinct from Europe’s. - Challenges in South American Development - South American countries have faced political instability, characterized by coups (sudden unlawful seizure from government) and declining living standards. - Political leaders believed that developing export economies based on one or two raw materials or semi-finished goods would suffice (enough). - This approach made the region vulnerable to: - Global demand fluctuations. - External political influence from European and American banking and corporate interests. - Comparison with Asia and Africa - In the early 20th century, South America shared more in common with Asia and Africa than with Europe. - In all three regions, countries specialized in specific products as the global economy integrated. - Rubber in Asia, cocoa and coffee in South America, and cocoa in Africa, along with mining industries, came under European control. - Focus on Three Major South American Countries - With this broad overview in mind, we now focus on the specific experiences of three of South America’s largest countries in the pre-1945 period. - Argentina - Independence and Early Struggles (1816-1853) - Independence: Argentina won its independence from Spain in 1816. - Internal Struggles: In the early years, Argentina faced a struggle between centrifugal and regional forces, with debates over which region would dominate the country’s polity and economy. - Bernardino Rivadavia (1821-1827): Tried to create a liberal political state but was bogged down by conflicts between Buenos Aires and the provinces. - Juan Manuel de Rosas (1827-1852): After Rivadavia’s resignation, Rosas established a period of dictatorship. His style of governance, while centralizing foreign policy and customs in Buenos Aires, caused resentment (bitterness) among the provinces. - Federal Constitution (1852): Rosas was overthrown by Justo José de Urquiza, who established a Federal Constitution for Argentina. Buenos Aires became a separate federal province in 1880. - Economic Expansion and Immigration (1853-1916) - Economic Growth: The period saw significant economic progress, with Argentina’s economy based largely on agriculture, particularly wool, hides, tallow, and beef. - Refrigeration and Export: In 1876, refrigeration facilities in cargo ships enabled the export of beef to Europe. - Foreign Investment: Britain invested in railways to connect Argentina’s interior to coastal ports, facilitating the export of wheat. - Immigration and Industrialization: Large-scale European immigration transformed Argentina into a highly urbanized, industrialized country with a diverse population. It became one of South America’s wealthiest nations, rivaling the United States. - Democratic Suppression: Despite economic success, democracy was suppressed during this period. - Political Developments and Struggles for Democracy (1916-1930) - Rise of the Radical Party (1890s): The Radical Party emerged as a political expression of Argentina’s middle class, which had little influence in governance. - Saenz Peña Law (1912): The conservative government expanded the franchise and introduced the secret ballot, helping the Radical Party’s cause. - Hipólito Yrigoyen (1916): The leader of the Radical Party was elected president in 1916. However, his administration exhibited authoritarian tendencies and failed to deliver land and labor reforms, favoring the rich landlords instead. - Decline of the Radical Party (1920s): The Radical Party degenerated into a corrupt and directionless entity by the 1920s. - Military Coup (1930): In 1930, the military staged a coup against Yrigoyen, who had returned to power in 1928. Argentina was suffering from the effects of the Great Depression. - Nacionalismo and World War II Impact (1930-1943) - US-British Rivalry: The United States began competing with Britain for influence in Argentina, giving rise to the right-wing Nacionalismo movement. This movement opposed both the US and Britain while advocating for social reforms. - World War II: The war was disastrous for Argentina, as its European markets were closed. The country’s political institutions were ill-equipped to handle the crisis. - Military Takeover (1943): In 1943, the military once again seized control of the government. Most military officers were first or second-generation immigrants, supportive of Nacionalismo. - Peronism and Political Stability (1943-1956) - Juan Perón and Peronism: Led by Colonel Juan Perón, the military’s new leadership combined social discontent and authoritarian politics into a movement known as Peronism. The movement, though dictatorial, was socially progressive and anti-communist. - Trade Union Support: Perón gained support from trade unions and even attracted communist backing, consolidating power. - Eva Perón: Perón’s wife, Eva Duarte, was a charismatic figure and played a key role in securing public support for her husband’s movement. - Political Stability: Peronism brought much-needed political stability to Argentina. - Perón’s Ouster (1956): Perón was ousted in 1956, leading to prolonged instability, poor governance, and economic decline in the following years. - Brazil - Early History and Independence: - Pedro Alvares Cabral, a Portuguese seafarer, was the first European to reach Brazil in 1500. - The country was sparsely populated by Amerindian tribes, most of whom were exterminated. - The Portuguese brought in hundreds of thousands of African slaves; slavery lasted until 1888. - In 1808, the King of Portugal moved the seat of government to Brazil, and Don Joao VI ruled. - After Don Joao VI’s return to Portugal, his son, Pedro, proclaimed Brazil’s independence in 1822 and became its first Emperor. - In 1889, the monarchy was deposed, and a republic was established. A military junta took control in 1930, and Getulio Vargas assumed dictatorial powers until 1945. - Slavery and Economic Shift: - Brazil’s economy relied heavily on sugar plantations, which depended on slavery. - By the 1850s, coffee cultivation began spreading in southern Brazil. - Coffee producers made significant profits and supported labor reforms. - The Brazilian government stopped importing slaves by 1850, under British pressure. - In 1871, the Rio Branco Law was passed, freeing slaves owned by the crown. - Slavery was finally abolished in 1888 through a law passed by Christina, the Regent. - This law caused resentment (bitterness) among the Army, planters, and the Church, leading to a coup and the fall of the Empire. - Economic Dominance of Coffee: - By the early 20th century, Brazil produced three-fourths of the world’s coffee. - Surplus production led to government loans from foreign banks to maintain coffee prices. - Brazil’s neighboring countries became dependent on this coffee economy. - To repay the loans, the Brazilian government raised taxes from other sectors, harming other industries. - The Great Depression in 1929 caused coffee prices to crash, leading to an economic collapse. - In October 1930, a military coup ended the Republican regime. - Getulio Vargas and Economic Transformation: - After the coup, Vargas reduced Brazil’s dependence on coffee. - He set up new industries to diversify Brazil’s economy. - By the end of the 1930s, Brazil’s manufacturing base was firmly established. - Vargas introduced a new Constitution that included labor welfare laws and a planned development program. - By 1945, Brazil had become an industrial power in South America. - South American States - Mexico - Independence and Early Struggles: - After over a decade of rebellion, Mexico gained independence from Spain and became a republic in 1823. - For the first 90 years, Mexico was ruled by conservative presidents who failed to address social and economic issues. - Antonio Lopez de Santa Anna, a dictatorial leader, was the most prominent among them. - A liberal political movement, La Reforma, emerged in the mid-19th century, seeking democratic governance and foreign investment. - In 1855, Santa Anna was exiled, and liberals passed reforms to break up large estates and establish small farms. - The reforms led to a civil war, and Benito Juarez emerged victorious in 1861. - The Empire of Maximilian: - Following Juarez’s victory, conservatives sought to install an Emperor to protect their interests and invited Austrian Archduke Maximilian to take the throne. - Supported by French troops under Napoleon III, Maximilian was installed as emperor in 1864. - Juarez organized resistance, and in 1867, Maximilian was captured and executed. - Porfirio Diaz and the Revolution: - In 1876, Porfirio Diaz led a revolution to install a “true republic.” - Diaz became president and ruled with weak conservative prime ministers until 1911. - He allowed landowners to buy public land cheaply, favored the Army, and permitted the Church to operate freely. - Mexico’s rich became richer, while the majority remained impoverished. - Labour unrest and strikes erupted, especially in the textile and mining industries, leading Diaz to use brutal repression. - By 1900, foreign (especially American) investments flowed into the country. - Madero’s Insurrection and Radical Reforms: - Diaz rigged (conduct something fraudently to gain advantage) the 1910 elections, where Francisco Madero emerged as his opponent. - Despite being defeated, Madero launched an insurrection against Diaz, forcing him to flee in May 1911. - Madero became president but failed to address the needs of the poor. - Radical leaders such as Pancho Villa, Venustiano Carranza, and Emiliano Zapata emerged, promising land reforms. - In 1911, Zapata’s program included large-scale land confiscations, dividing Mexican society. - Madero was overthrown by General Victoriano Huerta in 1913, but Huerta’s government collapsed in 1914. - Carranza, with American backing, emerged as a leader. U.S. forces occupied Veracruz to support Carranza’s cause. - Carranza used nationalism to win public support and positioned himself as a national hero. - Carranza’s Leadership and Constitution: - Carranza promised reforms to workers and peasants but aligned with landowners and industrialists. - Growing public discontent led to another civil war between Carranza’s army and Zapata’s forces. - In 1917, Carranza presented a new Constitution that launched a social revolution and political reforms. - Post-Revolutionary Mexico: - After 1917, Mexico entered a period of consolidation (act of making something stronger). - During the 1920s, military leaders from Carranza’s faction served as presidents. - These leaders followed the example of California but did not implement land reforms. - In 1929, Plutarco Elias Calles founded the Institutional Revolutionary Party (PRD), which transformed Mexico’s political system into a single-party system. - While criticized for limiting democracy, the PRD provided Mexico with the longest period of political stability in South America. - The era of revolutionary politics ended in 1940 with the election of Manuel Avila Camacho, and Mexico began resolving major issues democratically. - World war II 1939-1945 - The Stages of War - Prelude to World War II: - The war began on 3 September 1939, when England and France declared war on Germany, two days after Hitler’s blitzkrieg (intense military campaign to bring swift victory) attack on Poland. - Over the next six years, the conflict spread across Europe, the Sahara Desert, West Asia, South East Asia, India, Pacific Islands, and the Arctic. - Rapid advancements in aviation technology brought the war to the United States, while German U-boats clashed with the Allied Navy off Uruguay. - The war ended with the devastating use of atomic bombs on Japan, exposing the horrors of atomic power. - However, the birth of the United Nations, built on the ruins of the League of Nations, symbolized a renewed global commitment to avoid future warfare. - German Invasion of Poland and the Outbreak of War: - Germany’s invasion of Poland marked the first use of blitzkrieg ((intense military campaign to bring swift victory)), with German warplanes supporting the Army and the Soviet Army attacking from the east. - By the end of September 1939, Poland was divided between Germany and the USSR. - After a five-month pause (the ‘Phoney War’), fighting resumed in April 1940, with Germany invading Norway and Denmark. - In May, Germany invaded Holland and Belgium, followed by France’s surrender in six weeks. - Italy joined Germany’s side, and Hitler considered ‘Operation Sea Lion’ to invade Britain. - Realizing that air superiority was needed, the Luftwaffe launched air strikes against the Royal Air Force (RAF). - The RAF, bolstered (padded support) by Winston Churchill’s leadership, repelled these attacks in the Battle of Britain (July-September 1940). - Despite extensive bombings, the British resolve remained unbroken, and the invasion of Britain was abandoned. - - North African Campaign: - The war shifted to North Africa when Mussolini’s Italy invaded Greece and Egypt. - German forces landed in support of the Italians. - Rebel armies, known as Partisans and supported by Britain, harassed the German occupation forces in Europe. - While the United States remained neutral, it provided arms and supplies to Britain. - Meanwhile, tensions between the United States and Japan escalated as both sought dominance in the Pacific. - German Invasion of the USSR: - On 21 June 1941, Germany launched an unprovoked invasion of the USSR. - The Germans initially achieved significant successes but underestimated the vastness of Russia and the challenge of supplying their advancing troops. - Hitler’s overconfidence led to disastrous consequences, as German troops were unprepared for the harsh Russian winter. - Japan’s Expansion and U.S. Entry into the War: - On 7 December 1941, Japan’s surprise attack on Pearl Harbor led to the United States’ entry into World War II, marking a turning point in the conflict. - Japan initially scored notable victories, forcing British forces to surrender Singapore, Malaysia, and Burma. - The fall of Australia and India seemed imminent, and the U.S. was driven out of the Philippines, Guam, and Wake Island. - Under General Douglas MacArthur, the Allies launched a prolonged counter-offensive using land, sea, and air warfare. - Aircraft carriers became central to the Pacific War, symbolizing the power of naval warfare in this theater. - The German Campaign in Russia - Initial Advance and Stalingrad: - In 1941, the German armies, despite being slowed by harsh winter conditions, reached Stalingrad (Volgograd) by August 1942. - The Germans aimed to capture Stalingrad and use it as a springboard to attack Moscow from the southeast. - However, the Red Army, supported by local populations, mounted a heroic defense. - The Winter of 1942 and German Defeat at Stalingrad: - By the end of 1942, the Russian winter helped turn the tide, causing the Germans to lose their supply line. - With no option to retreat, German General von Paulus surrendered in February 1943, along with 100,000 soldiers. - This defeat shattered the myth of the invincibility of Hitler’s Army. - Defensive Successes in the North and Center: - In the north, the German Army was halted at Leningrad (St. Petersburg). - In the center, the Red Army revived at the gates of Moscow, bolstered (provided by padded support) by Russian industries that had been relocated deep within the country by Stalin. - These industries continued to produce essential material for the counter-offensive. - Allied Support and the Red Army’s Counterattack: - The British and Americans provided support through Iran in the east. - In December 1942, the Red Army launched a counter-attack on all fronts, pushing the Germans back to Berlin by April 1945, through Ukraine and Poland. - Mussolini’s Involvement and the North African Front - Mussolini’s Ambitions and Initial Setbacks: - Mussolini, who had long coveted (dezired) Greece and Egypt for Italy, opened a new front in the war by attacking Egypt and Greece in September 1940. - However, the British quickly drove the inexperienced Italian forces out of Egypt, and the Greeks also repelled the Italian armies. - German Intervention and the Afrika Korps: - Seeking help, Mussolini turned to Hitler, who responded by sending the German ‘Afrika Korps’ to Tripoli, the capital of the Italian colony of Libya, in February 1941. - A joint German-Italian counter-offensive pushed the British out of the region. - German Success and the Battle of El Alamein: - Under the leadership of General Erwin Rommel, known as the ‘Desert Fox’ for his strategic brilliance, the German forces overwhelmed (have a strong emotional effect on) the British in Egypt. By July 1942, they were within 80 miles of Alexandria. - However, the British, Indian, Australian, and New Zealand armies, led by Generals Montgomery and Alexander, launched a successful counter-attack. - On 23rd October 1942, the Battle of El Alamein resulted in a decisive British victory, shattering Rommel’s forces. - The Germans were forced to retreat westward, and their hopes of advancing into India through West Asia were crushed. - The Allied Advance in Italy (Surrender of Italy) - The Allied Forces’ Push into Europe: - The British and Indian forces, later joined by the Americans and the French from Tunisia, gradually pushed the Germans and Italians back into Europe. - The Invasion of Southern Italy and Mussolini’s Fall: - In July 1943, the Allied forces landed in southern Italy, leading to Mussolini’s dismissal by the King. - Italy surrendered unconditionally, and Mussolini was imprisoned on an island. However, Hitler’s commandos eventually rescued him. - The Battle for Cassino: - The Germans fiercely defended the mountainous area southeast of Rome, particularly around the heights of Cassino. - Indian soldiers showed exceptional bravery in the battle for Cassino, which ultimately fell to the Allies in June 1944. - The Battle for the Atlantic - German U-boats: - German U-boats caused significant damage to Allied shipping. These submarines would surface suddenly and attack large ships. - The Allies knew that without establishing superiority in the Atlantic, victory in Europe would remain out of reach. - Challenges with Neutral Countries: - The Republic of Ireland remained firmly neutral, limiting the Allies’ ability to launch air strikes on German ships from nearby bases. - Turning the Tide: - The tide of the sea-war turned when Portugal allowed the Allies to use the Azores islands in the mid-Atlantic, providing a critical air base for launching strikes against German shipping. - Allied Bombing Campaign and the Push from the West - 1943 Bombing Campaign: - Throughout 1943, British and American planes relentlessly bombed Germany. - Despite the heavy bombing, German morale remained intact, and production in its military industrial complex was largely unaffected. - Russian Advances: - As the Soviet forces advanced towards Germany from the east, defeating German armies, the Allies began planning an invasion from the west. - Operation Overlord and D-Day - Operation Overlord: - Under the leadership of American General Dwight D. Eisenhower, Operation Overlord was launched. - D-Day (6 June 1944): - The combined forces of Britain, America, and Canada landed on the northern coast of France. - This event, known as D-Day, marked the beginning of the liberation of Western Europe. - After establishing a toehold, the Allies pushed the Germans back, and Paris was liberated by August. - Belgium was also freed shortly after. - Arnhem and the German Counter-offensive - Arnhem and the Defeat: - A second “D-Day” type operation was launched in Arnhem, aimed at liberating Holland. - However, adverse weather conditions led to the Allies’ defeat in this operation. - German Counter-offensive: - This defeat emboldened (give courage) the Germans, who launched a major counter-offensive in December 1944. - For two weeks, it appeared the Allies might be routed. However, the Germans struggled to sustain the momentum of their offensive, unable to secure enough fuel for their tanks and vehicles. - The Final Push - Restoration of Parity (state of being equal): - By February 1945, the Allies restored parity. - Crossing the Rhine: - In March, the Allies crossed the Rhine River and entered Germany. - They destroyed the remaining German forces, leading to the eventual collapse of Nazi Germany. - End of the Third Reich and the Fall of Nazi Germany - Nazi Dream of a Thousand-Year Reich: - The Nazis had envisioned their ‘Third Reich’ lasting a thousand years, but it ended in just 12 years. - The Red Army’s Advance: - The Red Army crossed the Oder River and fought its way into Berlin. - Despite fierce resistance from the German armies, there was no hope left for victory. - Mussolini’s Fate: - Mussolini, who had been freed by Nazi paratroopers earlier, was captured by anti-Fascist partisans while attempting to escape to neutral Switzerland. - He was shot dead. - Hitler’s Suicide: - Adolf Hitler, wishing to avoid Mussolini’s fate, committed suicide on 29 April 1945 as Soviet troops entered the Reichstag (Parliament). - Germany’s Surrender and Post-War Division - German Surrender: - On 7 April 1945, Germany’s armed forces surrendered, marking the end of World War II in Europe. - Yalta and Potsdam Agreements: - According to agreements made at the Yalta Conference in February 1945 and later confirmed at the Potsdam Conference in July 1945, Germany was stripped of all wartime gains. - The country was divided into four zones of occupation: British, American, Soviet, and French. - Berlin, located within the Soviet zone, was also split into four sectors. - Nazi Trials: - A large number of Nazi leaders were captured and put on trial for their war crimes. - - The Final Phase of the War in the Pacific (Japanese Surrender) - Allied Advances: - In June 1945, British and Indian forces successfully regained Burma after a series of fierce battles. - Earlier, in January 1945, American forces had driven the Japanese out of the Philippines and made significant landings on islands near Japan, such as the Marianas and Okinawa. - The Dilemma of the Allies: - Following the death of President Roosevelt in April 1945, President Harry S. Truman took office. - Truman had overseen the successful test of the first atom bomb and faced a tough decision on how to bring the war with Japan to a swift end. - His generals advised that Japan would be more difficult to conquer than Germany, especially since Japan was still holding on to territories like Malaysia, Singapore, Korea, Manchuria, and Formosa (Taiwan). - A prolonged war would be required to ensure Japan’s complete defeat. - Use of the Atom Bomb: - To avoid a drawn-out conflict, Truman decided to use the atom bomb. - On 6 August 1945, the city of Hiroshima was destroyed by the first atomic bomb. - Three days later, a second bomb was dropped on Nagasaki, causing devastating destruction. - - The Aftermath: - The bombings killed hundreds of thousands of people, with many others maimed (injured) for life due to radiation exposure. - Future generations were also affected by the long-term effects of radiation. - With no other option, Japan surrendered on 14 August 1945, bringing an end to World War II. - Causes of the US Entry into World War II - Isolationist Policy: - During the inter-war period, the United States maintained a policy of isolation. - For the first 26 months of World War II, President Roosevelt kept the country out of the war, while supporting Britain with supplies of armaments and food. This assistance was formalized through the Lend-Lease Agreement signed in April 1941. - Under this agreement, the US provided warplanes, ships, and other defense equipment to Britain, with the understanding that Britain would pay for them after the war. - Churchill’s Push for American Involvement: - Winston Churchill, the British Prime Minister, urged the US to enter the war. Germany’s defeat required an ally with immense resources and the capacity to endure prolonged warfare. - America, with its vast resources, could hold the key to success, but President Roosevelt rejected Churchill’s repeated overtures for US involvement. - Japan’s Expansion in Asia: - Japan, initially neutral in the war, sought to exploit (derive benefit from) the setbacks suffered by Dutch, French, and British colonies in Asia by capturing their colonial bases. - Japan was already engaged in a war with China, and the US supported China in this conflict, resulting in a stalemate between Japan and the Kuomintang (Chinese nationalists) and the Communists. - Japanese Actions in Indochina: - After France’s fall, the Germans installed a puppet government in Vichy France. Japan used its diplomatic influence over the Vichy Government to acquire French Indochina. - Japan established military bases in Indochina, particularly in Vietnam, which bordered China. The US became alarmed at the strategic depth Japan was gaining and feared the possibility of Japan opening a new front against China. - The US demanded Japan’s withdrawal from Indochina, and to enforce this demand, the US Navy imposed a naval blockade in July 1941, preventing oil shipments to Japan. - US-Japan Negotiations: - Japan was forced to enter negotiations with the US. During talks, the US demanded that Japan withdraw from both Indochina and China. - On 16 October 1941, General Tojo became the new Prime Minister of Japan and adopted an aggressive foreign policy. He began preparing for an attack on the US. - Attack on Pearl Harbor: - On 7 December 1941, without a formal declaration of war, a large Japanese armada attacked the US naval base at Pearl Harbor. - 335 Japanese planes launched from aircraft carriers bombed the base for over two hours, resulting in significant damage: - 350 planes were destroyed, five battleships were sunk, and around 3,700 Americans were killed. - US Declaration of War: - Two days later, the US Senate voted in favor of declaring war on Japan. - The US entry into the war turned the tide in favor of the Allies. - Adolf Hitler, despite the advice of his close advisors, declared war on the United States. - Had he not done so, the US-Japanese conflict might have remained confined to the Asia-Pacific region. - Causes of World War II - The Versailles Treaty: - The treaty imposed harsh terms on Germany, taking away its territories and colonies, and forcing it to pay large reparations. - This created economic hardship and led to the rise of fascism and resentment (bitterness), fueling the idea of “betrayal” and the need for revenge. - Failure of the League of Nations: - The League of Nations failed to enforce collective security and did not push colonial powers to grant independence. - This created competition among European powers and contributed to the rise of fascism. - Economic Factors: - The Great Depression of 1929 worsened conditions, fostering the rise of fascist parties in Britain and France. - Hitler used the economic crisis to rearm Germany and remilitarize the Rhineland in violation of the Versailles Treaty. - Hitler’s Expansionist Ideology: - Hitler aimed to create Lebensraum (living space) by conquering Austria, Czechoslovakia, and parts of Poland and Eastern Europe. - His expansionist policies led to aggression that other powers could not tolerate. - Chamberlain’s Policy of Appeasement: - Britain and France failed to take a tough stand at the Munich talks (1938), allowing Hitler to expand unchecked. - This reinforced Hitler’s belief that the Western powers would tolerate his actions, especially against Poland. - Hitler’s Misjudgment: - A.J.P. Taylor argued that Hitler did not initially want a world war, but misjudged the situation and hoped for Britain’s non-interference in Poland. - Hitler thought the Western powers would accept his claims for the Danzig corridor, but he miscalculated their response. - Joseph Stalin’s Role: - Stalin signed the Non-Aggression Pact with Germany, giving Hitler the confidence to invade Poland. - The pact had secret clauses allowing Stalin to annex Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania, which betrayed Poland and the entire continent. - World War II: Devastation and Aftermath - Loss of Life and Destruction: - Around 40 million people were killed, including both soldiers and civilians. - Cities, villages, industries, and communication systems were destroyed. - Over 21 million people became homeless, with the USSR being the hardest hit, suffering more than half of the casualties. - Germany and Japan also faced significant destruction, and Poland lost nearly an entire generation. - Peace Treaties: - Unlike World War I, there was no comprehensive peace treaty due to growing distrust between the Allies and the USSR. - Several separate treaties were signed, including: - Italy had to give up its African colonies and claims to Albania and Ethiopia. - Trieste was declared a free territory under the United Nations. - The Treaty of San Francisco forced Japan to renounce all its overseas territories. - Tension Between the USSR and Allies: - Stalin sought to maintain Soviet control over Eastern Europe, which had been liberated by the Red Army. - Territories like parts of Czechoslovakia, Finland, Ukraine, Latvia, Lithuania, and Estonia were annexed by the USSR. - Puppet communist governments were established in countries such as Czechoslovakia, Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, and Poland. - Yugoslavia, led by Marshal Tito, became a communist country but remained independent from Moscow. - Germany was divided into East Germany (under communist influence) and West Germany (democratic), escalating tensions. - Social Upheaval: - Large-scale population movements occurred, with 10 million Germans fleeing to West Germany. - The Jewish population in Eastern Europe, Holland, Greece, and Germany was decimated, with nearly 6 million Jews killed in Nazi extermination camps. - In countries like Ukraine and the Baltic states, Jews were shot and buried in mass graves. - The Soviets, under Stalin, also committed atrocities like the Katyn Massacre, where over 4,000 Polish Army officers were executed. - After the war, most of Europe’s Jewish population migrated to America or Israel. - Decline of European Powers: - Britain failed to achieve its pre-war objectives, including maintaining Naval supremacy and strategic interests in Europe. - In July 1946, Churchill’s coalition government was defeated, and a Labour government led by Clement Attlee took over. - Former European powers were in decline, and decolonization began under American pressure, leading to independence for many colonies in Asia and Africa. - Rise of Superpowers: - After the war, two superpowers emerged: the USA and the USSR. - The two countries competed for global influence, leading to the Cold War. - The Iron Curtain, a term coined by Winston Churchill in 1946, marked the ideological and physical divide between the spheres of influence of the USA and the USSR. - The Cold War was characterized by an arms race and the constant threat of nuclear war, lasting until the USSR’s collapse in 1991. - The Birth of the United Nations - Formation of the United Nations: - During World War II, the need for a more effective global organization was recognized, to address the failures of the League of Nations. - In 1944, leaders from the USSR, America, China, and Britain met at Dumbarton Oaks in the United States to discuss forming a new organization: the United Nations (UN). - The UN Charter was drafted in San Francisco in 1945. The aims outlined in the Charter are: - Preserve peace and eliminate war. - Remove the causes of conflict through economic, social, educational, scientific, and cultural progress, particularly in underdeveloped nations. - Safeguard human rights for all individuals, and the rights of people and nations. - It officially opposed colonialism and imperialism. - Gave newly emerging nations an international platform to raise their voices. - The UN pressured colonial powers like Britain, France, Portugal, Belgium to decolonize peacefully. - UN’s Role and Achievements: - Over the next 60 years, the UN gained global support but was unable to resolve all international conflicts. - Wars continue to break out with or without the UN’s sanction, as seen recently in Iraq. - However, the UN has prevented numerous wars by setting up dispute settlement bodies, organizing ceasefires, and facilitating negotiations, mediations, and peacekeeping missions. - The UN also plays a significant role in humanitarian and development work, addressing issues like health, education, sanitation, water, and the environment, especially in poor countries. - Comparison with the League of Nations: - The UN has been more successful than its predecessor, the League of Nations. - The League failed to address the needs of the vast majority of the world’s population living under colonial rule. - Unlike the League, which was primarily dominated by Anglo-French interests, the UN focuses on global peace and social-economic development in underdeveloped countries. - The UN spends significant resources on economic and social development through specialized agencies such as UNICEF, WHO, and FAO. - It is also committed to human rights, something the League largely ignored. - Challenges and Areas for Improvement: - Despite its successes, the UN has several contradictions and inconsistencies. - One major issue is the Security Council, which is still dominated by the original UN founders. India, the world’s largest democracy, has yet to secure a seat on the Council. - The UN now has 189 members, and there is a growing consensus that these anomalies should be addressed. - In the post-Cold War era, US actions have undermined the authority of the UN, an issue that needs to be rectified (corrected). - Decolonisation and the birth of new natinos in the post-war world - The Impact of World War II - World War II was undoubtedly the most significant upheaval in human history, shaking mankind to its core. - As explored in a previous chapter, the war caused massive devastation in terms of human life and economic destruction. - It also brought about tectonic shifts in the trajectory of nations and civilizations. - One of the most profound impacts was the end of the colonial order, which had caused centuries of human misery and conflict. - Decolonization - The Impact of World War II on European Empires - Before World War II - The European empires were characterized by a wide gap between the rulers and the ruled. - Most colonial populations lived in extreme poverty, with little hope of challenging the entrenched power structures. - The awe of European power, sustained by military force and foreign-origin laws, prevented many colonized peoples from imagining any significant upheaval. - World War II’s Role in the Dissolution of Empires - World War II accelerated the dissolution of these empires, leading to rapid changes after 1945. - European Empires in 1945 - Britain’s Empire - The largest empire, consisting of: - The Indian subcontinent, Burma (Myanmar), Malaya (Malaysia), and Singapore - Vast regions in Africa, Cyprus, and Hong Kong - Islands in the Caribbean (West Indies), Pacific (Fiji), and territories like the Falklands and Gibraltar - France’s Empire - The second largest empire, comprising: - Large territories in Africa, Indo-China, and the Caribbean - Other European Powers - Holland: Indonesia and Dutch East Indies - Belgium: Congo and Rwanda - Portugal: Angola, Mozambique, Guinea, and Goa - Spain: Morocco, Spanish Guinea, Ifni, and Spanish Sahara - Italy: Libya, Somalia, and Eritrea - The Aftermath of World War II - The Shake-up - The end of World War II led to a rapid shake-up of the colonial system, continuing for three decades. - Transfer of Power - By 1975, most former colonies had gained independence, with new nations emerging from the old empires. - Reluctance to Relinquish (renounce / give up) Control - While some colonial powers, like Britain and France, relinquished control, others like the Dutch and French were more reluctant to do so, as seen in Indonesia and Indo-China. - Causes of the End of the Colonial Empires - Impact of World War II - World War II shattered the imperial order, particularly in colonial territories. - Nationalist movements gained new momentum due to the war, with oppressed peoples receiving fresh inspiration. - The defeat of European powers by Japan in regions like Malaya, Hong Kong, the Dutch East Indies, and Indo-China shattered the myth of European invincibility. - After the war, local people in Indo-China and Indonesia continued their resistance instead of meekly (quite, subtle, gentle and submissive manner) accepting their former colonial rulers back. - Effect on Soldiers and Loyalty - Many colonies provided recruits for the Allied forces during the war. - These soldiers returned home after the war with a resolve to address their own countries’ issues, challenging their European rulers’ authority and loyalty. - Weakening of European Powers - Loss of Resources and Energy - The European powers were severely weakened by the war, with many of their best soldiers lost in battle. - A sense of fatigue (extreme tiredness) replaced the earlier vigor (physical strength and good health) with which they had defended their empires. - Dependency on the United States - European powers found themselves increasingly under the shadow of the United States, a country that opposed colonialism. - During the war, President Roosevelt pressured Allied powers to accept the Atlantic Charter (1941), which outlined two key principles: - Nations should not expand by taking territory from others. - All people should have the right to choose their own form of government. - Roosevelt’s Influence and British Resistance - Though Churchill attempted to limit the Charter’s application to Nazi Germany, Roosevelt made it clear that its principles applied globally. - President Harry S. Truman and Clement Atlee pressured Britain to relinquish control over India, although Britain delayed independence for other colonies. - United States’ Pressure for Decolonization - Fear of Communism - The United States feared that communism, supported by Moscow, could inspire colonial liberation struggles. - Economic Interests - The U.S. was also motivated by commercial interests, seeking new markets in the newly independent countries. - United Nations’ Role - The United Nations, which was heavily influenced by the U.S., periodically issued statements against colonialism. - National Struggles and Independence Movements - India’s Independence - The history of India’s independence serves as an example of how nationalist movements gained energy and ultimately led to the partition of India, creating two nations. - India’s experience mirrored the struggles faced by other colonies seeking independence after World War II. - West Indies, Malaya and Cyprus - - British West Indies - Overview of the British West Indies - The British West Indies comprised various islands in the Caribbean Sea, including large islands like Jamaica and Trinidad, as well as smaller islands like Grenada, St. Vincent, Barbados, Seychelles, Bahamas, and Antigua. - The British also controlled Honduras in Central America and Guyana in South America, with a total population of roughly 6 million. - There were doubts about the viability of small islands like Grenada, St. Vincent, and Antigua, each with populations under 100,000. - Attempts at Federation (a group of states with cetral government but independent in internal affairs / confederation) - Initially, the British considered a federation of these islands but faced resistance. - Honduras and Guyana wanted to retain separate identities, while Jamaica and Trinidad were unwilling to join. - Despite this, the West Indies Federation was established in 1958. - The federation faced resentment (bitterness) as the islands had few commonalities. - Jamaica and Trinidad withdrew in 1961, and the British granted independence to each island separately. - Jamaica and Trinidad-Tobago gained independence first, followed by Guyana in 1966. - Other islands gained independence gradually, with the Bahamas (renamed Belize) in 1981, and St. Kitts and Nevis in 1983. - Despite rejecting a federation, the islands soon realized the benefits of regional cooperation. - In 1968, they formed the Caribbean Free Trade Association, and in 1973, they established the Caribbean Community and Common Market (CARICOM), excluding Guyana and Belize. - Malaya’s Struggle for Independence - Ethnic Tensions and Federation - After Japanese occupation ended in 1945, Malaya faced ethnic rivalry between the Malay population, Chinese, Indians, and Europeans. - As a step toward independence, the British encouraged the creation of the Federation of Malaya in 1948, uniting nine states, each ruled by a Sultan, along with the territories of Malacca and Penang. - Each state had its own legislature for local affairs, while a central government maintained overall control. - Singapore was retained as a colony at this stage. - Communist Insurgency and Independence - Communist insurgents, led by Chin Pen, launched a guerrilla war against British rule. - The British declared a state of emergency in 1948, which lasted until 1960. - Under Tunku Abdul Rahman, the Malaya population gained political ascendancy (domination) and mobilized support from non-communist Chinese and Indian communities. - In 1957, Britain transferred power, and in 1963, the Federation of Malaysia was established. - Brunei’s Sultan did not join and remained independent, while Singapore initially joined the Federation but became independent in 1965. - The Malaysian federation remains intact today. - Cyprus’ Struggle for Independence - Ethnic and Religious Conflict - Cyprus had a population of 80% Greek Orthodox Christians and 20% Muslims of Turkish origin. - The Greek Cypriots wanted to unite with Greece, which was opposed by the Turkish Cypriots. - The British Labour government initially attempted to grant Cyprus independence but faced resistance. - Winston Churchill’s return to power in 1951 slowed the independence process, causing strong anti-British sentiment, particularly among the Greeks. - Struggle for Freedom - A guerilla movement led by Archbishop Makarios and General Grivas (Eoka) waged constant warfare against British troops. - The British were reluctant to grant independence, instead wanting to retain Cyprus as a military base. - Over 35,000 British troops were stationed in the country. - The Turkish Cypriots launched a campaign to divide the country along religious lines. - Independence and Aftermath - In 1960, British Prime Minister Harold Macmillan granted full independence to Cyprus to avoid civil war. - Makarios dropped plans for union with Greece and became the country’s first president. - However, peace lasted only three years, and civil war erupted again in 1974. - Turkey intervened, establishing a separate state in the north. - Cyprus remains divided to this day, with Greek Cypriots controlling the south, and United Nations peacekeepers maintaining peace between the two sides. - Hong Kong’s Accession (succession) to China - Early History of Hong Kong - Before 1842, Hong Kong was a tiny island inhabited by fishermen, with little economic importance. - The strategic location of the island was first recognized by the British, who needed a naval base in the eastern Indian Ocean. - British Acquisition (purchase / accession) of Hong Kong - At the end of the First Opium War in 1842, China ceded Hong Kong to Britain permanently. - In 1860, China ceded Kowloon, the mainland area opposite Hong Kong, to Britain under the Peking Convention. - Conversion to Leasehold - Following the Second Opium War, in 1898, a treaty resulted in the British converting their ownership of Hong Kong into a 99-year lease, with the inclusion of more strategic mainland territory. - Immigration and Growth of Hong Kong - Starting in 1900, Hong Kong experienced a steady influx of immigrants fleeing the civil war in mainland China. - Over the next 90 years, Hong Kong’s service-oriented economy attracted people from around the world, with a significant wave of immigration after mainland China became communist in 1949. - Hong Kong’s rule under Britain was not democratic, with the island’s Governor being nominated by the British Prime Minister. - Concerns Over the Lease (rental agreement) Expiry - In the 1980s, Britain grew concerned about the approaching end of its leasehold over Hong Kong. - By this time, living standards in Hong Kong were among the highest in Asia. - There was fear that the spirit of free enterprise that made Hong Kong prosperous would be stifled (suffocate / choke) under communist rule. - Many of the island’s Chinese-origin citizens began emigrating to Britain, Australia, Canada, and other countries. - Negotiations for Sovereignty (supreme power or authority) Transfer - Britain formally requested to renew the leasehold, but Deng Xiaoping, China’s communist chairman, refused and demanded the full return of Hong Kong to China. - To avoid confrontation, Britain agreed, and in 1984, Prime Minister Margaret Thatcher and Chinese counterpart Zhao Ziyang signed a Joint Declaration to transfer sovereignty of Hong Kong to China on 30 June 1997. - China’s Commitments - In return for the sovereignty (supreme power or authority) transfer, China agreed to grant Hong Kong and its people greater freedom and autonomy, allowing the retention of its capitalist system up to 2047. - Challenges Before Transfer - The road to the transfer was marked by bickering (argue / quarrel). - After the Tiananmen Square massacre in 1989, where China’s totalitarian regime suppressed a peaceful democratic demonstration, people in Hong Kong became nervous about their future. - Thousands of Hong Kong citizens applied for British citizenship. - To quell these fears, the British introduced democratic reforms, but they were largely meaningless, as China could nullify them after the transfer. - Post-Transfer Changes - In July 1997, the last British Governor of Hong Kong, Chris Patten, left. - The Chinese government installed an interim legislature to replace the colonial chamber. - The democratic measures introduced by Patten were abolished. - Hong Kong was declared a Special Administrative Region (SAR) of China, and Tung Chee-hwa, a businessman, was named the Chief Executive. - Tung was granted a second term in February 2002. - Independence of British Africa - The Rise of Nationalism in Africa - By 1945, nationalism spread across Africa. - Colonial troops in World War I and educated African youth in Britain imbibed ideas of democracy and nationalism. - The British Labour government under Clement Attlee was willing to transfer power but aimed to maintain economic control through neo-colonialism. - The decolonization process was delayed, as Britain tried to hold onto power. - Types of British Colonies in Africa (1945) - West Africa: Gold Coast, Nigeria, Sierra Leone, and Gambia. Minimal European presence made independence easier. - East Africa: Kenya, Uganda, and Tanganyika. Presence of British and Asian settlers complicated the process. - Central Africa: Nyasaland, Northern Rhodesia, and Southern Rhodesia. Europeans controlled the local economy and land, keeping locals marginalized. - Decolonization in West Africa - Sierra Leone became independent in 1961, followed by Gambia in 1965. - Independence was straightforward due to minimal European settlement. - - Challenges in East Africa - Tanganyika (later Tanzania) gained independence in 1961 under Julius Nyerere. - Uganda became independent in 1962 but struggled with tribal rivalries. - Kenya was complicated by white settlers resisting black majority rule. - Jomo Kenyatta and the Kenya African Unity Party led the independence movement. - Mau Mau secret society fought white settlers, leading to brutal suppression by the British. - Kenyatta was jailed for six years but released in 1963. Kenya became a moderate state. - Central Africa’s Push for Independence - In Central Africa, white settlers pushed for a Central African Federation to maintain control. - Leaders like Kenneth Kaunda, Joshua Nkomo, and Hastings Banda campaigned for black majority rule. - A 1960 commission recommended universal suffrage and ending racial discrimination. - Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia became independent in 1963. - Southern Rhodesia and Struggles for Independence - In Southern Rhodesia, white settlers clung to power, leading to Ian Smith declaring unilateral independence in 1965. - The UN condemned the move and imposed sanctions, but neighboring countries and US corporations continued support. - After Mozambique’s independence in 1975, the situation improved. - Robert Mugabe’s forces controlled most of Rhodesia by 1979, leading to negotiations. - Formation of Zimbabwe - In December 1979, an agreement created Zimbabwe with 80 out of 100 parliamentary seats for Africans. - Zimbabwe became independent in 1980, with Robert Mugabe as its Prime Minister. - Comparison with Other Colonizers - Britain made efforts to exit gracefully (in an attractive elegant way), unlike the French, Dutch, and Portuguese, who clung (hold on tightly) to their colonies. - After WWII, the French adopted the Brazzaville Declaration, denying autonomy to their colonies, resulting in forced independence later. - Independence of the French Colonies - French Indo-China and the War of Independence - French Indo-China included Vietnam, Laos, and Cambodia. - During World War II, Japanese occupation drove out the French. - Ho Chi Minh led the Vietnamese communists in resistance against the Japanese. - After the war, the French tried to reassert control, but the Vietnamese revolted. - After eight years of struggle, the Geneva Conference in 1954 granted independence to Vietnam, Laos, and Cambodia. - Tunisia’s Independence Struggle - In Tunisia, the New Destour group led by Habib Bourghiba opposed French rule. - The French sent 70,000 troops, imprisoned Bourghiba, and tried to suppress the nationalist movement. - Communist infiltration raised concerns in Paris, prompting the French to negotiate. - Bourghiba was released, and Tunisia gained independence in March 1956 under his leadership. - Morocco’s Path to Independence - Morocco faced a similar nationalist movement led by the Istiqlal party and trade unions. - France realized it couldn’t wage wars in both Indo-China and Africa. - King Muhammed V was allowed to return from exile, and Morocco gained independence in 1956. - Algeria’s Complex Struggle - Algeria posed a bigger challenge due to the presence of over 1 million French settlers. - Initially peaceful, the independence movement became militant under Ben Bella’s National Liberation Front after World War II. - A full-scale war broke out, with 700,000 French troops involved in brutal suppression. - The war caused significant social unrest in France, leading to the fall of the Fourth Republic and the rise of General Charles de Gaulle. - Charles de Gaulle and Negotiations - In October 1958, the Fifth Republic was established in France under Charles de Gaulle. - As the French army faced defeats in Algeria, de Gaulle realized victory was impossible and began negotiating with Ben Bella. - The OAS (terrorist group) supported by French generals fought against those favoring Algerian independence. - In July 1962, France withdrew, and Ben Bella became Algeria’s first President. - Charles de Gaulle’s Reconciliation (restoration of friendly relations) with Colonies - De Gaulle sought to reconcile with the colonies, moving away from treating them as part of France. - He proposed a new plan in 1958 for French West Africa and French Equatorial Africa to have self-government, while foreign affairs and taxation remained controlled by France. - A referendum was held in these countries, and Guinea under Sékou Touré chose outright independence. - Following Guinea’s example, Togo, Cameroon, and Madagascar also gained independence in 1960. - Dissolution of Other Empires - Dutch Empire - The Dutch exploited Indonesia (East Indies), offering little to alleviate the people’s suffering. - Ahmed Sukarno was the nationalist leader of Indonesia. - During World War II, the Japanese allowed Sukarno to administer Indonesia, promising independence after the war. - After the war, Sukarno declared Indonesia’s independence. - Holland attempted to regain control by sending its army but was pressured by the United Nations to grant Indonesia independence. - In 1949, Indonesia became independent, but Holland retained West Irian. - Sukarno expelled Europeans from West Irian in 1950. - Surinam, another Dutch possession, gained independence in 1975. - Belgian Empire - Belgium had colonies in Africa, including Belgian Congo and Ruanda-Urundi. - The Belgian colonial rule was brutal, denying locals education and fostering tribal divisions. - Nationalist movements in Leopoldville (capital of Belgian Congo) began in 1959. - The Belgian government, after suppression, decided to grant independence. - Congo gained independence in June 1960 under Patrice Lumumba. - Ruanda-Urundi became independent in 1962, splitting into Rwanda and Burundi, both ruled by the Tutsi. - Spanish Empire - Spain held colonies such as Spanish Sahara, Spanish Morocco, Ifni, and Spanish Guinea. - General Franco, the Spanish dictator, showed little resistance to nationalist movements and had no interest in colonies. - Spanish Morocco was relinquished (given up / renounced) to unify with independent Morocco in 1956. - Ifni joined Morocco in 1969. - Equatorial Guinea (Spanish Guinea) gained independence in 1968. - Spanish Sahara was retained by Franco due to its phosphate resources but was abandoned after his death in 1975. - Portuguese Empire - Portugal resisted granting independence to its African colonies, including Angola, Mozambique, and Portuguese Guinea, along with the eastern half of Timor. - Revolts broke out in Angola in 1961, led by the MPLA (People’s Movement for Angolan Liberation), supported by the USSR due to its communist leanings. - Portugal fought a costly war, leading to widespread public resistance. - In 1974, a military coup overthrew the Salazar dictatorship, leading to the independence of Portuguese colonies. - Italian Empire - Italy was the only colonizing power to accept indigenous independence demands with some grace. - After World War II, Italy lacked the power to resist independence movements. - Ethiopia was returned to Emperor Haile Selassie in 1941 after the Italian invasion in 1935. - Libya gained independence in 1951. - Eritrea was handed to Ethiopia in 1952 but retained autonomy. - Italian Somaliland merged with British Somaliland to form Somalia in 1960. - The British Commonwealth of Nations and Neo-Colonialism - Neo-colonialism means controlling or influencing a country politically, economically, or culturally even after it has become independent — without direct political rule. - Formation of the British Commonwealth - The British Commonwealth began with special relationships between white dominions like Australia, New Zealand, Canada, and South Africa in the early 20th century. - After World War II, the Labour government decided to grant independence to colonies, but British industrial lobbies pushed for preferential trade ties. - Neo-colonialism emerged, with Britain maintaining control over former colonies due to their weakened economies. - The British Commonwealth expanded, and former colonies continued to rely on Britain for trade. - British companies retained monopolies in key sectors until the 1980s. - Britain avoided joining the European Economic Community to maintain control over a market of 800 million people. - Role of the British Commonwealth - The Commonwealth was not just about exploitation. - It opposed the racist regime of South Africa in 1961. - It promoted economic, scientific, and technical cooperation among members. - The British monarch remains a symbolic head of the forum. - French Neo-Colonialism - France was more forceful in implementing neo-colonialism. - It initially resisted granting independence but later gave up political control. - However, French economic interests were maintained, particularly in Tunisia and Morocco. - Charles de Gaulle pushed for indirect control over former colonies’ economies. - Many African republics still felt the influence of French economic and foreign policies. - American Involvement in Neo-Colonialism - The United States also took part in neo-colonialism, leveraging its influence over former European powers. - Newly independent nations became dependent on American investments and markets, falling into debt traps. - African nations were the worst hit, struggling with internal conflicts. - Countries like Nigeria and the former Belgian Congo faced civil wars due to tribal divisions. - Attempts at socialist reforms were branded as “communist”, resulting in the cut-off of Western aid, worsening political and economic crises. - World War II and Global Change - World War II sparked independence movements worldwide. - It changed the geographical contours of nations, with new countries emerging. - The process of decolonization is ongoing, affecting global politics. - The next chapter discusses how the birth of Israel continues to impact West Asia. - West Asian conflicts - The Middle East Crisis - Overview of West Asia - West Asia, or the Middle East from the Western perspective, has been one of the most troubled regions since 1945. - The region has witnessed frequent violence, escalating into wars and terrorism sponsored by fanatical forces on both sides. - The Genesis (origin) of the Crisis - After World War II, Zionist movements (Jewish homeland activists) intensified their efforts to create a Jewish state. - As part of the Zionist movement, Jewish settlers began migrating to Palestine, then part of the Ottoman Empire and later under British rule after World War I. - The Holocaust in Germany, where six million Jews were systematically murdered, was the key catalyst. - The Balfour Declaration (1917) by Britain supported the establishment of a "national home for the Jewish people" in Palestine. - Jews, feeling vulnerable as refugees (people who are forced to leave their country) in foreign lands, believed that only a Jewish homeland could ensure their safety and prevent further persecution. - Biblical accounts of their expulsion (removal) from Israel reinforced their determination to restore the Jewish state. - Conflict with Arab Muslims - This led to a direct conflict with Muslim Arabs who had lived in Palestine for over a millennium. - The arrival of Jewish settlers, backed by American support after World War II, led to the formation of Israel in 1948, this left the Arabs feeling like refugees in their own land. - Why backed by America - Religious and Cultural Sympathy - Many Americans (especially Christians) historically felt a religious connection to the idea of a "Jewish homeland" because of the Bible. - Impact of the Holocaust (World War II) - Geopolitical Interests (Cold War) - After WWII, the Cold War started between the USA and the Soviet Union. - The Middle East became strategically important because of oil and geographical location. - Supporting Israel gave the USA a strong, loyal ally in a critical region. - Jewish American Influence - The USA had (and still has) a large and influential Jewish community. - They lobbied for American political support to establish and later to protect Israel. - Democracy and Shared Values - Many Americans felt that supporting Israel was like supporting democracy and "Western values" in the Middle East. - This marked the beginning of the Middle East Crisis, a conflict that remains unresolved. - Origins of the Arab-Israeli Conflicts - - - Historical Background - Jews were driven out of Palestine almost 2,000 years ago and faced persecution and violence across Europe for centuries. - The persecution culminated in mass murders such as pogroms (organized massacre of particular ethnic group) in many European countries. - The Zionist Movement - In 1897, wealthy European Jews founded the World Zionist Organisation in Basle, Switzerland, advocating for the return of Jews to their homeland. - They began to push their cause with European powers, especially Britain, which controlled Palestine at the time. - British Support for a Jewish Homeland - In 1917, British Foreign Minister Arthur Balfour announced support for a Jewish national home in Palestine. - Palestine became a British mandate (official approval) in 1919, and Jews started returning to the region, which caused dismay among the Arabs. - To calm Arab concerns, the British assured that not all of Palestine would be given to the Jews, aiming to create peaceful coexistence between Jews and Arabs. - Tensions After World War II - Post-WWII, Jews rejected the second-class citizen status in Europe and Britain faced pressure from the United States to allow 100,000 Jews into Palestine. - Reluctant to anger Arab sentiment, Britain initially refused to allow the influx, leading Jews to start a terrorist campaign targeting both the British and the Arabs. - Major Terrorist Incidents: - King David Hotel Bombing (July 1946) - Target: British administrative offices in Jerusalem (inside King David Hotel). - Executed by: Irgun. - Casualties: 91 people killed (British officials, Arabs, and Jews). - Goal: Attack British authority and push them to leave Palestine. - Assassination of Lord Moyne (1944) - Target: British Minister of State in the Middle East. - Executed by: Lehi (Stern Gang). - Goal: Protest British anti-immigration policies. - Attacks on British Soldiers and Police - Ambushes, bombings, and kidnappings became common. - They also attacked Arab villages when they saw them as a threat. - Illegal Immigration ("Aliyah Bet") and Ship Battles - Jews tried to smuggle Holocaust survivors into Palestine. - Armed confrontations happened between Jewish militias and British naval forces. - The Exodus (mass departure) ship, filled with European Jews, including Holocaust survivors, made a notable attempt to enter Palestine during this period. - UN Intervention and Partition - The British sought the United Nations intervention (involvement), and in November 1947, the UN voted in favor of partitioning Palestine. - Half of the territory was allocated to the Jews, establishing the new state of Israel. - The British withdrew from Palestine, and immediately afterward, the new state of Israel was attacked by a coalition of Egypt, Syria, Jordan, Iraq, and Lebanon. - Outcome of the First Arab-Israeli War - The First Arab-Israeli War (1948-1949), also known as the War of Independence by Israelis or the Nakba ("catastrophe") by Palestinians. - Initial Expectations - The world expected Israel to be easily defeated in the first Arab-Israeli war as it faced attacks from four sides. - However, Israel’s fight became a matter of survival, marking the Israeli style of warfare with fierce (ferocious / savage) defense and desperation (hopelessness). - Israel’s Success - Contrary to expectations, the Israelis not only fended (look after onself without outside help) off the attackers but also captured three-fourths of Palestine and the Egyptian port of Eilat on the Red Sea. - Reasons for Israel dominance - Strong national identity and leadership - Arab lack of coordination and underestimation of Israeli strength - Highly organized superious military, experience from participating in world war and strong defence - International aid and arms support - Psychological factors and the holocaust legacy - Nearly one million Arab Palestinians fled to Egypt, Lebanon, Jordan, and Syria, where they were placed in refugee camps. - Aftermath and Arab Response - Jordan occupied part of Jerusalem, while the US, Britain, and France guaranteed Israel’s frontiers. - Despite this, the Arab nations refused to recognize Israel’s legal status, viewing the conflict as just the first step in a larger struggle to liberate Palestine and destroy Israel. - From this point onward, Israel has remained in a state of war-preparedness. - The Suez Crisis, 1956 - Nasser’s Arab Unity Efforts - President Gamal Abdel Nasser of Egypt focused on building Arab unity, realizing that Israel would thrive if Arab states remained divided. - He formed secret fidayeen (suicide) squads, which carried out explosions deep inside Israel. - Nasser also targeted Western support for Israel and adopted aggressive policies against Britain and France. - Nationalization of the Suez Canal - The 1936 treaty allowing Britain to maintain troops on the Suez Canal expired in 1956. Nasser demanded British withdrawal. - He also incited (encouraged or stir up) other Arab states against Western powers. - Tensions escalated after Nasser signed an arms deal with the USSR in September 1955, which concerned the US. - In retaliation for the US freezing aid for the Aswan Dam, Nasser nationalized the Suez Canal. - Escalating Tensions and Western Response - Western powers feared a communist conspiracy to block their main oil source and began media propaganda likening Nasser to Hitler. - British politicians recalled the pre-WWII appeasement policy, which had proven ineffective. Plans were made to wage war against Egypt. - Israeli Attack and International Reaction - On 29 October 1956, Israeli forces attacked Egypt and captured the entire Sinai Peninsula within a week. - Britain and France bombed Egyptian airfields. - Approx. 100 aircrafts destroyed in action. - However, global opinion turned against the three countries. The US initially supported the plan but reversed its stance (posture / pose) and joined the USSR in calling for a cease-fire at the UN Security Council. - As a result, the Israelis withdrew, and UN peacekeepers were deployed to patrol the Israel-Egypt border. - Aftermath and Long-Term Consequences - The Suez Crisis marked the greatest humiliation for Britain and France in the 20th century. Britain’s status as a world power was severely diminished. - The US took control of much of Britain’s foreign policy. - Nasser emerged as a hero of Arab nationalism, and he strengthened ties with the USSR, creating a powerful Arab alliance. - These developments ultimately set the stage for the 1967 war. - The Six-Day War, 1967 - Background Factors - Iraq’s Ba’ath Party, in power since 1963, supported Arab independence and unity, with pro-Communist leanings and a willingness to cooperate with Egypt. - Syria’s Ba’ath Party came to power in 1966, backing fidayeen operations inside Israel. - With Soviet cooperation, Nasser had developed Egypt into a modern state and felt confident about launching another strike against Israel by 1967. - The Military Build-Up - By June 1967, Arab armies from Egypt, Syria, Jordan, Lebanon, Iraq, Saudi Arabia, and Algeria were all arrayed against Israel, putting Israel in a seemingly hopeless position. - However, Israel decided that offense was the best defense. On 5 June 1967, the Israeli air force launched a surprise attack and destroyed the entire Egyptian air force on the ground. - The blow to Egypt's air force was devastating, and it left the Egyptian military unable to effectively engage in further combat operations from the air. - The Egyptian army on the ground was also underprepared for the fast-moving Israeli offensive. - Simultaneously, Israeli ground forces captured the Gaza Strip and Sinai Peninsula from Egypt, the West Bank from Jordan, and the Golan Heights from Syria. - International Response and Aftermath - The United Nations passed a resolution calling for an immediate cease-fire, which the Arabs accepted on 10 June 1967. - The Israelis halted their advance but refused to return the captured territories, realizing they could use them as buffer zones between themselves and the Arabs in the future. - Consequences for Nasser and Arab Forces - For Nasser, the war was a major setback. Despite having large, well-armed armies, the Arab forces were ineffective in combat. - The Soviets, who had promised support, stayed out during the conflict. - After the war, the Soviets renewed efforts to supply the Arab states with weapons, and the Arab resolve (settle) was revived (bring back to life), leading to future attacks on Israel. - The Yom Kippur War, 1973 - Rise of Terrorism (1967-1973) - Between 1967 and 1973, terrorism became a potent force in the region. - The Palestinian Liberation Organization (PLO), led by Yasser Arafat, pressured Arab states to attack Israel, but they were less enthusiastic after their 1967 defeat. - The Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine (a faction of the PLO) carried out airline hijackings, which embarrassed King Hussein of Jordan, leading to the expulsion of PLO leaders from Jordan. - In September 1970, members of the PFLP hijacked four international flights bound for the United States and Israel. These hijackings were coordinated and involved planes being diverted to a remote desert airstrip in Jordan. - The hijackings were seen as a direct challenge to the stability of the Jordanian monarchy, and the kingdom was under increasing pressure from both internal and external actors to resolve the situation. - Terrorism escalated, with Israeli athletes attacked at the Munich Olympic Games in 1972. - 11 Israeli athletes and coaches hostage in the Israeli team’s quarters. The group demanded the release of over 200 Palestinians held in Israeli prisons, as well as the departure of Israeli forces from Palestinian territories. - When negotiations failed, and the Israeli athletes were killed. - The attack shocked the world and brought terrorism into sharp focus on the global stage. - Leadership Transition in Egypt - Nasser died in 1970, and his successor, Anwar Sadat, was less inclined to war. - Sadat favored a peace settlement with Israel to resolve the Palestinian issue and sent diplomatic missions to the US. - When the Nixon administration refused to mediate, Sadat, along with Syria, planned an attack on Israel, hoping to prompt American intervention. - The War Breaks Out - On 6 October 1973, during Yom Kippur, the Egyptians and Syrians attacked Israel. - Despite initial Arab successes, the Israelis counterattacked, retaining their territories and even crossing the Suez Canal into Egypt. - The Soviets refused to intervene on behalf of the Arabs, but Americans, as Sadat hoped, played a key role in enforcing a ceasefire. - Aftermath and Global Consequences - The Geneva talks led to Israel agreeing to withdraw troops from the Suez Canal. - The Arab states, angered by Western support for Israel, decided to restrict oil supplies to the West. - The Organisation of Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) raised oil prices, leading to global inflation and an energy crisis. - The Egypt-Israel Peace - - Sadat’s Realization and Decision - President Sadat recognized that Israel could not be defeated outright in war. - However, engaging in dialogue with Israel meant acknowledging its existence, which could isolate Egypt from the Arab world. - Despite this, Sadat took the historic step of visiting Israel in November 1977, a decision that would change the course of relations. - Initial Steps Towards Peace - Menachem Begin, the Israeli Prime Minister, reciprocated (do the same) by visiting Egypt in December 1977. - At this time, Israel was facing economic challenges, and Begin realized that indefinite war-readiness was unsustainable. - President Jimmy Carter of the US recognized the emerging desire for peace and mediated peace talks, which began in September 1978 at Camp David near Washington D.C. - The Peace Treaty - In March 1979, the Camp David Accords resulted in a peace treaty, ending the state of war between Israel and Egypt, which had existed since 1948. - Israel agreed to withdraw its troops from Sinai. - In return, Egypt promised not to attack Israel again and ensured oil supplies from Southern Sinai. - Israel gained the right to use the Suez Canal. - Reactions and Aftermath - Both Sadat and Begin received international recognition for achieving the peace treaty. - However, the PLO and most of the Arab world (except Morocco and Sudan) condemned Egypt for the agreement. - Following the treaty, Israel became more assertive (confident), declaring it would never return the Golan Heights to Syria, even if a separate peace agreement was made. - Israel also stated that it would not give up the West Bank to Arab Palestinians, justifying these positions on the grounds of security. - BEGINNING OF THE MIDDLE EAST PEACE PROCESS - Sadat’s Assassination and Mubarak’s Stance - Sadat was assassinated in October 1981 by extremists in his own army, angry over his peace efforts. - His successor, Hosni Mubarak, upheld the Camp David Agreement, ensuring that Egypt would continue its peace with Israel. - Israeli Actions and Palestinian Response - Begin intensified settlement of Jewish people on the West Bank. - From 1987, Israeli suppression of Palestinian protests attracted global condemnation. - Shift Towards Negotiation - In 1992, Prime Minister Yitzhak Rabin and Foreign Minister Shimon Peres responded to international calls to negotiate with the PLO. - By 1994, the Oslo Accords were signed, with Israel formally recognizing the PLO and vice versa. - The agreement set the framework for “land for peace”, including Palestinian self-rule in the West Bank and Gaza Strip. - Terms of the Oslo Accords - Key issues for future negotiation included: - Palestinian statehood - Status of Jerusalem - Jewish settlements in occupied territories - Return of Palestinian refugees - Arafat promised to end violence, and Israel was to withdraw based on UN resolutions. - Resistance and Domestic Challenges - Extremist groups opposed the Oslo deal, with the Popular Front demanding full independence. - Israeli settlers resisted leaving the West Bank. - In September 1995, Israel agreed to withdraw from most of the West Bank, and Palestinians were given voting rights for a Palestinian parliament. - Prime Minister Rabin was assassinated by a Jewish fundamentalist in November 1995, dampening the peace process. - Continued Violence and US Involvement - Terrorist attacks by groups like Hamas and Hezbollah hindered progress. - In 2000, Camp David II talks almost resulted in a final settlement but broke down over the status of Jerusalem. - Israel claimed Jerusalem as its eternal capital, while Palestinians wanted East Jerusalem as their capital. - Renewed Violence and Israeli Response - Ariel Sharon’s visit to Haram al-Sharif in 2000 led to widespread violence. - By 2001, over 1,000 people had been killed, mainly Palestinians. - In March 2002, Israel launched Operation Defensive Shield, aimed at dismantling Palestinian terrorist infrastructure, and confined (restricted) Arafat to his headquarters (head office). - Suicide bombings in June 2002 led to further Israeli occupation of Palestinian towns. - The US Peace Plan - In June 2002, US President George W. Bush outlined a vision for a post-Oslo solution, focusing on a two-state resolution. - A Quartet (US, EU, Russia, and UN) proposed a “Road Map”, supporting Palestinian self-determination and Israeli security. - However, this plan was criticized by Israel for lacking a commitment from Palestinians to renounce (give up) violence. - OIL AND GEOPOLITICS - Discovery of Oil and Its Impact - West Asia contains the world’s largest petroleum reserves. - In the 1930s, the discovery of oil in eastern Arabia significantly shifted global politics. - The British and Americans rushed to Saudi Arabia, securing deals with King Abdul-Aziz ibn Saud, who ruled from 1906-1953 and united Hejaz and Najd to form the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia. - King Abdul-Aziz granted concessions to the Arabian-American Oil Company (Aramco), a joint venture between Texaco and Standard Oil Company of California (USA). - Royalties from Aramco helped fund Saudi Arabia’s rapid development. - Saudi Arabia’s Political System - Saudi Arabia follows an 18th-century absolute monarchy model, with power concentrated in the King and a few princes. - Women are excluded from public offices and must seek permission from their male guardians to travel abroad, despite being educated. - Society is strictly Islamic, and Sharia law is absolute, with public beheadings for criminals and stoning of women deemed (regarded as) immoral. - Despite lacking political freedoms, the people benefit from extensive social security, unparalleled in many parts of the world. - Oil’s Influence on Saudi Arabia’s Power - Saudi Arabia’s wealth from oil grants it significant control over the Islamic world and serves as both an economic and political weapon. - The country’s foreign policy is anti-Israel, but during the Cold War, the United States supported Saudi Arabia due to its fiercely anti-communist stance. - ORGANISATION OF PETROLEUM EXPORTING COUNTRIES (OPEC) - Emergence of Regionalism - After World War II, regional arrangements and organizations grew in importance, as they proved more effective in organizational action compared to global ones. - Regionalism refers to the concept of mobilizing states and areas on a geopolitical basis. - Distribution of Oil Reserves and Production - Oil reserves and production are unevenly distributed across the world. - 66% of global oil reserves are located around the Persian Gulf. - Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Iran, Iraq, and the UAE are the main producers, accounting for 75% of global oil exports. - Formation of OPEC - In 1962, conflicts with international oil companies and their decision to reduce the price of Middle Eastern crude oil led to the Baghdad Declaration. - The declaration called for the formation of OPEC (Organisation of Petroleum Exporting Countries). -
