Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Iran, and Venezuela were the founding members, accounting for 80% of the global oil trade. - Since then, the membership of OPEC has expanded. - OPEC played a key role in countering the manipulation of oil prices and other selfish practices by international companies. - Saudi Arabia and OPEC’s Influence - The formation of OPEC is considered a landmark in efforts toward a more equitable global economic order, despite Ecuador and Iran withdrawing from the organization in 1992. - Saudi Arabia played a significant role in forming OPEC. - In 1973-74, angry with Western attitudes towards Israel, King Faisal of Saudi Arabia imposed an oil embargo (official ban), advised by his oil minister, Sheikh Ahmed Zaki Yamani. - The embargo sent shockwaves through the global oil market, as the U.S. relied on Saudi Arabia for 20% of its oil imports. - Saudi Arabia invested its oil royalties in U.S. banks, stocks, and bonds. - Iran’s Oil and Political History - Iran, Saudi Arabia’s eastern neighbor, also possesses vast oil reserves. - British interests in Iran’s oil were driven by the collapse of the Russian Empire. - Lord Curzon, the British foreign secretary, secured political and military control over Persia (Iran) to serve as a buffer state between India and Bolshevik Russia. - The Anglo-Persian Oil Company held lucrative oil exploration concessions until 1932, when Reza Khan (later Shah of Iran) canceled them. - The British were forced to accept profit-sharing with the Iranian government. - During World War II, Iran was occupied by Britain, Russia, and the U.S.. - Conflict over Nationalisation of Oil - In 1951, Iranian parliament authorized the nationalization of its petroleum industry to improve its economy. - This move angered the U.S. and Britain, who opposed economic independence in a semi-colonial state. - The U.S. government cut off aid, and the British enforced an oil embargo, preventing tankers from reaching Iran’s markets. - As a result, Iran faced economic collapse, with refineries shutting down. - In 1953, the CIA sponsored a coup to restore the Shah to power. - The Shah, Reza Pahlavi, was reduced to a puppet leader, and under a treaty, Iran’s oil industry was handed over to five American companies, with the U.S. controlling 40% of the industry. - ORGANISATION OF ISLAMIC COUNTRIES - Formation and Early Deliberations (long and careful consideration or discussion) - In September 1969, a summit of Muslim Heads of State was held at Rabat. - This was followed by Islamic Foreign Ministers’ Conferences in March at Jeddah and in December 1970 at Karachi. - These deliberations led to the formation of the Organisation of Islamic States (OIS), initially with 45 members. - Structure and Functioning - The Conference of Heads of State is the supreme body of the OIS. - It is meant to meet once every three years. - The priorities of the OIS include: - Cooperation for mutual and collective development - Elimination of racism - Safety of holy places - Promotion of world peace and Islamic solidarity - Headquarters - The headquarters of the Organisation of Islamic States is located in Saudi Arabia. - THE ISLAMIC REVOLUTION IN IRAN, 1978 - Reign of Muhammad Reza Pahlavi - Reign lasted four decades, making Iran wealthy. - The Shah ruled with repression (subduing by force), using secret police. - Terror and torture were used to maintain loyalty. - Westernisation caused resentment (bitterness) among Shia population. - Uprising and Revolution - Economic slump (flop) in the late 1970s led to protests. - Shah used violence to suppress the revolt but failed. - Ayatollah Khomeini, exiled in 1964, led the revolution from Paris. - Shah vacated the throne on 16 January 1979. - Impact on the Islamic World - Inspired Muslim clergy (the body of all people ordained for religios duties) to reject Western-style development. - Khomeini imposed Shariat law and an anti-American, anti-Israeli policy. - ‘Shaitan-e-Buzurg’ (great Satan) became a rallying cry against the U.S. - The Hostage Crisis - Shah admitted to the U.S. for treatment, angering Khomeini. - 52 American diplomats taken hostage in November 1979. - Hostage crisis ended in January 1981 after negotiations. - The Shah moved to Panama, then Egypt, where he died in July 1980. - THE IRAN-IRAQ WAR - Background and Causes - Shatt al Arab Waterway: Disputes over ownership between Iran and Iraq. - Important for oil exports and forms part of their border. - Iraq controlled it until Shah of Iran forced shared control. - Saddam Hussein’s Decision: - Saw Iran’s Islamic revolution as an opportunity to strike. - Assumed the revolution would keep Iran’s army busy. - Launched attack in September 1980. - Early War Dynamics - Saddam’s Strategy: - Poor strategist, underestimated Iranian resistance. - Tensions had been brewing over Iraq’s secular leadership fearing Khomeini’s influence. - Both regimes supported rebel movements against each other. - Diplomatic Relations Severed: - In June 1980, Iraq and Iran severed ties. - Border clashes broke out. - Global Involvement and Strategic Concerns - Global Impact: - Both sides engaged in a prolonged war, weakening both countries. - U.S. interests: Wanted a drawn-out, inconclusive war to weaken both sides, maintaining stability in the region. - Support for Iran: - Syria, Libya, China, North Korea, and Israel supported Iran. - Israel: Despite anti-Zionist Ayatollah Khomeini, Israel supported Iran due to Saddam’s ambitions. - Support for Iraq: - Saudi Arabia, Kuwait, Jordan, Egypt, the U.S., France, and the USSR backed Iraq. - U.S.: Supplied Iraq with advanced weapons. - France and USSR: Both sided with Iraq. - Shifting War Dynamics - Iraq’s Early Success: - Seized southern Iran and besieged refinery towns. - Turning Point: - Mid-1981: Iran broke out of Abadan and recaptured Khorramshahr. - By June 1982, Iraq was driven out of Iran, and Iraq fought defensively. - Iran’s Stubbornness: - Ayatollah rejected peace offers from Saddam. - War continued for six more years with neither side gaining a clear victory. - American Involvement and Arms Deals - American Policy: - U.S. didn’t want either side to win. - Supplied arms to both Iran and Iraq. - Arms deal with Iran was clandestine (secret) due to U.S. arms embargo (official ban). - Embarrassing Revelation: The arms deal to Iran became politically sensitive when exposed (unprotected / visible). - Conclusion and Aftermath - End of the War: - By 1988, both sides were exhausted. - UN brokered (middleman) a ceasefire, with Iran accepting first. - No victory claimed by either side, and the border dispute remained unresolved. - Human Cost: - One million deaths, including civilians, and two million wounded. - Financial cost: Over $200 billion for both sides. - Diplomatic Relations: - Resumed two years later after Iraq withdrew troops and focused on invading Kuwait. - Agreement over the Shatt al Arab and exchanged prisoners. - EFFECTS OF THE WAR - Long-term Impact - Iran: - Continued spreading instability across West Asia. - Supported Shiite terrorists, threatening Gulf monarchies, especially Saudi Arabia. - Used Lebanon as a base for attacks on Israel and Western nations backing Israel. - Iraq: - Saddam Hussein remained dictator until March 2003. - Briefly invaded Kuwait in 1990, but was driven out by a U.S.-led international coalition. - From 1991 to 2003, Iraq faced UN sanctions which severely impacted Saddam’s regime. - Global Consequences - The world, especially the United States, continued to deal with Iraq’s ambitions to dominate West Asia, leading to further international intervention. - RISE OF SADDAM HUSSEIN - Early Life and Political Ideology - Born: 1937, Tikrit district of Iraq, into a humble rural family. - Childhood: A lonely child, often carrying an iron bar. - Political Ideas: - Influenced by Pan-Arab nationalism of President Nasser of Egypt. - Associated with American intelligence services during his youth. - Joined the Ba’ath Party, which had a presence across the Arab world, and became a socialist. - Rise to Power - 1968: Ba’ath Party came to power in Iraq; Saddam became vice-chairman. - Popularity: - Implemented a mass literacy program, making failure to send a child to school punishable by three years in prison. - Received an award from UNESCO for this initiative. - Transformed Iraq with improved education, roads, public housing, and health systems. - Political Shifts and Aggression - Islamic Revolution in Iran (1979): Changed Saddam’s outlook. - Concerned about Islamic fundamentalism spreading into Iraq, a secular state. - Became externally aggressive to prevent this, receiving support from Western powers keen to protect regional oil reserves from fundamentalists. - Dictatorship and Brutality - Brutal Leadership: - Known for using biological and chemical weapons against Iraq’s Kurdish population. - Employed torture and murder to suppress dissent. - Family Influence: His two sons were equally feared figures. - Admiration for Stalin: Saddam openly acknowledged Stalin as his role model. - Western Support and Demonization - Initially supported and backed by Western powers. - Over time, these same powers turned against him, demonizing him for defying their geopolitical interests. - INVASION OF KUWAIT AND THE FIRST GULF WAR - Motivations for Invasion - Saddam’s Economic Struggles: After the Iran-Iraq war, Saddam needed funds to revive Iraq’s economy. - Kuwait’s Oil Reserves: Viewed Kuwait’s oil wealth as a potential solution. - International Miscalculation: Saddam believed no world power would intervene if Iraq took over Kuwait, as the US, USSR, Britain, and France had supported him during the Iran conflict. - Claim for Justification: Saddam claimed Kuwait had once been part of Iraq, though it had been a British protectorate since 1899. - Events Leading to Invasion - Oil Overproduction: In May 1990, Kuwait and the UAE started overproducing oil, which caused a drop in Iraqi oil prices. - Saddam termed this an ‘act of economic warfare’ against Iraq. - Oil Theft Accusations: In July, Saddam accused Kuwait of stealing oil from the Rumaila oil fields on the Iraq-Kuwait border. - Military Buildup: By the end of July 1990, Iraq began amassing troops on the Kuwaiti border. - Invasion: On 2nd August 1990, Iraqi troops crossed into Kuwait and seized its oil fields. The Emir of Kuwait fled to Saudi Arabia. - International Response and Escalation - Saudi Plea for Help: Iraq threatened Saudi Arabia by positioning troops on its border, prompting Saudi Arabia to urgently request US military assistance. - US and UN Action: - UN Sanctions: Imposed a trade embargo on Iraq on 6th August 1990. - Saddam’s Reaction: Saddam declared a jihad against the US and Israel, and renamed Kuwait City al-Khadima. - International Coalition: - Britain and France pledged military support for action. - Several countries from Europe, Asia, Africa, and South America joined the coalition. - The USSR remained neutral but attempted to mediate. - UN Deadline: On 17th December 1990, the UN set a deadline for Iraqi withdrawal from Kuwait by 15th January 1991. Saddam rejected all UN resolutions. - Operation Desert Storm - Military Action Begins: After diplomatic efforts failed, Operation Desert Storm commenced on 17th January 1991. - Over the course of four months, US planes bombed strategic targets in Iraq. - Saddam’s response was weaker than expected. - Saddam’s Actions: - Ordered Iraqi air force pilots to fly planes to Iran for safekeeping. - Instead of attacking Saudi coalition bases, he launched Scud (moving fast in straight line) missiles at Israel. - Ordered Iraqi troops to destroy Kuwait’s oil installations and create an environmental disaster by dumping crude oil into the Persian Gulf. - Liberation of Kuwait - Ground Operations: On 24th February 1991, coalition forces began ground operations to liberate Kuwait. - Weak Iraqi Resistance: Iraqi soldiers offered little resistance and quickly retreated. - Kuwait’s Freedom: By 27th February, Kuwait was liberated. - Cease-fire: On 3rd March 1991, Iraq officially accepted a cease-fire. - AFTERMATH OF THE WAR - Lack of Iraqi Revolt - The Gulf War raised hopes for an Iraqi revolt, especially among Kurds and Shia Muslims, but these uprisings were crushed when the Allies did not intervene. - Kurds: Suppressed by Saddam, with neighboring countries staying passive due to their own Kurdish populations. - Shia Revolt: The US did not support the Shia uprising, fearing that it would strengthen Iran. - US and UN Response - The US pushed for a safe haven for Kurds north of 32°N latitude and imposed a no-fly zone south of 32°S latitude to limit Saddam’s power. - Preparation for Future Conflict - The groundwork for another conflict was laid over the 12 years following the 1991 ceasefire. - Bill Clinton focused on other global issues, but Saddam remained a concern. - Continued Hostility During Clinton’s Presidency - 1993 Attack: The US struck Iraq’s military intelligence headquarters in retaliation for the attempted assassination of former President Bush. - Weapons of Mass Destruction: The US continued to suspect Iraq of developing chemical and biological weapons, leading to further sanctions. - UN Sanctions and Their Impact - Resolution 687 (1991): The UN imposed strict sanctions on Iraq, demanding the destruction of weapons of mass destruction. - Impact: Iraq’s economy was devastated, and Iraq was barred from selling oil until it complied with the UN’s disarmament requirements. - The Oil-for-Food Program - Resolution 986 (1995): The UN allowed Iraq to sell oil, but the funds went to a UN-managed account for buying essential supplies. - Impact: The program provided only 32 cents per day per person, insufficient to meet basic needs. The allocation was later doubled in 1997. - Humanitarian Consequences - Child Mortality: - 1990: 56 children per 1,000 died. - 1999: 131 per 1,000. - The trend would have dropped to 25 per 1,000 by 2000 if it had continued as in the 1980s. - THE RUN-UP TO ANOTHER WAR - Saddam’s Continued Defiance - Saddam Hussein defied UN resolutions and strengthened his power base. - In 1995, he held a referendum that granted him the right to rule for another seven years. A similar exercise occurred in 2002, with 99.9% support claimed for his continued rule. - UNSCOM and the Weapons Inspections - The UN Weapons Inspection team (UNSCOM) continued to check Iraq’s civilian and military sites for disarmament compliance. - The US government claimed Iraq was uncooperative. - In October 1998, Iraq announced it would no longer accept UNSCOM inspections. - In retaliation, the US and Britain launched Operation Desert Fox to destroy Iraq’s weapons programs. - Continued Defiance and New Inspection Body - Saddam remained defiant, rejecting a December 1999 UN resolution that created the UNMOVIC (UN Monitoring, Verification, and Inspection Commission). - In February 2001, US and British planes conducted another bombing operation to destroy Iraq’s air defense system, without international approval. - September 11 Attacks and the War on Terror - September 11, 2001: Terrorists hijacked four planes and attacked the World Trade Center in New York, killing over 4,000 people. - In response, President George W. Bush declared a “war against terrorism” and attacked Afghanistan, targeting the Taliban regime that hosted Osama bin Laden. - Shift Towards Iraq - After defeating the Taliban, Bush turned attention to Saddam Hussein. - Bush advocated for “regime change”, accusing Saddam of evading weapons inspections and being a regional threat. - The US and Britain were united in supporting this stance, with the US acting unilaterally as the sole global superpower post-USSR dissolution. - Iraq’s Response - Saddam realized the seriousness of the situation and allowed UNMOVIC inspectors to return, claiming cooperation. - Hans Blix, the Chairman of UNMOVIC, acknowledged Iraq’s increased cooperation, stating more time was needed for inspections. - Despite no evidence of concealed weapons of mass destruction, the US and Britain continued to demand Saddam’s removal. - US Ultimatum and the Invasion - On 17 March 2003, the US gave Saddam and his sons 48 hours to leave Iraq or face war. - 20 March 2003: US and British forces began bombing Iraq. - Ground operations followed immediately, and by 9 April 2003, US forces entered Baghdad. - Kurdish forces, under US-British control, seized Kirkuk and Mosul. - Saddam and his sons, however, managed to escape before Baghdad fell. - LONG TERM EFFECTS OF THE SECOND GULF WAR - Operation Iraq Freedom’s Outcome - “Operation Iraq Freedom” ended quicker than expected, showcasing the full force of America’s military might. - Thanks to television, people worldwide witnessed the conflict unfold in real-time. - Saddam Hussein and the Iraqi People - While Saddam was no saint, many Iraqis viewed the US troops as liberators due to the oppression they faced under Saddam’s dictatorship. - However, the US applied different standards in its global approach, offering full support to Pervez Musharraf’s regime in Pakistan, despite its undemocratic actions and sponsorship of terrorism. - Expectations and Reality Post-Gulf War - After the Gulf War, there were hopes for a “chain reaction” of reforms across West Asia, where many believed that the undemocratic regimes would evolve after 1991. - Instead, many countries, including Saudi Arabia, UAE, and Kuwait, became more conservative, with women’s rights remaining severely restricted. - These countries continued to secretly sponsor Islamic fundamentalism and terrorism, raising concerns about the future of the region. - The Enduring Fundamentals of West Asia - In conclusion, the fundamentals of West Asia have changed little since 1945. - Western interests in oil continue to dominate regional politics. - The dream of Jewish-Palestinian good neighborliness remains as elusive (difficult to find or catch) as it was in the 1940s. - Despite unprecedented material prosperity, the region’s socio-economic structure remains largely backward. - West Asia, much like the desert it rests upon, remains resistant to significant change. - The cold war years - TWO YEARS OF MISTRUST - The Cold War’s Threat - For 45 years after WWII, the world feared a catastrophic war between the US and the Soviet Union. - Weapons of mass destruction were stockpiled, ready to be deployed within minutes. - The conflict was ideological: free enterprise vs. communism, democracy vs. totalitarianism. - The End of the Cold War - Surprisingly, World War III did not happen. - Bernard Baruch coined the term “Cold War” in 1946 after tensions in Berlin. - Rise of Superpowers - The US and USSR became the world’s two superpowers. - Britain and France became secondary powers. - The USSR replaced Nazi governments in Eastern Europe with communist regimes. - Polarization of the World - The world split into two blocs: the Western Bloc (led by the US) and the Eastern Bloc (led by the USSR). - Western Europe joined the US fearing communism. - A Tense Stand-off - The Cold War remained “cold” with no direct conflict. - Both sides feared accidental war, which could destroy humanity. - Spies and pacts were common, but direct war was avoided. - ORIGINS OF THE COLD WAR - Strained Relations Before WWII - Allied powers and the USSR had tense relations until Hitler’s invasion of Russia in 1941. - The US sent significant war material and humanitarian supplies to the USSR under the Lend-Lease system, with repayment expected later. - President Roosevelt of the US had a good relationship with Stalin, but Harry S. Truman (Roosevelt’s successor) was highly suspicious of Stalin’s intentions. - Stalin’s Concerns - Stalin feared that once Nazi Germany was defeated, the US and Britain would turn their war machinery against the USSR. - The Yalta Conference - In February 1945, Roosevelt, Stalin, and Winston Churchill met at Yalta to plan the post-WWII world. - Germany was to be divided into three zones: British, American, and Soviet. The French zone was added later. - Berlin, in the middle of Germany, was also divided into three zones (later four). - The creation of the United Nations was agreed to replace the League of Nations. - Democracies would be established in the countries freed from Nazi rule, allowing the people to elect their governments. - The USSR would join the war against Japan, with the condition of receiving the Sakhalin Islands and some territory in Manchuria. - The Potsdam Conference - In July 1945, a follow-up conference was held at Potsdam between Churchill, Stalin, and Truman (Roosevelt had died in April 1945). - The meeting revealed tensions between the three powers, and no decision could be reached on the long-term future of Germany. - The biggest issue was Poland. Stalin had broken his promise by occupying a large part of Eastern Germany. - A pro-communist regime was established in Poland, leading to atrocities and mass evictions of German people. - Communist Satellite States - Stalin’s Puppet Governments - Stalin exacerbated the crisis by establishing puppet communist governments in Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria, Albania, and Romania. - Political opponents were systematically eliminated in most of these countries. - In Hungary, where Soviets allowed elections, the communists won less than 20% of the vote, but Stalin ordered mass murder and terrorized non-communists. - By the end of 1947, every Eastern European country, except Czechoslovakia, had a Stalinist government. - Yugoslavia’s Resistance - Yugoslavia was an exception. Marshal Tito, who had led a heroic partisan struggle against the Nazis, became the President in 1945. - - Since the Soviets did not have an army stationed there, Stalin could not take action against Tito. - Stalin’s Speech on Communism - In February 1946, Stalin stated that communism and capitalism could never coexist peacefully. - He declared that future wars were inevitable until the final victory of world communism. - The Coup in Czechoslovakia - In February 1948, Czechoslovakia—the last free Eastern European country—suffered a Moscow-backed coup and a complete communist takeover. - The country had previously had a coalition government of communists and other left-wing parties after the 1946 elections, but the coup shocked the world. - The Truman Doctrine - In March 1947, President Truman announced the Truman Doctrine, stating that America would support free people resisting subjugation by armed minorities or outside pressures. - The doctrine was spurred by the civil war in Greece, where communists began fighting post-war. - It also committed the US to a policy of containing communism not only in Europe but globally. - Marshall Plan and Cominform - Post-War Economic Recovery - World War II devastated the economies of Western Europe under US influence. - In June 1947, George C. Marshall announced the European Recovery Programme, offering financial aid to rebuild Europe. - The goal was to revive economies and open up markets, addressing poverty, which could breed communism. - Exclusion of Eastern Europe - The Marshall Plan was offered to Eastern Europe, but Soviet regimes (government) couldn’t decide independently. - The Soviets called it ‘dollar imperialism’ and blocked funds from reaching Eastern Europe. - Stalin’s Response: Cominform - In September 1947, Stalin set up Cominform to: - Assert the supremacy of Soviet-style communism. - Support industrialization of the East Bloc. - Announce exclusive trade among East Bloc nations. - Yugoslavia’s Expulsion - Yugoslavia, opposing Stalin’s policy, was expelled from Cominform. - Comecon and Molotov Plan - Comecon coordinated the Eastern Bloc’s economic development. - The Molotov Plan was the Soviet alternative to the Marshall Plan to finance Comecon. - Berlin Blockade - Crisis Overview - The Berlin Blockade (seize), starting in 1948, was the biggest crisis of the early Cold War. - Stalin not only prevented the implementation of the Marshall Plan in Soviet-controlled Germany but also exploited the region. - German civilians suffered from starvation and oppression under Soviet control. - Meanwhile, the British, American, and French zones merged into a single economic unit and quickly began recovering. - As a result, thousands of refugees fled westward. - Soviet Actions - The Soviets sealed all roads, rails, and canal links between West and East Berlin, cutting off western aid to the people in East Berlin. - East Berliners faced starvation due to the blockade. - Western Response - The United States launched an airlift, flying in food and essential supplies, even at the risk of intruding into Soviet airspace. - This airlift continued for 10 months without interruption. - - Conclusion - The Soviets ultimately lifted the blockade in 1949, unable to prevent the ongoing supply effort. - Formation of NATO - Creation of NATO - After their success, the western powers sought to create a strategic (long terma aims and means to achieving them) alliance (union / association) to prevent a communist invasion of Western Europe backed by the USSR. - The North Atlantic Treaty Organisation (NATO) was established in April 1949. - A key principle of NATO was that an attack on any one of the 10 founding members would be regarded as an attack on all. - Expansion and Response - Over time, NATO grew into a formidable (intimidating) military bloc focused on defending the Western Hemisphere. - In 1954, the Soviets created their own version of NATO, called the Warsaw Pact. - Nuclear Arms Race - After the United States became the first country with nuclear capability, their use of the atomic bomb on Hiroshima and Nagasaki in World War II was met with global condemnation. - Stalin directed his secret agents worldwide to steal U.S. nuclear secrets, with the help of communist sympathizers (well-wishers / supporters). - These efforts enabled the Soviets to obtain the blueprints for the atomic bomb, leading to a successful test in September 1949. - This triggered the start of the nuclear arms race, a continual global threat to peace. - U.S. Response and Global Arms Race - In response, President Truman ordered the development and testing of a hydrogen bomb, much more powerful than an atomic bomb. - This led to an ongoing arms race involving many nations, diverting resources away from development to military production. - End of Stalin Era and the Rise of Khrushchev - Death of Stalin and Leadership Transition - Joseph Stalin died on 5 March 1953. New evidence suggests he may have been murdered by his close aides (assistant). - His war-mongering (encouragement or advocacy towards violence towards other nations) nature had created anxiety even among the top ranks of the Soviet Communist Party. - After his death, Georgi Malenkov and Nikita Khrushchev took over, showing inclination toward peace with the United States. - In 1956, Khrushchev fundamentally altered Stalin’s worldview by advocating peaceful co-existence between the West and East, stating: - “There are only two ways—either peaceful co-existence or the most destructive war in history. There is no third way.” - Though Khrushchev remained committed to world communism, he did not believe in war as a means to achieve it. - The ‘Thaw’ in Cold War Tensions - The thaw (soften) in relations between the USSR and the West began under Khrushchev. - He made key concessions (compromises), including: - Withdrawing USSR’s military bases from Finland. - Lifting the Soviet veto in the UN Security Council, allowing 16 new member states to join. - Patching up relations with Yugoslavia. - Formally abandoning Cominform. - Soviet Control Over Satellite States (states that are formally independent but are under heavy influence of anouther state or country) - Despite the thaw, Khrushchev had no intention of loosening control over Soviet satellite states. - In 1956, a popular uprising (resistance / rebellion) erupted (become active / explode) in Budapest, Hungary. - Khrushchev responded by sending troops to brutally crush the rebellion, proving that the thaw was only superficial. - To further solidify Soviet influence, he formed the Warsaw Pact (1955), a military alliance including: - Albania, Bulgaria, Czechoslovakia, East Germany, Hungary, Poland, Romania, and the USSR. - A unified military command was established in Moscow. - The Arms Race and Space Race - Khrushchev aggressively armed the USSR with advanced weapons. - The Soviet Hydrogen Bomb was developed using espionage (spying) networks and communist sympathizers in the West. - The USSR also developed: - A long-range bomber plane capable of reaching the United States. - The Intercontinental Ballistic Missile (ICBM), capable of striking U.S. cities with nuclear warheads. - The U.S. responded by developing their own ICBM, the Atlas, escalating the arms race. - The rivalry extended into space: - The USSR launched Sputnik I (1958), the world’s first artificial satellite. - The U.S. countered with Explorer 1. - The Berlin Crisis and the Cold War’s Continuation - By 1958, Khrushchev had gained a strategic edge in the arms race and used it to challenge the West. - He declared that the USSR no longer recognized Western rights in West Berlin. - The U.S. responded with a firm (solid) stance (pose), warning against any Soviet aggression. - In 1961, the Berlin Wall was built to stop East Germans from escaping to the West. - The Berlin Wall stood for 29 years, symbolizing the division of Germany. - Many people made daring escape attempts, but most were shot down by communist forces. - - The Cuban Missile Crisis (1962) - Communist Takeover of Cuba - In January 1959, Cuba came under communist rule when Fidel Castro’s guerrilla army overthrew the corrupt Batista regime. - The United States had significant (notable / worthy of attention) business interests in Cuba. - Castro retaliated (fight back) by nationalizing U.S. plantations and industries, causing thousands of Cubans to flee to the U.S. by boat. - U.S. Response and the Bay of Pigs Invasion (1961) - President Dwight D. Eisenhower adopted an aggressive stance toward Cuba. - The CIA began training Cuban refugees in the U.S. to launch an attack and overthrow Castro’s communist government. - President John F. Kennedy, Eisenhower’s successor, authorized the invasion. - In April 1961, 1,400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs from the U.S. military base in Guantanamo Bay. - The operation was poorly planned, and Castro’s forces easily crushed the invaders. - Soviet Involvement and Missile Deployment - The USSR provided economic assistance to Cuba to counter the impact of the U.S. blockade. - In 1962, U.S. intelligence discovered that the Soviets had established (having taken root) missile bases inside Cuba. - Satellite and aerial photographs confirmed the presence of nuclear missiles. - This directly threatened the U.S., as Soviet missiles could strike American cities in the event of war. - The USSR resumed atmospheric nuclear testing on 1 September 1961, while the U.S. responded with underground nuclear tests. - The Crisis and Resolution - The UN Secretary-General, U Thant, appealed for restraint (restriction / constraint) from both sides. - The crisis lasted for several tense days. - Ultimately, Khrushchev agreed to remove Soviet missiles from Cuba in exchange for a U.S. pledge not to attack Cuba. - President Kennedy also agreed to dismantle American missiles in Turkey as part of the deal. - To prevent future misunderstandings, a direct telephone link, known as the ‘hotline’, was established between the U.S. and Soviet leaders for swift communication in times of crisis. - Regional Military Alliances During the Cold War - During the Cold War, nations formed military alliances to protect themselves against perceived threats from the superpowers—the United States and the Soviet Union. - These alliances were primarily politico-military pacts, with the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) being the largest and most influential (powerful). - U.S.-Led Military Alliances - NATO (North Atlantic Treaty Organization) – The most significant military alliance, formed to counter the Soviet threat in Europe. - US-Japan Mutual Security Treaty – A defense pact ensuring U.S. military protection for Japan. - US-Korea Mutual Security Treaty – A similar agreement for South Korea’s security. - ANZUS (Australia, New Zealand, and the U.S.) – A defense alliance among these three Pacific nations. - SEATO (Southeast Asia Treaty Organization, 1954) – Modeled after NATO, this alliance included most of the Southeast Asian countries to counter communist expansion. - CENTO (Central Treaty Organization, 1955) – A defense alliance involving Arab states, designed to block Soviet influence in the Middle East. - Limitations of These Alliances - Despite their strategic intent, these alliances often proved impractical in execution. - Many member states had conflicting interests, limiting their effectiveness in addressing regional security challenges. - The Bandung Conference and the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM) - Growing Concerns of the 1950s - By the mid-1950s, the world was increasingly militarized and divided into two opposing camps. - A devastating war seemed imminent. - Poverty, inequality, and colonialism were being ignored. - Leaders of Indonesia, Ceylon (Sri Lanka), and the Philippines initiated a conference to address these concerns. - The Bandung Conference (1955) - Held in Bandung, Indonesia, with representatives from 29 African and Asian nations. - Aimed to promote cultural and economic cooperation and resist colonialism. - Prominent leaders: - Jawaharlal Nehru (India) - Gamal Abdel Nasser (Egypt) - Zhou Enlai (China) - Ho Chi Minh (Vietnam) - Kwame Nkrumah (Gold Coast, later Ghana) - Excluded countries: South Africa, Israel, Taiwan, South Korea, and North Korea. - Led to the formation of the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM) in 1961. - The Non-Aligned Movement (NAM) - Started with the goal of staying neutral during the Cold War. - Played a moral role against the arms race and military expansion of superpowers. - Passed resolutions condemning superpower actions. - Actively opposed apartheid in South Africa. - Challenges Faced by NAM - Lacked a permanent secretariat (permanent administrative office). - Was largely inactive between summit meetings. - Failed to prevent conflicts among its own member states, such as the Iran-Iraq War. - Could not act when the USSR invaded Afghanistan in 1979. - Many member states depended on superpowers for economic support. - NAM After the Cold War - After the Cold War ended, many questioned its relevance. - SEATO and CENTO had already dissolved, but some members wanted to retain NAM. - New concern: Unipolarism (US dominance as the world’s only superpower). - Despite reaffirming its goals, NAM failed to take action during the 2003 Iraq crisis. - Nuclear Arms Control and Disarmament - The Nuclear Test Ban Treaty (1963) - Signed by the US, USSR, and Britain. - Marked the beginning of détente (easing of tensions). - Banned nuclear tests in the atmosphere, underwater, and outer space. - Shift Towards Arms Control - By the 1960s, both superpowers realized that the arms race was endless. - Research and development ensured that one power would always catch up with the other. - They began signing treaties to limit research, testing, and deployment of nuclear weapons. - The Outer Space Treaty (1967) - Signed by the US, USSR, and 60 other countries. - Declared outer space a zone of peace. - The Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT) (1970) - Signed by the US, USSR, and 58 other nations. - Aimed to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons. - Key provisions: - US and USSR agreed not to supply nuclear weapons or technology to other countries. - Banned nuclear testing by countries not capable of building nuclear weapons. - India opposed the NPT, calling it discriminatory and insisting (stand firm) on universal disarmament. - Strategic Arms Limitation Treaty (SALT-I) (1972) - Signed on 26 May 1972. - Restricted the development of: - Anti-Ballistic Missiles (ABMs) - Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles (ICBMs) - Submarine-Launched Ballistic Missiles (SLBMs) - Introduced the Mutually Assured Destruction (MAD) doctrine, ensuring that neither power had an advantage. - SALT-II Talks (1979) - Initiated by US President Jimmy Carter and Soviet leader Leonid Brezhnev. - Aimed to further limit long-range nuclear missiles. - Talks collapsed after the USSR invaded Afghanistan in 1979. - NATO escalated tensions by announcing Dual Track deployment in Europe to counter Warsaw Pact missiles. - The Strategic Arms Reduction Treaty (START) (1982-1983) - Proposed by US President Ronald Reagan. - Introduced the Zero Option, aiming to eliminate an entire class of nuclear missiles. - Suggested reducing ICBMs and signing a verifiable arms control agreement. - Talks broke down in 1983 due to Reagan’s insistence on the Strategic Defense Initiative (SDI), or “Star Wars”. - SDI aimed to intercept enemy missiles from space, leading to fears of a new arms race in outer space. - Breakthrough with Mikhail Gorbachev (1985-1991) - Gorbachev revived START negotiations with new proposals. - At the Geneva Summit (1985), Reagan and Gorbachev agreed to reduce nuclear arms by 50%. - Nuclear Risk Reduction Centre Agreement (1987) improved communication and trust. - Treaty of Conventional Armed Forces in Europe (1990) reduced land army strength. - START Treaty signed on 31 July 1991, just five months before the USSR dissolved. - Post-Cold War Nuclear Disarmament - The Cold War ended, but the START Treaty remains in effect. - Russia inherited the USSR’s international obligations. - Both Russia and the US continue to uphold the treaty’s nuclear disarmament commitments. - Socio-Economic Impact of the Cold War - Global Economic Growth Amidst Uncertainty - The Cold War era saw rapid industrialization and global economic expansion. - However, constant regional conflicts created uncertainty, with fears of a potential World War III. - Economic Revival in the Western World - Superpowers used financial resources to gain the loyalty of allies. - This accelerated economic recovery in Western Europe. - After centuries of war, Western European nations realized the need for economic and social cooperation. - Early Steps Toward European Economic Unity - Winston Churchill (1943) proposed a Council of Europe and a “United States of Europe”. - 1948: The Organisation of European Economic Cooperation (OEEC) was established, doubling trade volumes within its first six years. - The International Trade Organisation and GATT (General Agreement on Trade and Tariffs) were formed to reduce import duties and promote free global trade. - Formation of the European Economic Community (EEC) (1957) - The most significant Cold War economic bloc. - Established by France, West Germany, Italy, Netherlands, Belgium, and Luxembourg under the Treaty of Rome. - Inspired by the European Coal and Steel Community (1951-56), which boosted European steel production by 50%. - EEC’s goals: - Remove customs duties and trade quotas. - Encourage free competition among member states. - Success of the EEC (by 1961): - Reflected in economic growth statistics. - Led Britain to join in 1973, despite initially staying away. - Struggles of the Communist Bloc - While Western Europe prospered, the Eastern Bloc economies struggled. - Khrushchev’s attempt to unify Eastern Bloc economies under COMECON failed due to internal resistance. - Though economic institutions remained weak, the Communist bloc had a strong record in social services. - Asia in ferment (state of unrest or agitation) - Asia’s Transformation in the 20th Century - The 21st century is often anticipated (expected) as the ‘Asian Century’, potentially bringing extraordinary progress for one-third of humanity. - However, in the 20th century, Asia started at a major disadvantage compared to Europe and the Americas. - The second half of the 20th century saw widespread turmoil (state of great distrubance) across Asia: - Violent revolutions - Mass movements - Social upheavals - Economic struggles followed by recovery in some cases - To understand this transformation, we must begin with the Chinese Communist Revolution, which serves as the starting point of Asia’s dramatic evolution. - China - The Chinese Communist Revolution and Mao’s Reforms - Establishment of Communist Rule (1949) - The Chinese Communist Party (CCP), led by Mao Zedong, gained control over mainland China in 1949 after a long and violent civil war. - At the time, China was devastated due to: - Years of civil war - Japanese occupation - Severe food shortages - Underdeveloped agricultural infrastructure - Minimal industrial base - Despite extreme poverty, the people placed their faith in Mao and the Communists. - Mao adopted Marxist ideology, blending it with Chinese traditions, tailoring reforms to China’s unique challenges. - A Peasant-Led Revolution - The Chinese Communist Revolution was the world’s first large-scale peasant revolution, unlike the Russian Revolution, which was led by industrial workers. - Mao’s strategy was to build a coalition of: - Peasants - Workers - Petty bourgeoisie - National bourgeoisie (small business owners and industrialists) - Mao initially allowed businessmen to retain their properties if they supported the revolution, but by 1956, the state took over all private lands and businesses. - Industrialisation and Economic Growth - Industrialisation was given high priority: - Oil production made China one of the world’s largest producers by 1977. - China ranked third in coal production and led in various minerals. - Biogas plants were set up to produce methane gas and fertiliser from waste. - By 1960, China’s steel production rivaled France, and power generation increased tenfold. - Irrigated land doubled (1949–1960). - Large-scale afforestation projects were launched. - The railway network was expanded, enabling industrial dispersal beyond Manchuria and the East. - Small-scale industries thrived, aided by Soviet assistance in: - Precision tools - Electronics - Daily-use goods (scissors, pens, toys, surgical instruments, switches, etc.) - ‘Made in China’ products gained credibility in international markets due to their high quality and low cost. - Land Reforms and Collectivisation - Land reforms began by confiscating (seize) large landholdings from landlords. - Cooperative farming was introduced, where multiple villages pooled resources. - By 1957, more than 90% of agricultural land was collectivised. - In the next phase, state-controlled collective farms replaced cooperatives, ensuring direct Communist Party oversight. - The ‘Great Leap Forward’ (1959) and Its Failure - Mao launched the ‘Great Leap Forward’ to accelerate industrialisation and agricultural production. - Key measures: - Rural cooperatives were merged into large communes, symbolizing state ownership over local control. - A mobile labor force was created to support industrial projects. - Results: - The plan failed catastrophically (extremely unsuccessful) due to incompetent Communist Party officials managing it. - Food shortages worsened, and industrial production collapsed. - In 1962, the strategy was revised: - Agriculture was prioritized. - Communes were downsized. - Farmers were allowed to cultivate small private plots. - The Great Chinese Famine - The Promise vs. Reality - Mao’s ‘Great Leap Forward’ was launched with the slogan: - “Three years of hard work and suffering, and a thousand years of prosperity.” - However, instead of prosperity, China faced an unprecedented (never seen before) famine. - Initially, everything appeared successful as: - Communist Party units reported exaggerated (overstated) agricultural output. - Encouraged by false reports, the government set even higher production targets. - Technical Failures and Agricultural Mismanagement - The primary reasons for failure were flawed agricultural techniques: - Seeds were sown at ten times the normal density, suffocating young plants. - Wheat and maize were grown on the same fields, which proved ineffective. - Mass extermination (killing) of sparrows (to prevent seed loss) led to uncontrolled pest infestations (the presence of an unusually large number of insects). - Poorly planned irrigation projects caused massive soil erosion. - Focus on a few select crops resulted in neglect of essential food crops and vegetables. - Official Denial and Propaganda - Chinese leaders believed in false production figures, leading to severe miscalculations. - Despite famine, the Party continued propaganda, creating misleading slogans: - In 1959, when people were starving and boiling grass for food, they were advised to: - “Live frugally (costing little) in a year of plenty.” - The state-run radio glorified starvation, urging citizens to: - “Take naps” since they were too weak to work. - Doctors were instructed to convince people that Chinese physiology required neither proteins nor fats. - Human Cost and Economic Collapse - The 1960 harvest was 26% lower than 1957 levels. - By 1960, food production had fallen to 1950 levels, but China’s population had increased by over 100 million. - Consequences: - Rice prices skyrocketed—rising 30 times in some provinces. - Coal shortages disrupted emergency rail transport of food. - Miners abandoned work to search for food, worsening coal production. - Mortality rate surged (sudden upward movement) from 15% to 68% in 1960. - Birth rate plummeted (fall or drop down at high rate) from 30% to 11%. - The famine remained largely unknown to the outside world as China tightly controlled information. - The Great Chinese Famine stands as one of the deadliest man-made disasters in history, caused by a combination of faulty policies, misinformation, and rigid state control. - The Cultural Revolution - Background and Mao’s Motivation - The failure of the ‘Great Leap Forward’ weakened Mao’s influence. - To reassert his control, he launched the Cultural Revolution, one of the darkest periods in Chinese communism. - Mao, a hardline communist, feared that: - Party officials were becoming complacent (self-satisfied). - Communist ideology was losing ground. - Resentment (bitterness) was growing due to the failures of the ‘Great Leap Forward’. - Right-wing party leaders like Deng Xiaoping and Liu Shaoqi suggested reforms: - Higher wages for quality labor. - Allowing larger private holdings. - Guiding the revolution with expert managerial oversight rather than party cadres (small group). - Mao rejected these ideas, considering them a betrayal (disloyalty) of Marxist-Leninist principles. - The Launch of the Cultural Revolution - In 1966, Mao announced the ‘Proletarian (working class / common) Cultural Revolution’, aiming to mobilize the youth to “save” the revolution. - The Red Guards, composed mostly of students, became his strongest supporters. - They traveled across China, carrying Mao’s ‘Little Red Book’, and: - Led massive propaganda campaigns to reinforce communist ideology. - Denounced (criticize) and physically attacked ‘counter-revolutionaries’. - Targeted teachers, intellectuals, professionals, and local party officials. - However, the movement soon spiraled out of control, leading to widespread chaos and violence. - Anarchic (lawless) Totalitarianism (1966-1976) - The Death Toll and Lingering Famine Effects - Unlike the Great Leap Forward, where millions died from famine, the Cultural Revolution resulted in: - 400,000 to 1 million deaths, according to general estimates. - Some scholars, like Domenach, estimate 1 million to 3 million deaths. - The effects of the Great Leap Forward famine were still being felt, making it difficult to determine which policy caused more devastation. - The Red Guards’ Reign of Terror - By 1967, daily executions were widespread. - Mao encouraged the youth to embark on their own “Long March”, claiming they would “learn revolution by making revolution”. - Schools shut down as students joined the Red Guards, often to save themselves from persecution. - The Red Guards turned against their own families and communities: - Teenagers denounced their parents, teachers, and relatives. - Ancient monuments were destroyed, private homes raided, and random killings became common. - The movement developed its own armed investigators, parallel police forces, and judiciary, complete with judges and prisons. - Chaos and Civil War Threat - The Cultural Revolution soon spiraled into anarchy, pushing China to the brink of civil war. - Strikes and protests erupted against the Cultural Revolution. - China became isolated internationally, with even the communist bloc distancing itself. - Mao reversed his stance and called upon the People’s Liberation Army (PLA) to suppress the Red Guards. - Lin Biao, a key architect of the Cultural Revolution, was removed from power and died in a mysterious air crash in 1971. - The Second Phase of the Cultural Revolution - The Red Guards turned against each other. - Students attacked workers. - Workers targeted day laborers. - Industrial production collapsed, and civil society disappeared, replaced by violence, slogans, and political campaigns. - Mao continued purging his close allies, even ordering the mysterious execution of Lin Biao, whom he had once named as his successor. - Disillusionment (feeling of disappointment knowing something is not as good as expected) grew, and when Mao died in 1976, the nation heaved a sigh of relief. - The ‘Gang of Four’, led by Mao’s wife Jiang Qing, was jailed, reflecting the public’s deep resentment (bitterness) toward Mao’s rule. - The US-China Rapprochement (incresed understanding) (1972) - Nixon’s Historic Visit to China - In February 1972, the world was shocked to learn that US President Richard Nixon would visit Beijing. - This was unprecedented (unparalleled / unmatched), as US-China relations had been severed since the Korean War. - ‘Ping Pong’ Diplomacy: The Secret US-China Talks - ‘Ping Pong’ diplomacy was the term for clandestine (secret) meetings between US and Chinese officials from the late 1960s to early 1970s. - Since the US had not formally recognized Communist China, it could not engage with its officials openly. - Meanwhile, China’s relations with the USSR had deteriorated due to: - Ideological differences. - Leadership disputes over the world communist movement. - Border conflicts between China and the USSR. - The US sought to exploit this Sino-Soviet rift to counter Soviet influence. - Pakistan, an ally of both the US and China, acted as a facilitator in these secret negotiations. - Outcomes of Nixon’s Visit - The visit led to the US officially recognizing the People’s Republic of China (PRC) as the legitimate (legal) government of China. - Taiwan was sidelined: - It lost its seat in the UN Security Council to Communist China. - However, it continued to receive American aid. - The visit paved the way for formal US-China diplomatic relations, reshaping global geopolitics. - Deng Xiaoping’s Rise and Reforms - After Mao’s death, a power struggle followed. - Deng Xiaoping emerged victorious in 1979. - He had been removed during the Cultural Revolution for being “too liberal.” - Once in power, he reversed many policies of that era. - Political and Social Reforms - Revolutionary committees were replaced with democratically elected groups. - Confiscated (seized) property was returned to survivors of original owners. - Religious freedom was restored. - Intellectuals were given more freedom of expression. - Economic Reforms and Modernization - Deng and Hu Yaobang sought Western investment. - China joined the World Bank and IMF in 1980. - State farms gained more control over administration and planning. - Profit-sharing and bonuses were encouraged. - Tax reductions boosted agricultural output. - Grain production increased, improving nutrition levels. - Impact - Deng’s message: “To get rich is not a crime.” - The Party reduced its role in the economy. - By the late 20th century, per capita income rose to $1,200. - China transformed into a modern industrial state. - Democracy Wall Movement and Deng’s Response - November 1978: People in Beijing and other cities began protesting for more reforms. - The government banned marches, leading people to create “democracy walls.” - Anonymous posters and graffiti (writings or drawings scribbled) demanded human rights and criticized Mao. - Deng’s Reaction - Deng, a party loyalist, opposed uncontrolled dissent. - He believed China would fall into chaos without the Party. - Democracy walls were banned, and leaders of the movement were arrested and sentenced. - However, Mao-era terror did not return under Deng. - Tiananmen Square Protests and Government Crackdown - Party Hardliners vs. Reformists: - Deng faced pressure from hardliners like Li Peng, who wanted his reforms to fail. - He promoted liberal leaders like Zhao Ziyang and Hu Yaobang but kept criticism within limits. - Economic Issues (1988-89): - Inflation rose, and wages lagged behind prices. - Students in Beijing began peaceful protests in Tiananmen Square (May 1989). - They demanded political reforms, democracy, and an end to corruption. - The Protest and Government Response: - Zhao assured them their demands would be addressed. - The demonstrations were festive, with music, flags, and a ‘Democracy’ statue. - The world press covered the events. - The Crackdown (June 3-4, 1989): - The government saw it as a threat to Communist rule. - Deng turned hardliner, replaced Zhao with Li Peng, and sent in the Army. - Tanks rolled in, and 1,500–3,000 people were killed. - The world was outraged (extremely strong reaction of anger), but China refused to repent (feel remorse or guilt). - Deng justified the massacre, claiming one-party control was necessary for a ‘socialist market economy.’ - Tibet and Sino-Indian Relations - Tibet’s Strategic Importance: - Tibet, a high-altitude region, was strategically important for British India. - China had ruled Tibet since the 18th century, while the British secretly mapped the region. - In 1911, China granted Tibet independence. - The Dalai Lama became both spiritual and political head. - China’s Takeover of Tibet (1951): - After British India’s exit, diplomatic ties between New Delhi and Lhasa continued. - In 1951, China sponsored a communist uprising and seized Tibet. - The Dalai Lama retained nominal control, but Buddhism was suppressed, causing tension. - Tibetan Revolt and Dalai Lama’s Exile (expulsion) (1956-1959): - Ethnic Tibetans in China revolted in 1956 against the persecution of religious leaders. - The movement spread to Tibet, and in 1959, China crushed it with brute force. - Hundreds were massacred, and sacred monuments were destroyed. - Facing persecution, Dalai Lama fled to India with 100,000 followers. - Nehru granted them refuge in Dalhousie, Himachal Pradesh. - Sino-Indian Conflict (1962): - China refused to recognise British-drawn borders. - Though Nehru and Zhou Enlai agreed on Panchsheel (Five Principles), China’s People’s Liberation Army (PLA) frequently intruded into Indian territory. - In November 1962, China launched a full-scale invasion. - The ill-equipped Indian Army struggled, and China occupied large territories. - Global condemnation followed, and the US provided military aid to India. - China withdrew partially but retained over 50,000 sq. km of Indian land. - In 1963, Pakistan illegally transferred part of Kashmir to China. - Diplomatic Standoff and Normalization (1962-1988): - India downgraded diplomatic ties with China from 1962 to 1979. - In 1979, Atal Bihari Vajpayee visited Beijing, initiating dialogue (conversation). - In 1988, PM Rajiv Gandhi visited China, restarting the normalization process. - A Joint Working Group was formed to resolve the border dispute, but no progress was made. - Present-Day Sino-Indian Relations: - Trade relations have grown significantly. - However, China is still seen as supporting Pakistan’s hostile (aggressive) actions. - China refuses to acknowledge Sikkim as part of India. - Vietnam’s Struggles and Wars (Post-World War II) - War Against French Colonial Rule (1946-1954) - Vietnam, the largest country in former French Indo-China, went through three major wars after World War II. - After Japan’s surrender (1945), the French attempted to reclaim Vietnam. - Ho Chi Minh’s Viet Minh (League for the Independence of Vietnam) had gained military experience fighting the Japanese. - With the Japanese gone, Ho established the Democratic Republic of Vietnam. - However, non-communist nationalists and religious leaders opposed his rule. - In 1946, the French launched an attack to reclaim Vietnam, installing a puppet regime in 1948. - Battle of Dien Bien Phu (1954): - The French were completely defeated. - Geneva Conference (1954): Vietnam was divided into communist North Vietnam and non-communist South Vietnam. - The Vietnam War (1955-1975) - - Initially, the US urged France to negotiate with Ho Chi Minh and nationalist leader Diem Ngo Dinh. - But the rise of Communist China and the Korean War changed US policy. - President Eisenhower backed France to prevent Ho’s communist rule in Vietnam. - After the French withdrew, the US supported the Diem regime in South Vietnam. - Escalation of Conflict - From 1959, tensions between North and South Vietnam escalated. - Diem’s Unpopularity: - He persecuted (oppress / abused) his political rivals, leading some to resort to guerrilla warfare. - Another perspective suggests that North Vietnam sponsored the insurgency (active revolt or uprising). - A full-scale war erupted: - North Vietnam was backed by USSR and China. - US openly supported South Vietnam. - US Involvement and Defeat - Initially, the US sent military advisers to assist South Vietnam. - By 1963, US troops landed in Vietnam, and by 1969, over 500,000 American soldiers were involved. - Guerrilla Warfare Challenges: - Despite technological superiority, the US could not match North Vietnam’s fighting skills. - The Vietnamese used jungle warfare, drawing Americans into unfamiliar terrain. - War Crimes and Public Backlash: - US troops committed massacres, making the war unpopular. - Example: My Lai Massacre (1967) – innocent civilians were slaughtered. - American public opinion turned against the war. - End of the Vietnam War - President Nixon (1971) decided the US could not achieve victory. - The US had lost over 50,000 soldiers, and public opposition was strong. - Paris Peace Talks led to a ceasefire agreement (January 1973). - US troops withdrew by March 1973, but North-South fighting continued. - In 1975, South Vietnam was defeated, and Vietnam was reunified. - Post-War Conflicts (1975-1989) - War with Cambodia (1978-1989) - Cambodia was ruled by the Khmer Rouge (Communist regime) after a bloody civil war (1975). - Khmer Rouge massacred over 1 million Cambodians in forced labor camps. - Border clashes with Vietnam led to a Vietnamese invasion (1978). - The Vietnamese Army occupied much of Cambodia, forcing Khmer Rouge into the jungles near Thailand. - Conflict with China (1979) - China supported Khmer Rouge leader Pol Pot. - In 1979, China invaded Vietnam, but Vietnamese troops repelled the attack. - China retreated but continued supporting Pol Pot. - Vietnam’s Withdrawal from Cambodia (1989) - Under UN intervention, Vietnam withdrew from Cambodia in 1989. - Cambodia transitioned to democracy, and Vietnam focused on economic rebuilding. - The Partition and War in Korea - - Partition of Korea (Post-World War II) - Korea was under Japanese rule from 1910 to 1945. - After World War II, the country was partitioned into communist North Korea and non-communist South Korea. - At the time of Japan’s surrender, - The US occupied the South (below the 38th Parallel). - The Soviet Army controlled the North. - The US backed an anti-communist government under Syngman Rhee in South Korea. - The Soviets supported a communist regime under Kim Il Sung in North Korea. - Rising Tensions and the Outbreak of War (1950-1953) - 1948: The Soviets withdrew from North Korea. - 1949: The Americans left South Korea. - However, hostility remained, with: - Large-scale refugee crossings. - Armed incursions from both sides. - 25 June 1950: North Korea invaded South Korea, sparking a three-year war. - The Cold War Context and US Involvement - The Cold War between the US and the USSR had begun in Europe. - February 1950: The Sino-Soviet Alliance was signed. - It remains uncertain whether North Korea was encouraged by China or the USSR to start the war. - The US saw the conflict as part of a broader communist expansion. - America intervened, fearing that Soviet and Chinese influence would spread across Asia. - US troops, backed by the UN, were sent from Japan to Korea. - Major Phases of the Korean War - The war saw multiple shifts in control: - UN Commander General Douglas MacArthur led an amphibious landing at Inchon, pushing North Korean forces beyond the 38th Parallel. - UN forces crossed the Yalu River, nearing China’s border. - China retaliated, sending troops to aid North Korea, pushing UN forces back. - Three years of stalemate followed. - 27 July 1953: An armistice was signed, but no peace treaty was ever concluded. - Post-War Korea: A Divided Nation - The Korean border remains closed. - Relations between North and South Korea remain tense even after six decades. - The issue of Korean unification remains unresolved. - Post-War Reconstruction and Economic Growth in South Korea - Challenges After the Korean War - South Korea faced major obstacles in rebuilding its society and economy after the war. - While two-thirds of the peninsula’s population resided in the South, most industrial infrastructure was located in the North. - With almost no industrial base, South Korea had to start from scratch, focusing on: - Power plants - Agriculture - Basic industries - US economic assistance in the 1950s was crucial in helping South Korea feed its population and rebuild its economy. - Economic Development and the Role of the State - South Korea embraced private ownership and capitalism. - Since the 1960s, a government-business partnership coordinated a successful growth strategy based on: - Foreign investment - Export-driven industries - Sustained economic planning - Per capita income rose dramatically from around $100 in the late 1950s to nearly $2,000. - The rapid economic transformation was not a mere “miracle”, but the result of: - Effective government planning - Strategic use of foreign capital - A disciplined and highly educated workforce - Strong collaboration between political leaders and private entrepreneurs - Social and Economic Inequality - Despite economic growth, income distribution remained uneven. - Workers’ wages did not keep pace with the economy’s rapid expansion. - The government restricted labor unions and strikes, limiting workers’ ability to demand better conditions. - Many believed that only certain social groups benefited from the economic boom. - Even the new urban middle class, which gained from economic success, began demanding greater political freedoms. - Shifting Priorities: Social and Political Changes - As South Korea moved beyond rapid industrialization, new challenges emerged: - Equity and income distribution - Increased social spending - Economic liberalization (reducing government controls and fostering free markets) - Democratic reforms to create a more open political system - The government had to shift from a “growth at all costs” mindset to a more balanced approach, addressing both economic and social concerns. - Cambodia’s (Kampuchea) Path to Turmoil: From Independence to Civil War - Independence and Neutrality (1953–1969) - Cambodia gained independence from French Indo-China along with Vietnam in 1953. - Under King Norodom Sihanouk, Cambodia maintained neutrality during regional conflicts in the 1950s. - The rise of communism in Cambodia was seen as a threat by the US, which was stationed in neighboring South Vietnam. - Tensions with the US increased when South Vietnamese forces pursued Vietnamese communist guerrillas into Cambodian territory. - In response, Cambodia severed ties with the US. - However, when evidence emerged that Ho Chi Minh was secretly supporting Cambodian communists, Sihanouk restored relations with the US in 1969. - Political Upheaval and Civil War (1970–1975) - In 1970, pro-American Premier Lon Nol overthrew King Sihanouk and abolished the monarchy. - Lon Nol demanded that North Vietnam withdraw its 40,000 soldiers who had established bases in Cambodia. - Sihanouk fled to China, where he later aligned with the communist Khmer Rouge. - A civil war broke out between: - The Vietnam-backed Khmer Rouge (communist insurgents) - The US-backed government of Lon Nol - Khmer Rouge Rule and Mass Atrocities (1975–1979) - In April 1975, the Khmer Rouge captured Phnom Penh, Cambodia’s capital. - Pol Pot, the group’s leader, enforced a radical vision of agrarian communism, believing cities and their inhabitants were undesirable. - Hundreds of thousands of city dwellers were forcibly relocated to rural areas for: - Agriculture - Forest clearing - Hard labor - The country’s name was changed to Kampuchea. - Mass atrocities followed, leading to an estimated two million deaths over the next four years due to: - Execution - Forced labor - Starvation and disease - The Khmer Rouge turned the entire country into a minefield to prevent refugees from escaping into camps set up in Thailand. - Vietnamese Invasion and Continued Conflict (1978–1980s) - In 1978, border conflicts with Vietnam escalated into a full-scale invasion in January 1979. - The Vietnamese installed a Hanoi-backed government in Kampuchea. - However, fearing that one communist regime had simply replaced another, thousands of Cambodians fled to Thailand. - Starvation and hardship were widespread across the country. - The Khmer Rouge retreated into the western jungles, using guerrilla warfare to continue their resistance. - Post-war shakeup in Africa and Asia - Post-Independence Challenges in Asia and Africa - Independence and Its Challenges - As discussed in a previous chapter, many countries in Asia and Africa gained independence after World War II. - However, independence was just the beginning; the real challenge lay in: - Fulfilling the needs and aspirations of the people - Building a stable civil society - Ensuring peace and long-term stability - Africa’s Struggles After Liberation - The leaders of newly independent African nations faced overwhelming (have a strong emotional effect on) problems post-liberation. - Political, economic, and social challenges made nation-building a difficult task. - This chapter will explore the early struggles of some African countries that have remained in the news in recent decades. - The Emerging Republics of Central Asia - After the collapse of the Soviet Union, several new republics emerged in Central Asia. - These nations face immense challenges in governance, economy, and security. - Their strategic location near India makes their stability crucial for the broader region. - The developments in Central Asia hold significant implications (the conclusion that can be drawn from something although it's not explicitly stated) for global security. - African scenerio - Challenges Faced by Newly Independent African Nations - Struggles of Newly Freed African Nations - Most newly independent African nations faced severe handicaps from the start. - These countries were ethnically diverse, composed of multiple tribes that had been forcibly united under colonial rule. - During their freedom struggles, a fragile sense of unity existed, but once the colonial powers left, deep divisions resurfaced. - Many people struggled with dual loyalties—whether to their tribe or the nation. - In several nations like Nigeria, Congo (Zaire), Burundi, and Rwanda, tribal rivalries escalated into full-scale civil wars. - Artificial Boundaries and Economic Struggles - Most modern African nations were not natural entities; rather, they were created due to the colonial partition of the continent. - After independence, many of these countries found their economies heavily reliant on a single export product, making them vulnerable to global market fluctuations. - Nigeria depended almost entirely on oil exports. - Ghana relied on cocoa. - Kenya’s economy was centered around tea exports. - The lack of economic diversity and an educated workforce left many nations struggling to build self-sufficient economies. - The Impact of Neo-Colonialism - Even after independence, former imperial powers continued to exert influence over these nations, leading to a form of neo-colonialism. - Many African governments remained economically and politically dependent on their former rulers. - Sovereignty was often compromised, as foreign nations intervened or withheld support based on their approval or disapproval of African governments. - Political and Economic Transitions in Ghana - The Nkrumah Era (1957–1966) - Ghana, formerly the Gold Coast, became independent in 1957 with Kwame Nkrumah as its first President. - Under his socialist government, Ghana’s population enjoyed better living conditions than many other African nations. - He expanded the cocoa industry, Ghana’s principal export crop, and sought to diversify the economy by investing in: - Forestry - Fishing - Cattle-breeding - Gold and bauxite mining - Nkrumah was a strong advocate of Pan-Africanism and worked towards African economic unions with Guinea and Mali. - He also played a key role in the formation of the Organisation of African Unity (OAU) in 1963. - Shift to Authoritarianism and Military Coup (1964–1966) - Nkrumah’s administration became increasingly authoritarian due to over-centralisation of power. - He abandoned the Parliamentary system and established one-party rule, leading to: - Suppression of political opponents - Harsh laws, including a 1959 order that allowed opponents to be expelled or jailed for up to five years without trial - A personality cult built around himself - These moves angered the military, and while Nkrumah was on a tour of China in 1966, the military staged a coup. - The new military rulers promised democratic reforms and drafted a new Constitution by 1969. - Kofi Busia, leader of the Progressive Party, was elected Prime Minister. - Economic Decline and Military Interventions (1969–1981) - Busia, an Oxford scholar, struggled to reassert civilian control over the military-dominated system. - Under his leadership: - The economy deteriorated due to a debt crisis, forcing currency devaluation. - Tribal tensions resurfaced, further weakening national stability. - In 1972, the military staged another coup, replacing him with Colonel Ignatius Acheampong. - However, Acheampong’s government was corrupt and ineffective, failing to address the economic crisis. - In July 1978, the military removed Acheampong on corruption charges and installed General Fred Akuffo. - A year later, in June 1979, Air Force officer Jerry Rawlings led a coup, promising to eliminate corruption and restore democracy. - In July 1979, elections were held, and Hilla Limann became President. - The Rawlings Era and Path to Democracy (1981–1991) - Economic hardships continued, with unemployment and industrial strikes destabilizing the country. - In 1981, Rawlings staged another coup, removing Limann and establishing the Provisional National Defence Council (PNDC). - Unlike previous military rulers, Rawlings did not seek complete military control but wanted the army to have influence in national decision-making. - He appointed a civilian government under the PNDC, consisting of renowned leaders and academics. - A comprehensive economic recovery program was launched, leading to growth and stability. - Over time, Ghana transitioned towards full democracy, culminating in the 1991 elections, marking the end of military rule. - Nigeria: Political Instability and the ‘Coup Culture’ - Oil Wealth and Political Instability - Nigeria possesses huge oil reserves, giving it a significant economic advantage over its African neighbors. - However, the country has been plagued (sickness) by political instability, marked by a persistent ‘Coup Culture’, where military factions (small organized dessenting group) repeatedly overthrow democratic governments. - The military leaders who seize power often become victims of new conspiracies, leading to a cycle of instability. - Early Years and the 1966 Coup - From 1960 to 1966, Nigeria remained stable under Prime Minister Abubakar Tafawa Balewa. - However, in 1966, a military coup overthrew Balewa’s government, leading to a civil war caused by tribal conflicts: - In the north, Muslims competed for power with the Hausa and Fulani tribes. - In the west, the Yorubas held grievances (unjustice). - In the east, the Ibos felt marginalized (treated as insignificant) by the dominant northern region, from which Balewa hailed (call out to attract attention). - Ibo officers in the Army staged a coup, assassinating Balewa and several politicians, which triggered ethnic violence. - General Ironsi, the new leader, was soon killed by northern soldiers, who then installed Colonel Yakubu Gowon as the head of state. - This led to the mass migration of Ibos to the east, where in 1967, under Colonel Ojukwu, they declared independence and formed the Republic of Biafra. - The Nigerian Civil War (1967–1970) - A brutal civil war erupted between Lagos (the federal government) and Biafra. - The war lasted for three years, causing mass starvation and heavy casualties, especially in the east. - Neither the UN nor the Commonwealth could mediate peace until the final Biafran resistance collapsed (crumbled). - After the war, Gowon restructured Nigeria by introducing a federal system with 12 states (later expanded to 19) to give better representation to all tribes. - In the mid-1970s, the oil boom temporarily improved Nigeria’s economy, though political instability persisted (carried on). - Military Coups and Civilian Rule (1975–1983) - In 1975, the Army overthrew Gowon, unhappy with his slow return to civilian rule. - Under international pressure, elections were held in 1979, bringing President Shagari to power under a civilian government. - However, Nigeria’s economy remained heavily dependent on oil, and when global oil prices collapsed in the 1980s, the country faced an economic crisis. - Military Takeovers and Economic Collapse (1983–1993) - In August 1983, Shagari was overthrown in a coup led by Major General Muhammadu Buhari, who became President. - However, Buhari himself was ousted by Major General Ibrahim Babangida, who attempted economic diversification but struggled against falling oil prices. - In 1986, Nigeria was forced to accept a World Bank loan for the first time to sustain its economy. - Babangida initiated steps toward democratic governance, but in 1993, another military coup derailed progress. - The Sani Abacha Regime and International Isolation (1993–1998) - In 1993, General Sani Abacha staged a bloodless coup and established a highly authoritarian rule. - Under his regime: - Political opponents and intellectuals were imprisoned or executed. - Ken Saro-Wiwa, a famous novelist and activist, was hanged on flimsy charges, drawing global condemnation. - International sanctions were imposed, isolating Nigeria and blocking oil exports. - Sharia law was enforced in the north, leading to widespread human rights violations. - Once a nation with the potential to rival South Africa as an economic power, Nigeria declined into an international pariah (outcast) state. - Angola: Civil War and Struggle for Power - Independence and Internal Conflict (1975) - Angola gained independence from Portuguese rule in 1975, but a bitter civil war erupted immediately. - The conflict arose because there were three separate liberation movements, each fighting for control of the country after the Portuguese withdrew. - Key Factions in the Civil War - Popular Movement for the Liberation of Angola (MPLA): - A left-wing movement that took control under Agostinho Neto. - Had a communist ideology (the set of beliefs / system of ideas), which alarmed Western nations. - National Union for the Total Independence of Angola (UNITA): - Led by Jonas Savimbi and supported by the Ovimbundu tribe in the south. - Backed by the United States and Mobutu’s Zaire with arms and ammunition. - National Front for the Liberation of Angola (FNLA): - Weaker than MPLA and UNITA, but had some support, particularly from the Bakongo tribe in the northwest. - Foreign Involvement and Escalation - Western concerns over the communist nature of the MPLA led to foreign interventions: - United States and Zaire supported UNITA. - Cuba backed MPLA with military aid. - South Africa, opposing MPLA’s support for Namibia’s SWAPO, invaded from the south. - Zairean forces attacked from the northeast, further complicating the war. - Decade-Long Civil War and UN Intervention - The civil war lasted over a decade, with heavy casualties and widespread destruction. - In December 1988, the UN brokered a peace agreement: - South Africa agreed to grant Namibia independence. - Cuban troops withdrew from Angola. - In 1990, under Sam Nujoma, Namibia became a democratic republic. - Elections in 1992, held under UN supervision, resulted in a victory for Nujoma’s MPLA. - UNITA’s Final Resistance and Resolution (1992–1993) - By 1992, global communism was collapsing, and Soviet support to MPLA ended. - This encouraged UNITA to make another attempt to seize power. - Jonas Savimbi rejected the election results, leading to six months of renewed fighting. - In 1993, under international pressure, including US recognition of the MPLA government, UNITA was forced back to negotiations. - UNITA accepted the election results and agreed to join a coalition government with MPLA, bringing an end to the conflict. - Burundi: Ethnic Conflict and Political Turmoil - Historical Tensions and Ethnic Divide (1962–1988) - Burundi and Rwanda, both former Belgian colonies, experienced ethnic tensions from the moment they gained independence in 1962. - The Tutsi minority controlled power in Burundi, which was deeply resented by the Hutu majority. - In 1988, tensions escalated when Hutu soldiers in the Burundi Army revolted, massacring thousands of Tutsis. - Democratic Elections and Renewed Violence (1993–1996) - In 1993, Burundi held its first democratic elections, in which the Hutus won. - The Tutsis, unwilling to accept Hutu rule, assassinated the newly elected President, triggering a cycle of killings and reprisal attacks. - The country plunged into complete chaos, with fighting continuing until 1996. - International Peace Efforts and Military Takeover (1996) - Alarmed by the endless bloodshed, the Organisation of African Unity (OAU) deployed a peacekeeping force in 1996. - A power-sharing government was established: - The Prime Minister was a Tutsi. - The President was a Hutu. - However, even the peacekeeping forces failed to restore stability. - Ultimately, the Burundi Army dismissed the government and seized power under Major Pierre Buyoya, a Tutsi. - Rwanda: The Tutsi-Hutu Conflict and Genocide - Ethnic Rivalry and Civil War (1990–1993) - Rwanda, like Burundi, was plagued by intense Tutsi-Hutu rivalry, leading to frequent genocidal conflicts. - In 1990, war erupted between the Tutsi-dominated Rwandese Patriotic Front (RPF) and the Hutu-controlled Rwandan Army. -
