The UN deployed a peacekeeping force while international mediation worked to end the civil war. - A temporary peace was achieved in October 1993. - Assassination of Leaders and Massacre (1994) - In early 1994, an unusual assassination occurred: - The Presidents of Rwanda and Burundi (both Hutus) attended a meeting in Tanzania. - While returning to Kigali, their aircraft was hit by a missile, killing all on board. - This triggered one of the worst genocides in African history: - Thousands of Tutsi men, women, and children were massacred. - Though a UN peacekeeping force was present, it failed to stop the killings. - The Aftermath: Mass Killings and Refugee Crisis - Over half a million Tutsis were murdered in just a few months. - More than a million refugees fled to Zaire and Tanzania. - The endless cycle of violence continued despite repeated UN peace missions and efforts by moderate Hutus. - The Turbulent History of the Democratic Republic of Congo (Zaire) - Independence and the Katanga Secession (withdrawal) (1960–1962) - In 1960, Belgium granted independence to Congo, leaving power in the hands of a coalition government. - However, conflicts erupted: - The Army, still controlled by Belgian officers, rebelled. - The rich copper province of Katanga, backed by the Belgian mining company Union Minière, attempted to secede (withdraw from) under Moise Tshombe. - The UN intervened, but its Secretary-General, Dag Hammarskjöld, was suspected of favoring Katanga’s independence, yielding to Belgian pressure. - President Patrice Lumumba sought UN support, but when the UN failed to stop Katanga’s separatism, he turned to Moscow for help, drawing American opposition. - With US backing, President Joseph Kasavubu had Lumumba imprisoned and executed. - After Hammarskjöld’s death, his successor, U Thant, reversed UN policy. In September 1962, UN forces ended Katanga’s secession and reunified the country. - Military Rule and Economic Decline (1965–1990) - Tribal conflicts continued to destabilize the nation. - In 1965, General Joseph Mobutu seized power using foreign mercenaries (private individual serving in armed conflict for personal gains) and established an authoritarian regime, bringing stability. - Congo was renamed Zaire. - In the late 1970s, Zaire faced: - An invasion by Angolan troops, seizing Katanga (renamed Shaba). - International intervention repelled the invasion. - Economic troubles due to the declining world copper prices, making food imports expensive. - Mobutu’s rule became increasingly repressive, and under international pressure, he lifted the ban on multiparty politics in 1990. - The Rwandan Refugee Crisis and the Fall of Mobutu (1994–1997) - In 1994, after the ethnic massacres in Rwanda, hundreds of thousands of Tutsi refugees crossed into eastern Zaire. - The Zairean Army intervened, forcing refugees back into Rwanda, where they faced Hutu reprisals. - Meanwhile, Mobutu’s health deteriorated, and he spent long periods in European hospitals. - In 1996, General Laurent Kabila, backed by Rwanda, led a rebellion from eastern Zaire. - With weak resistance from the Zairean Army, Kabila advanced westward. - In May 1997, Kabila’s forces entered Kinshasa, forcing Mobutu into exile. - The country was renamed the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). - Continued Instability and the Rise of Joseph Kabila (1998–2001) - Kabila failed to restore stability. - In August 1998, rebels backed by Uganda and Rwanda launched an uprising, raiding Kinshasa. - Kabila received military aid from Angola, Namibia, and Zimbabwe, pushing back the rebels. - A ceasefire was declared in August 1999. - In 2001, Kabila was assassinated by his own bodyguard and succeeded by his son, Joseph Kabila. - The Republic of Congo (Congo-Brazzaville) - Another country, the Republic of Congo (Congo-Brazzaville), lies in western Africa. - It gained independence from France in 1960. - Tanzania: A Model of Stability and Visionary Leadership - A Nation United by Swahili - Despite being one of Africa’s poorest countries, Tanzania did not suffer from tribalism. - The Swahili language served as a common bond, fostering national unity. - Nyerere’s Visionary Leadership (1960s–1985) - Julius Kambarage Nyerere, Tanzania’s first President, promoted socialist principles through his famous ‘Arusha Declaration’. - Key aspects of the Arusha Declaration: - The state should control all means of production. - It aimed to prevent the exploitation of the poor by the rich. - Emphasis on strengthening agriculture as the country’s economic backbone. - Despite single-party rule, genuine democracy existed under his leadership. - He offered an alternative model of growth and governance for developing nations, unlike other African countries that followed Western models, which led to: - Civil wars - Ethnic conflicts - Massive inequality between the rich and poor - Role in African Politics - Nyerere was an outspoken (forthright) critic (person with unfavourable opinion) of the global economic system, which he argued trapped nations in poverty. - He played a crucial role in: - Overthrowing General Idi Amin’s regime in Uganda. - Strengthening the Organisation of African Unity (OAU). - He retired in 1986 but remained an influential voice in African affairs. - Transition to Multi-Party Democracy - In 1992, the Constitution was amended to allow: - A multi-party system. - Gradual privatization of the economy. - Nyerere passed away in 1999, leaving behind a legacy of stability, unity, and self-reliance. - South Africa: A History of Apartheid and Resistance - A Nation of Wealth and Controversy - South Africa, one of Africa’s richest and most industrialized nations, was also infamous for its racist policies. - Until the 1990s, the country was synonymous with apartheid, a system of racial discrimination comparable to Nazi Germany and Stalinist USSR. - The White Minority’s Domination - Demographics: - 70% of the population were Blacks. - 12% were other non-Whites, including ethnic Indians and Asians. - 18% were Whites, who held absolute political and social control. - Apartheid Laws: - Blacks were confined to reserved areas, which comprised only 7% of the land. - Entry into White areas required passes, reinforcing a system of segregation. - Institutionalizing Apartheid (1948–1978) - Daniel Malan (1948–1954): - Introduced a stricter form of apartheid, fearing that White dominance would end following India’s independence and global pressure for racial equality. - Spread Nazi-style propaganda about White supremacy. - Received support from the Afrikaner Nationalist Party and the Dutch Reformed Church. - His Successors: - Strijdom (1954–1958) and Vorster (1966–1978) continued apartheid policies, despite global condemnation. - South Africa became a pariah nation due to its racist laws. - The Horrors of Apartheid - Living Conditions: - Non-Whites were forced into overcrowded, unsanitary townships. - Their homes could be demolished at any time if Whites felt they were “too close for comfort.” - Strict Segregation: - Separate buses, trains, public toilets, and even park benches for non-Whites. - Inferior schools for Black children. - Every person had to carry a racial identity card and prove their legitimacy in White areas. - No political rights, with the Parliament being exclusively White-dominated. - Suppression of Dissent: - The Suppression of Communism Act branded all opposition as communists. - Protesters (opposers) could be indefinitely detained without trial. - The African National Congress (ANC) and Resistance - The ANC led the fight against apartheid, inspired by Mahatma Gandhi’s Satyagraha. - Albert Luthuli (1952) launched: - A protest movement, including work stoppages and civil disobedience. - Non-Whites entered White-only shops in defiance (open resistance) of segregation laws. - Brutal Crackdown: - Over 8,000 people were flogged (beaten with whip) in public. - Luthuli was jailed, and the ANC was suppressed. - The Freedom Charter (1955): - The ANC, along with other non-White groups, proclaimed that “South Africa belongs to all who live in it, Black and White.” - Demands included: - Equality before the law. - Freedom of speech, religion, and the press. - Voting rights and fair wages. - Free medical care and education. - The Sharpville Massacre (1960) and Armed Resistance - In Sharpville, near Johannesburg, a peaceful protest against the Pass Law turned into a bloodbath. - Police fired on demonstrators, killing 67 and wounding hundreds. - Shift from Non-Violence to Armed Struggle: - Many Black leaders concluded that non-violence was ineffective. - The ANC turned to sabotage and bomb attacks. - Repression intensified: - The ANC was banned. - Nelson Mandela was arrested and sentenced to life imprisonment. - Luthuli died in 1967, widely suspected to be murdered. - South Africa’s long and painful struggle against apartheid continued, ultimately leading to its demise in the 1990s. - Apartheid and International Response - Apartheid: A 20th Century Anachronism (attributing something to a period to which it don't belong) - By the mid-20th century, apartheid was seen as an outdated and inhumane system. - South Africa faced expulsion from the Commonwealth, but Prime Minister Hendrik Verwoerd avoided this by withdrawing voluntarily and declaring the country a Republic. - Global Condemnation and Economic Sanctions - The United Nations voted for an economic boycott against South Africa. - However, the sanctions were largely ineffective because major Western powers, including: - The United States - Britain - France - West Germany - Italy - Publicly condemned apartheid but continued trade with South Africa. - During the Cold War, Western nations even supplied arms to South Africa, viewing it as a strategic ally against communism in Africa. - Continued Black Resistance and Government Repression (the act of subduing) - Protests persisted (carried on) despite harsh government crackdowns. - The Soweto Uprising (1976): - A massive protest erupted in Soweto, a Black township near Johannesburg. - The police responded with brutal force, killing 200 people. - Steve Biko’s Death (1977): - Steve Biko, a charismatic young leader of the Black Consciousness Movement, was arrested and beaten to death by the police. - Despite relentless (persistent) oppression, the resistance movement grew stronger, eventually leading to the fall of apartheid in the early 1990s. - The Road to the End of Apartheid - Reforms Under P.W. Botha (1979-1989) - In 1979, Prime Minister P.W. Botha initiated reforms to ease apartheid laws due to: - Economic problems caused by trade restrictions from hostile neighboring countries. - Demographic shifts, with the White population declining to 16% due to emigration (moving abroad), while the Black population increased. - Earlier, the government had promoted independent tribal ‘homeland’ states, but the plan failed due to: - Corruption among rulers co-opted by the racist regime. - Widespread poverty among their people. - Botha acknowledged apartheid’s dangers, stating: “A revolution in South Africa is no longer a remote possibility. Either we adapt or we perish. White domination and legally enforced apartheid are a recipe for permanent conflict.” - Partial Reforms and Black Resistance - Botha introduced some reforms: - Allowed Blacks to form trade unions and elect local councils in townships. - Established two new Houses of Parliament for Coloureds and Asians—but Blacks were still excluded from national elections. - Legalized interracial marriages (1985) and abolished the Pass Law (1986). - However, these reforms failed to appease Black South Africans, as full equality was still denied. - Blacks who participated in elections were accused of collaborating with the racist regime. - A state of civil war developed. - In Port Elizabeth (1985), police fired on a peaceful funeral procession, killing 40 people, on the 25th anniversary of the Sharpeville massacre. - International Pressure Intensifies - South Africa faced growing global condemnation: - Banned from international sporting events. - In 1986, the US Congress imposed strict sanctions, halting trade and investments. - President Reagan vetoed the decision, but public pressure ensured its enforcement. - Margaret Thatcher opposed sanctions, arguing they would harm Blacks, but Rajiv Gandhi led a global campaign for stricter sanctions that crippled South Africa’s economy. - Archbishop Desmond Tutu won the 1984 Nobel Peace Prize, mobilizing both local and international opposition against apartheid. - Moderate White politicians began advocating power-sharing with Blacks. - The Presidency of F.W. de Klerk (1989-1994) - In 1989, F.W. de Klerk was elected President. Though cautious at first, he was: - Opposed to apartheid and recognized the need for Black political inclusion. - Despite resistance from White supremacists, he initiated major reforms: - Released Nelson Mandela (1990) after 27 years in prison. - Granted Namibia independence. - Abolished all apartheid laws. - Started negotiations with the ANC to draft a new democratic Constitution. - By 1993, negotiations succeeded, leading to: - Merging of ‘homeland’ territories with the rest of South Africa. - Establishment of a fully democratic system. - The Rise of Nelson Mandela and the End of Apartheid - In April 1994, South Africa held its first-ever open elections. - The ANC emerged victorious, and Nelson Mandela became the country’s first Black President. - Mandela’s Leadership (1994-1999): - Chose reconciliation (restoration of friendly relations) over revenge, refusing to punish those who had persecuted him. - Established the Truth and Reconciliation Commission, allowing victims to confront perpetrators (person who carries out harmful, illegal, immoral act) without legal repercussions (consequences / unintended consequences), ensuring that apartheid’s atrocities were recorded but not repeated. - In 1999, Mandela retired from politics, handing over the Presidency to Thabo Mbeki. - - Mandela’s Legacy - Despite spending 27 years in prison, Mandela became a global symbol of courage, perseverance (persistence), and forgiveness. - Today, he remains one of the most revered figures worldwide, representing justice, peace, and the triumph (great victory) of humanity over oppression. - The Asian scenerio after the dissolution of the USSR in 1991 - Kazakhstan: History, Demographics, and Leadership - Geographic and Demographic Overview - Kazakhstan is almost as large as India but has a population smaller than the city of Chennai. - Unlike many nations, Kazakhstan never existed as an independent entity before the fall of the Soviet Union. - Historical Background and Soviet Influence - The partition of Central Asia mirrored Africa’s, where new nations were created to serve colonial interests in the 19th century. - In the early 20th century, Central Asia was a vast but sparsely populated region occupied by various tribes such as: - Kazakhs, Turkmen, Uzbeks, Mongols, and others. - Stalin’s Forced Relocations (1930s–1950s): - Stalin pursued (chased / go after) a policy of populating Central Asia with forcibly displaced people from Europe. - Hundreds of thousands of Poles, Ukrainians, Latvians, Lithuanians, and Germans were sent eastward. - Families were often separated permanently. - Kazakhstan’s Strategic and Economic Importance - The country housed most of the USSR’s nuclear facilities. - Rich in natural gas, but unable to capitalize on it due to its geographical isolation from key Asian markets. - Post-Soviet Leadership - In 1991, after independence, Nursultan Nazarbayev, the former communist chief, became President of Kazakhstan. - Like other ex-Soviet leaders, he swiftly adopted democratic rhetoric but governed with an authoritarian approach. - In 1999, he was re-elected for another seven-year term, as his main opponent was disqualified from the elections. - Uzbekistan: Historical Ties, Economic Potential, and Geopolitical Shifts - Historical and Cultural Ties with India - Uzbekistan shares deep civilizational links with India. - The Mughals originated from this region, establishing strong historical connections. - Economic and Industrial Landscape - Although Uzbekistan lacks significant natural gas reserves, it is highly industrialized. - It holds potential as a trading partner for India. - Security Challenges and Terrorism - The country has been a victim of international Islamic terrorism, particularly in the Ferghana Valley, which is shared with Tajikistan. - Geopolitical Shift: US Military Presence in Central Asia (2001) - In 2001, during the US-led war in Afghanistan, President Islam Karimov allowed the United States to establish military bases in Uzbekistan. - This move disrupted the existing balance of power, as Central Asian states were historically aligned with Russia under a common security pact. - Russia viewed this as a direct challenge to its regional dominance. - Tajikistan: Post-Independence Turmoil and the Fight Against Terrorism - Political Instability After Independence (1991-1993) - After gaining independence in 1991, Tajikistan experienced severe (very bad / serious) political instability. - President Rakhmon Nabiyev, a conservative former communist, was forced to resign within months of taking office. - In September 1992, a coalition government of Islamic, nationalist, and Western-oriented parties was formed. - However, this coalition was unstable, leading to a pro-Communist takeover in 1993 and the introduction of a new Presidential system. - Tajikistan as a ‘Frontline State’ Against Terrorism - Tajikistan is considered a key player in the global fight against international Islamic terrorism. - The country has been one of the worst affected by terrorism in the region. - The Rise of Militant Islam and Civil War (1992-1997) - Under communist rule, Islam in Central Asia had adopted a more liberal outlook: - Women did not wear veils. - They had equal access to education and employment. - After independence, economic struggles allowed militant Islam, allegedly supported by Pakistan and extremist Islamic states, to gain influence. - This led to a civil war in Tajikistan, which resulted in the deaths of at least 55,000 people by 1997. - Turkmenistan: Energy Wealth and Authoritarian Rule - Vast Oil and Gas Reserves - Turkmenistan possesses the largest oil and natural gas deposits in the Central Asian region. - Due to its landlocked geography, the country exports gas via pipelines through Iran. - Authoritarian Rule and Personality Cult (sect / religious group) - President Saparmurad Niazov, known as “Turkmenbashi” (Leader of the Turkmen), established a strong personality cult. - His statues are widespread, adorning (decorating) almost every street in the country. - Mongolia and Kyrgyzstan: Challenges of Geography and Politics - Mongolia: Isolation and Political Landscape - Due to its remoteness and extreme climate, Mongolia remains economically backward with limited natural resources. - The political scene is still dominated by former Communists, despite the country’s transition from socialism. - Kyrgyzstan: Similar Struggles with Added Security Concerns - Kyrgyzstan faces similar economic challenges but also grapples (wrestle) with Islamic fundamentalism. - In 2001, during the U.S. war against the Taliban, President Askar Akayev allowed the American Air Force to establish military bases in the country. - Afghanistan: A Crucial Link in India’s Security and a Battlefield of Power Struggles - Strategic Importance and Historical Connection - Afghanistan, situated at the junction of South and Central Asia, is crucial to India’s security. - Historically, it was part of Greater India and a gateway for numerous invaders since ancient times. - India has a de jure border with Afghanistan, but Pakistan’s illegal occupation of parts of Jammu and Kashmir has severed their traditional connection. - A Nation Devastated by War - Afghanistan has endured years of civil war and oppressive regimes, both communist and fundamentalist Islamic. - Presently, it is struggling to rebuild under international supervision, with efforts to establish a liberal democratic government. - The Communist Takeover and Soviet Invasion (1973-1979) - In 1973, King Mohammed Zahir Shah was overthrown in a military coup led by Daoud Khan, who was backed by the USSR. - The People’s Democratic Republic of Afghanistan was created as a Moscow-aligned state with a new constitution. - However, Daoud’s suppression of Islamic clergy (body of all people ordained for religious duties) angered tribal factions. - In 1978, Nur Mohammed Taraki, a communist leader with clergy connections, staged a bloody coup, killing Daoud and declaring Afghanistan’s independence from Moscow. - Taraki’s social reforms conflicted with Islamic values, leading to widespread revolts across Afghanistan. - His rival, Hafizullah Amin, seized control of most of the countryside. - Fearing Pakistan’s involvement, Soviet President Leonid Brezhnev ordered the invasion of Afghanistan in December 1979. - Amin was assassinated, and Babrak Karmal, a pro-Moscow leader, was installed as President. - The Cold War Battleground (1980s): U.S., Pakistan, and the Mujahideen - Afghanistan became a key Cold War battleground between the USSR and the US. - The United States, under President Jimmy Carter, used Pakistan as a frontline state against the Soviet Union. - Pakistan’s Zia-ul-Haque regime armed and trained Mujahideen fighters using U.S.-supplied weapons, including Stinger missiles. - The Mujahideen waged a fierce guerrilla war, making it impossible for the Soviets to gain full control. - Afghanistan’s rugged terrain further complicated Moscow’s efforts. - The war was marked by brutal atrocities, with villages burned and civilians massacred. - Despite immense Soviet firepower, the Mujahideen resisted for eight years, leading to an estimated 300,000 deaths. - Soviet Withdrawal and Continued Instability (1988-Onwards) - Under Mikhail Gorbachev, the USSR acknowledged the war’s futility (pointlessness). - In 1988, a peace treaty was signed in Geneva, and the Soviets withdrew. - However, Moscow-installed President Najibullah continued ruling, sparking civil war as the Mujahideen refused to accept his leadership. - The US lost interest in Afghanistan after the Soviets’ departure, abandoning (renounce) the region. - Both superpowers left behind a massive stockpile of arms, ensuring that conflict persisted (carried on) long after their withdrawal. - Afghanistan: From Civil War to Global Terrorism and the Fall of the Taliban - The Aftermath of the Cold War and Pakistan’s Proxy War - As civil war raged in Afghanistan, the USSR collapsed, marking the end of the Cold War. - Despite continued human suffering and massacres, global interest in Afghanistan declined. - Pakistan became home to armed Mujahideen, many of whom were sent to wage a proxy war in Jammu and Kashmir during Benazir Bhutto’s regime, leading to a sustained campaign of terror in India. - The Rise of the Mujahideen and Internal Power Struggles (1992-1996) - In 1992, President Najibullah fell, and the Mujahideen captured Kabul. - They installed Burhanuddin Rabbani, an ethnic Tajik, as the new leader, but this move angered the powerful Pashtun tribes of southern Afghanistan. - Meanwhile, Pakistan cultivated a new fundamentalist force—the Taliban, whose fighters were mostly madrasa students (talib). - The Taliban began a brutal military campaign, killing both combatants (fighting man) and civilians as they marched toward Kabul. - Pakistan backed the Taliban to gain ‘strategic depth’ in a future war against India, as Afghanistan’s previous rulers had denied such support during the Indo-Pak wars of 1965 and 1971. - The Taliban’s Rule and Extreme Fundamentalism (1996-2001) - In 1996, the Taliban captured Kabul and established one of the most extremist regimes in the world. - Women, who had been among the most educated and progressive in the Islamic world, faced severe repression: - Banned from working or receiving education. - Forced to wear the burqa at all times. - Subjected to brutal punishments for disobedience. - The Taliban imposed harsh Islamic restrictions on daily life and enforced extreme punishments. - Only three countries—Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE—recognized the Taliban government. - Al-Qaeda’s Rise and Global Terrorism - Former Mujahideen leader Ahmed Shah Masood regrouped anti-Taliban forces in the Panjsher Valley and launched resistance attacks. - The Taliban gained global notoriety (bad reputation) for harboring Al-Qaeda, the world’s most feared terrorist group. - Under Taliban protection, Osama bin Laden orchestrated multiple terrorist attacks: - 1993 World Trade Center bombing in New York, killing 6 and injuring over 1,000. - 1998 US embassy bombings in Nairobi (Kenya) and Dar es Salaam (Tanzania), killing 263 people. - Global Pressure and Taliban Defiance (open resistance) - Despite international pressure, the Taliban refused to hand over Osama bin Laden. - The Taliban and Al-Qaeda thrived on drug trafficking, moving narcotics through Central Asia to Russia, Europe, and America to fund terrorism. - In 1998, the US launched missile strikes on bin Laden’s camp, but he escaped. - India, Russia, China, and Central Asian republics convinced the UN to impose sanctions on Afghanistan in December 1999. - Further sanctions in January 2001 failed to break the Taliban’s resolve. - In a final act of defiance, the Taliban destroyed the giant Bamiyan Buddhas, shocking the world. - The 9/11 Attacks and the Fall of the Taliban - On September 11, 2001, Al-Qaeda launched the deadliest terrorist attack on the World Trade Center in New York. - The attack brought global attention to Afghanistan’s suffering and its role as a terrorist hub. - President George W. Bush declared a ‘War on Terror’, making Afghanistan the first battleground. - The Taliban resisted briefly (short time) but were soon overthrown by US-led coalition forces. - Hamid Karzai, a former Mujahideen, was installed as interim President until elections could be held. - Pakistan’s Policy Shift and the Aftermath - Pakistan’s Pervez Musharraf regime had been backing the Taliban, but under US pressure, it withdrew support. - Pakistan even allowed US military bases on its soil for the war against the Taliban. - This policy shift brought diplomatic and financial benefits to Pakistan but also exposed it as a global hub of terrorism. - The fall of the Taliban was a major blow to Pakistan, forcing it to abandon its ambition of gaining ‘strategic depth’ against India. - Climax and fall of communism in the USSR and easter Europe - The Rise and Fall of Soviet Communism - Collapse of the Communist Empire - Stalin’s empire stretched from East Germany to Mongolia. - Collapsed suddenly in 1991 after 74 years. - Left a lasting impact despite its short existence. - Achievements and Failures - Major achievements: Space conquest and industrial growth. - Totalitarian rule: No free speech or enterprise. - Economic decline: Couldn’t compete with the US. - Eastern Bloc resistance: Moscow’s control clashed with national pride. - Understanding the Fall - The chapter explores why Soviet communism collapsed. - Earlier discussions: - Russian Revolution: Bloodshed and economic shifts. - Stalin’s rule: Expansion and suppression. - Turning point: Stalin’s death on 5 March 1953. - Power Struggle After Stalin’s Death - Suspected Poisoning - Research suggests Stalin was poisoned by close aides. - They feared his aggressive policies could start another World War. - Power Struggle in the Kremlin - Stalin’s death led to a bloody power struggle. - Soviet citizens were shocked as they had known no other leader. - Key contenders: - Lavrenti Beria – KGB chief, oversaw mass executions. - Georgi Malenkov – Soviet premier. - Nikita Khrushchev – Senior party leader, responsible for purges in Ukraine. - Khrushchev Takes Control - Khrushchev emerged as the leader within months. - Beria was executed, marking the end of his brutal reign. - Khrushchev’s Reforms and De-Stalinization - Condemning Stalin (1956) - At the 20th Communist Party Congress, Khrushchev openly criticized Stalin. - Accused him of: - Promoting a personality cult. - Deviating from party principles. - Ordering massacres in the 1930s. - Mismanaging World War II. - Emphasized that socialism should succeed without Stalin’s methods. - Advocated better relations with the US to avoid nuclear war. - Economic Reforms - De-Stalinization process aimed at improving Soviet living standards. - Continued Five-Year Plans but shifted focus from: - Heavy industries → Agriculture & Consumer goods. - Replaced Stalin’s centralized system with Regional Economic Councils. - Allowed managers to focus on profits instead of quotas. - Economy became more stable but lagged behind the US and Western nations. - Agricultural Reforms - Launched the Virgin Lands Scheme to cultivate Siberia & Kazakhstan. - Allowed peasants to sell surplus crops from private plots. - Increased procurement (action of obtaining) rates to encourage production. - 1953-1958: Collective farm output rose by 56%. - 1962: Grain production reached 147 million tonnes. - 1963: Fell to 110 million tonnes, leading to dependence on food imports. - Political Changes - Reduced Stalinist oppression: - No personality cult. - Less KGB surveillance on ordinary citizens. - Dissidents (person opposing official policy) faced dismissal, not execution. - However, repression continued in cases like Hungary (1956). - Gulags (prison camps) remained, but mass terror was less frequent. - Eastern European Uprisings (1956) - The Hungarian Uprising - Led by Prime Minister Imre Nagy, a nationalist communist. - Hungarians demanded freedom from Soviet control. - Nagy: - Planned a broad-based government with non-communist parties. - Cut ties with Moscow, alarming Soviet leadership. - 24 October 1956: - Soviet tanks invaded Hungary. - Weeks of violent clashes followed. - After suppression: - 100,000+ arrested, many interned. - 25,000 jailed, 229 executed. - Nagy & his followers sentenced to death, body buried in secrecy. - Janos Kadar, a Soviet loyalist, installed as leader. - The Polish Uprising - June 1956: Polish workers revolted, demanding better wages & living standards. - Warsaw’s communist regime attempted to suppress the unrest. - October 1956: - Khrushchev deployed tanks but ordered no shooting. - A compromise was reached. - Władysław Gomułka, imprisoned by Stalin, was freed & made leader. - Outcome: - Poland allowed to follow its own version of communism. - Permitted to trade with non-communist nations. - Khrushchev’s Downfall (1964) - Main Reason: Failure of his agricultural policy. - October 1964: The Central Committee forced him into retirement. - Age & Health: Though 70 years old, he was in perfect health. - Other Factors: - Sino-Soviet Rift: Relations between the USSR & China had deteriorated. - Controversial Foreign Policy: - Grew too close to Egypt. - Awarded the Order of Lenin to President Nasser, despite Nasser suppressing communism. - Retirement & Death: - Lived in his dacha (country home) until his death in 1971. - Succeeded by Leonid Brezhnev. - The Sino-Soviet Rift - Early Friendship (Post-1949) - The USSR and China were close allies after the Chinese Communist Party came to power. - Soviet military aid helped Mao Zedong seize power. - Despite an unresolved border dispute, they signed the Treaty of Mutual Assistance and Friendship (1950). - Ideological Differences (Post-Stalin Era) - After Stalin’s death, ideological differences emerged. - Mao opposed Khrushchev’s reforms, defending Stalin’s use of terror. - China accused Khrushchev of “revisionism”—straying from Marxist-Leninist principles. - Territorial Dispute - Russia had seized large areas of Chinese land (19th century). - Mao demanded their return, but Khrushchev refused. - Cold War Conflicts - Vietnam War: - The USSR backed the Vietcong under Ho Chi Minh. - China & Vietnam disagreed over Pol Pot’s brutal regime in Cambodia (1975-79). - In 1979, China attacked Vietnam, worsening Sino-Soviet relations. - China-US Rapprochement (1972): - China reconciled with the US, sparking a Cold War with the USSR. - The USSR sought to isolate China diplomatically. - Afghanistan Invasion (1979): - The Soviet Army invaded Afghanistan, installing a puppet communist regime. - China supported the Mujahideen in their resistance. - Reconciliation (1989-Present) - Gorbachev’s Visit (1989): - Marked the beginning of improved Sino-Soviet ties. - Post-Soviet Era: - After the Soviet collapse, Russia mended (repaired) ties with China. - Boris Yeltsin (First Russian President) actively promoted defense & trade relations with China. - Economic cooperation has steadily grown since then. - The Prague Spring and Soviet Suppression (1968) - Brezhnev’s Hardline Approach - Leonid Brezhnev, who succeeded Khrushchev, followed a Stalinist approach. - Unlike Khrushchev, who allowed Eastern Bloc nations some autonomy, Brezhnev saw this as a threat to Soviet control. - By 1968, he was increasingly anxious about developments in Czechoslovakia. - Growing Czech Discontent - Czechoslovakia, the most industrially and culturally advanced nation in the Eastern Bloc, resented Soviet domination. - Antonin Novotny, a pro-Soviet hardliner, was widely hated for his authoritarian rule. - The Soviets forced Czechoslovakia to accept lower-quality goods, such as inferior steel from Siberia, over better imports from Sweden. - Suppression of free speech and violent crackdowns on demonstrations fueled unrest. - Rise of Alexander Dubcek & the Prague Spring (January-August 1968) - In January 1968, after mass protests, Novotny resigned. - Alexander Dubcek, the widely respected First Secretary of the Czech Communist Party, took office. - He initiated progressive reforms, which he called “socialism with a human face”: - Ending Communist Party interference in daily life. - Decentralizing industries, giving them freedom from party control. - Reforming agriculture, replacing collective farms with independent cooperatives. - Empowering trade unions with greater autonomy. - Introducing civil liberties, including freedom of speech, press, and foreign travel. - Czechoslovakia remained in the Warsaw Pact, but Moscow feared these changes would inspire similar movements across the Eastern Bloc. - Soviet Invasion & Brutal Crackdown (August 1968) - In August 1968, fearing the spread of reforms, Moscow launched a massive invasion under “Operation Danube”. - Warsaw Pact forces, including Soviet, Polish, East German, Bulgarian, and Hungarian troops, invaded Czechoslovakia. - The invasion force was larger than Nazi Germany’s in 1938. - Dubcek called for passive resistance, but brutal repression followed: - 90 civilians were killed. - Mass arrests of liberal leaders, some deported to Siberia. - Self-immolation protests by Czechs, drawing global condemnation. - Soviet Control & the Brezhnev Doctrine (belief or set of belief) - Facing international pressure, the Soviets released some arrested leaders but intensified their repression. - Gustav Husak was installed as a puppet leader under strict Soviet control. - Censorship was reimposed, and the Czech Communist Party was purged of reformists. - 40,000 people fled the country. - Brezhnev justified the invasion through the Brezhnev Doctrine, declaring that: - The USSR had the right to intervene in any communist country if its regime was threatened by reform movements. - The Prague Spring was crushed, reinforcing Soviet domination over the Eastern Bloc, but it remained a symbol of resistance against authoritarianism. - The Spread of the Prague Spring’s Spirit - International Exposure of Soviet Repression - Unlike previous Soviet crackdowns in East Germany (1953), Hungary (1956), and Poland (1956)—which occurred behind the Iron Curtain—the Prague Spring (1968) unfolded under global media scrutiny (careful examination). - When the Warsaw Pact Army invaded Czechoslovakia, international journalists were already present in Prague, ensuring that the world witnessed Soviet aggression firsthand. - Growing Unrest in the Eastern Bloc - Despite Soviet suppression, the spirit of resistance spread across the Eastern Bloc. - By 1970, riots erupted in Poland, marking the beginning of a decade-long struggle against Soviet rule. - This unrest culminated (reach a climax or point of highest development) in 1979 with the emergence of Solidarity (unity), an independent trade union movement, which became a major force in Poland’s push for freedom. - Widespread Dissidence (disagreement) and Defections (desertion of one's country or cuase in favour of an opposing one) - Across Eastern Europe, people began privately discussing communism’s failures. - In East Germany, the population longed for reunification with West Germany, which led to heightened surveillance and repression by the Stasi (secret police). - Dissidence spread rapidly, with many attempting to defect to the West. - Many failed escape attempts resulted in imprisonment or execution. - The Road to 1989 - The growing stirrings for freedom would continue building momentum, eventually reaching their climax in 1989, as discussed later in the chapter. - The Decline of the Soviet Communist Party and the Rise of Gorbachev - The Aging Leadership and Economic Crisis - By the 1980s, the Soviet Communist Party was dominated by elderly leaders, unable to cope with mounting (going up) crises. - Leonid Brezhnev, in his final years, was a shadow of his former self, struggling as the Soviet economy deteriorated (worsened). - The Ronald Reagan administration in the U.S. took an aggressive stance against communism, especially after Brezhnev ordered the invasion of Afghanistan. - Brezhnev died in October 1982, followed by two short-lived leaders: - Yuri Andropov (1982–1984) attempted some liberal reforms by replacing aging officials with younger ones. - Konstantin Chernenko (1984–1985) was a Stalinist revivalist who ignored economic hardship and persecuted intellectuals, including nuclear physicist Andrei Sakharov, jailed in Siberia since 1980. - The Rise of Mikhail Gorbachev - After Chernenko’s death, Mikhail Gorbachev was elected General Secretary in 1985 at the age of 54. - He was widely regarded as the most dynamic and intellectually evolved communist leader. - His leadership was based on two major policies: - Glasnost (Openness) - Perestroika (Restructuring) - - Glasnost: A New Era of Openness - Gorbachev encouraged political criticism within the Communist Party. - Key reforms under Glasnost: - Release of political dissidents, including Sakharov. - Revocation of laws that sent critics to mental institutions. - Restrictions on the KGB’s power were eased. - Historical transparency: He acknowledged and apologized for the 1940 Katyn massacre, in which over 4,000 Polish officers were executed. - Media freedom: Coverage of government meetings was allowed for the first time. - Arts and literature revival: Banned books and films were reintroduced. - Religious freedom: The communist suppression of religion was lifted. - Chernobyl disaster (1986): The Soviet media reported on government failures, a first in communist history. - Perestroika: Economic Restructuring - Gorbachev inherited a severely mismanaged economy, plagued by shortages of basic goods. - Key economic reforms under Perestroika: - Introduction of fiscal (tex / revenue) discipline to curb reckless currency printing. - Small-scale private enterprises were encouraged. - Quality control measures introduced in industries. - Automation and computerization allowed for efficiency. - Decentralization: Factories and businesses were given market-based autonomy. - State planning system dismantled, shifting focus to consumer demands. - The Failure of Reforms and Growing Discontent - Despite Gorbachev’s efforts, economic problems persisted (carried on): - 1988–1989: Economic growth remained stagnant. - 1990: National income fell by 15%. - Workers focused on producing high-value goods, neglecting basic necessities. - 25% of the population lived in extreme poverty. - By 1989, the working class lost patience: - Nationwide coal miners’ strike, the first since 1917, erupted over economic hardships. - Essential goods like soap, toothbrushes, and food were in short supply. - Gorbachev did not suppress the strikes, instead allowing independent trade unions. - Rising Opposition to Gorbachev - Gorbachev found himself caught between two factions: - Radicals, who criticized him for not reforming fast enough. - Hardliners, who resented his departure from strict communist principles (truth / concept / idea). - Although Gorbachev had pledged to preserve communism, his openness and reforms weakened the system. - Boris Yeltsin, the President of Russia, emerged as a major critic, openly challenging Gorbachev’s policies (plans / strategy). - Conclusion - Gorbachev’s reforms triggered political and economic upheavals that he struggled to control. - By the early 1990s, the Soviet Union was on the verge of collapse, as resentment (bitterness), economic decline, and political opposition intensified. - The Disintegration of the East Bloc - The winds of change set in motion by the Prague Spring continued to sweep across Eastern Europe throughout the 1970s. - By the 1980s, the circulation of free ideas and nationalist ideologies intensified, further fueling the demand for change. - The Fall of Communism in Poland - The Solidarity Movement and Initial Suppression - Led by Lech Walesa, the Solidarity movement gained momentum in Poland. - Brezhnev, nearing the end of his rule, had approved military pressure against Poland but was unable to act effectively. - The ongoing Afghan War weakened Soviet military strength and public support for foreign interventions. - Instead of direct Soviet intervention, Polish leader General Jaruzelski declared martial law to suppress Solidarity (unity / here solidarity means a union from poland). - Solidarity (here solidarity means a union from poland) was banned, and thousands of activists were arrested and suppressed. - Martial law was lifted in 1983, and Solidarity (here solidarity means a union from poland) leaders were released. - Economic Crisis and the Return of Solidarity - Poland’s economic problems persisted (carried on), and in 1988, General Wojciech Jaruzelski cut government subsidies, leading to mass job losses and rising food prices. - Despite being banned, Solidarity (here solidarity means a union from poland) organized new demonstrations across the country. - Unlike before, Jaruzelski did not suppress the protests, as Gorbachev in Moscow had adopted a policy of non-interference in Eastern Europe. - Facing an economic crisis, Jaruzelski was forced to negotiate with Solidarity (here solidarity means a union from poland), leading to a major constitutional overhaul (repair / maintain) in April 1989. - The 1989 Constitution and Free Elections - Poland introduced a new Constitution, creating a bicameral Parliament: - Lower House (Sejm): 65% of seats reserved for Communists. - Senate: Open for free elections, without Communist control. - The two Houses would elect a President, who in turn would appoint a Prime Minister. - Solidarity was officially recognized as a political party for the first time. - June 1989 elections: - Solidarity won 92 out of 100 Senate seats and 160 out of 161 open seats in the Lower House. - Jaruzelski retained the Presidency due to the reserved Communist seats. - However, under public pressure, he appointed Tadeusz Mazowiecki, the first non-Communist Prime Minister in Eastern Europe. - Final Collapse of Communism in Poland - In December 1990, the 1989 Constitution was amended to remove Communist guarantees. - By this time, Communism had collapsed across Eastern Europe, leaving Jaruzelski with no justification to stay in power. - Lech Walesa was elected President, and Poland became fully independent for the first time since 1939. - The Fall of Communism in Hungary - Economic Crisis and Internal Party Struggles - Janos Kadar, Hungary’s leader under Soviet influence, faced a crisis within the Communist Party in 1986. - Hungary was in a deep economic crisis, and Kadar struggled to find solutions. - The country’s industries were burdened with outdated Soviet-imposed machinery. - The Communist Party split into two factions: - Reformists urged decentralization, leading Kadar to establish company councils with the right to elect managers. - Hardliners, expecting Soviet support, insisted on maintaining the Stalinist system. - However, they miscalculated, as Gorbachev was not Stalin and did not intervene to suppress reforms. - Political Shakeup and Rise of the Opposition - In May 1988, a major shakeup within the Communist Party led to Kadar and eight of his supporters being voted out of the Politburo. - This shift allowed progressive communists to take control. - Meanwhile, two major opposition parties emerged: - Liberal Alliance of Free Democrats - Democratic Forum - The reformist Communists negotiated with the opposition and agreed to hold free elections. - In an attempt to reinvent itself, the Communist Party rebranded as the Hungarian Socialist Party. - The 1990 Elections and the End of Communism - Despite its rebranding, public sentiment had turned against the communists. - In Hungary’s first free elections, the Democratic Forum, led by Jozsef Antall, secured victory. - With this, Hungary permanently ended communist rule. - The Reunification of Germany - Division of Germany - Yalta Conference (1945) led to two Germanies. - West Germany (1949): Free, democratic, led by Adenauer. - East Germany (1949): Communist, controlled by Stasi. - Economy: Strongest in East Bloc but weaker than West Germany. - Rising Discontent - 1985: Honecker resisted reforms. - Gorbachev’s policies fueled protests. - Gorbachev’s Role - June 1989: Sought Western aid. - Kohl pushed for reunification. - Gorbachev agreed in exchange for aid. - East Germans fled via Poland, Hungary, Czechoslovakia. - Fall of the Berlin Wall - Honecker’s crackdown failed. - Communists ousted him. - Krenz opened the Wall (Nov 1989). - West German parties pushed for unity. - Reunification - 3 Oct 1990: Germany reunified. - Cold War neared its end. - The Velvet Revolution in Czechoslovakia - Persistent Resistance - 1970s: Brutal suppression failed to silence protests. - Despite political unrest, the economy remained the strongest in the region. - Economic Decline & Leadership Crisis - 1980s: USSR’s control harmed the economy. - Gustav Husak (since 1969) failed to resolve issues, resigned in 1987. - Milos Jakes took over but proved equally ineffective. - Rise of Vaclav Havel & Charter 77 - Havel, a playwright, led Czech Charter 77. - Velvet Revolution began, drawing massive public support. - Fall of Communism - November 1989: Alexander Dubcek re-emerged in politics. - Strikes & protests escalated. - Moscow did not intervene. - Jakes resigned. - Democratic Transition - 29 December 1989: Havel elected President. - End of Extreme Communist Tyranny in Romania - Nicolae Ceausescu’s Brutal Regime - Ruled since 1965, considered more brutal than East Germany’s Honecker. - Controlled Romania with Securitate, a secret police as oppressive as the Stasi. - The 1989 Uprising - Until November 1989, the regime seemed unshaken. - 17 December: Army fired on peaceful protestors in Timisoara, killing many. - Nationwide outrage erupted, branding Ceausescu and his wife as murderers. - 25 December (Christmas 1989): Crowds stormed the palace, captured them, and executed them after a short trial. - Struggle for Democracy - Romania lacked organized opposition like Solidarity (Poland) or Charter 77 (Czechoslovakia). - National Salvation Front, led by Ion Iliescu (a former minister under Ceausescu), took power. - 1996: Christian Democrat Emil Constantinescu won elections, leading the first non-communist government. - Bulgaria’s Easy Transition - Todor Zhivkov, in power since 1954, was a hardliner who refused reforms despite a collapsing economy. - Mikhail Gorbachev repeatedly urged him to implement changes, but he resisted. - By 1989, fearing the fate of their counterparts in neighboring countries, his own party turned against him. - December 1989: The Politburo voted him out and renamed the party as the Bulgarian Socialist Party. - End of Communism in Albania - Albania became communist in 1945 through an internal uprising, without Soviet intervention. - Enver Hoxha, the dictator for 40 years, admired Stalin’s system and kept the country isolated and backward. - His successor, Ramiz Alia, failed to address Albania’s worsening situation. - Unlike other East Bloc countries, Albanians had little experience in mass uprisings. - 1991: Thousands of young Albanians fled to Italy, drawing international criticism. - 1992: Free elections were held, and Sali Berisha became Albania’s first non-communist leader. - Reasons for the Collapse of Communism in Eastern Europe (1945–1989) - Communism Was Imposed, Not Accepted Naturally - The East Bloc countries were independent, culturally advanced, and historically accustomed to freedom. - After World War II, the Soviet Army liberated them from the Nazis but did not restore their sovereignty. - Instead of following the Allied example of democracy, Stalin brutally imposed communism on them. - Suffering Under Communist Tyranny - Moderate socialists and democrats were executed to eliminate opposition. - Hungary (1949): Leaders Laszlo Rajk and Janos Kadar were hanged for defying Stalin. - Bulgaria (1949): Prime Minister Traicho Kostov was arrested and executed. - Czechoslovakia (1952): Stalin ordered the execution of Rudolf Slansky and 10 other ministers. - Over time, people realized their leaders were mere puppets of Moscow. - Economic Decline and Youth Unemployment - The economies of the East Bloc were tied to the USSR, using outdated technology that made them uncompetitive. - While Western Europe prospered, East Germans, Poles, Czechs, and Hungarians saw their own economies collapse. - By the 1970s and 1980s, unemployment surged, especially among the youth. - Revolutions often stem from economic despair, and with nothing to lose, young people led the uprisings of 1989. - Gorbachev’s Leadership and Soviet Weakness - When Mikhail Gorbachev took power, he inherited the burden of the Afghan War. - Thousands of Soviet soldiers died, creating resentment (bitterness) at home. - Unlike previous leaders, Gorbachev did not suppress revolts in Eastern Europe. - Hardliners in Moscow opposed him and began plotting his downfall. - Religious Revival and Resistance to Communism - Communism suppressed religion, but the people of the East Bloc never accepted atheism. - 1970s: People reopened churches that had been shut since Stalin’s rule. - 1980s: Many young Catholics became priests, and spiritual movements like ISKCON and Ananda Marga gained popularity. - The rediscovery of religion gave people strength to resist communism. - Reagan’s Economic War and Western Support - Ronald Reagan, US President (1981–1989), was determined to defeat communism. - He launched an economic war against the USSR and the East Bloc, making communist goods uncompetitive. - Western Europe fully supported the US, further weakening Eastern economies. - Pope John Paul II, a Polish leader of the Catholic Church, encouraged religious revival, strengthening the resistance. - Conclusion - Communism in Eastern Europe collapsed because it was imposed, economically unsustainable, and ideologically rejected. - The youth, religious revival, economic failures, and external pressures combined to bring down 44 years of communist rule in a remarkably short time. - The Fall of the USSR - - The Collapse of Communism in the USSR - Communism collapsed at an astonishing speed in the world’s first communist country, the USSR, with minimal bloodshed. Freedom-loving people across the world witnessed this historic event unfold. - Political Reasons for the Fall of the USSR - Gorbachev’s Reforms and the Weakening of the Communist Party - Gorbachev introduced far-reaching reforms that reduced the Soviet Communist Party’s dominance. - In January 1987, he initiated steps toward democracy within the party. - By 1988, the old 1,450-member Supreme Soviet was replaced by a 2,250-member Congress of People’s Deputies. This body elected a new 450-member Supreme Soviet, whose chairman would serve as the Head of State. - In December 1989, the guarantees of the Communist Party were abolished (put to an end). - The Youth and Boris Yeltsin’s Radicalism - Soviet youth demanded faster reforms. - Boris Yeltsin, a popular leader, advocated (publicly supported or recommended) for a Western-style, market-oriented democracy. - Though Gorbachev was open to reform, he sought a gradual transition, advised by Yegor Ligachev. - This created a dangerous split within the Communist Party. - Radical Discontent and Street Protests - In the May 1989 elections, radicals (far reaching or thorough) failed to win enough seats in the Congress of People’s Deputies. - Yeltsin himself was unable to get elected. - Frustrated, he turned to mass demonstrations, using Gorbachev’s policy of glasnost against him. - Rising Nationalism in Soviet Republics - Republics such as Georgia, Lithuania, and Uzbekistan sought independence. - Many had strong underground pro-independence movements for decades. - Inspired by the fall of the East Bloc, nationalist movements gained momentum. - Yeltsin championed Russian independence, undermining (sabotaging / weakening) the USSR’s unity. - Soviet Defeat in Afghanistan - The USSR’s military defeat in Afghanistan shattered its image of invincibility. - The Afghan Mujahideen’s resistance inspired Soviet citizens to challenge their own system. - The Soviet people’s conviction (declaration of someone guilty) that communism could collapse was reinforced by the events in Eastern Europe in 1989. - Economic Reasons for the Fall of the USSR - Economic Decline and Recession - Gorbachev’s economic reforms failed to boost growth. - By 1990, the national income plummeted, worsening into 1991. - Some economists compare the USSR’s economic crisis in 1990 to the Great Depression in the US during the 1930s. - The Law on State Enterprises Backfires - Gorbachev’s law made wages directly proportional to output, but it had unintended consequences. - Workers prioritized luxury goods over essentials, leading to scarcity (shortage). - Basic necessities became difficult to find, resulting in long queues outside stores. - Widespread Strikes and Public Discontent (dissatisfaction) - Mass strikes erupted across the USSR. - Citizens openly questioned when their living standards would improve—an unprecedented event in a communist state. - It became evident (clearly seen or understood) that the Communist Party was losing control. - Burden of the Arms Race with the USA - The USSR struggled to keep pace with the US in military advancements. - Obsolete (out of date) Soviet weaponry required massive funding for upgrades. - Essential funds were drained into defense, worsening economic hardship. - Loss of Economic Support from Eastern Europe - The USSR’s preferential (privileged) trade agreements with Eastern European countries ended after 1989. - This deprived the USSR of crucial economic advantages. - Additionally, countries like India, which once provided lucrative trade opportunities (such as rupee-based tea purchases resold for dollars), began their own economic reforms in the early 1990s, further weakening Soviet financial stability. - The Final Collapse of the USSR - Gorbachev Caught Between Radicals and Conservatives - Gorbachev found himself trapped between two opposing factions: - Radicals, led by Boris Yeltsin, who demanded rapid reforms. - Conservatives, led by Yegor Ligachev, who resisted change. - By July 1990, many Soviet republics began demanding independence. - Massive demonstrations erupted in Latvia and Lithuania, while Georgia unilaterally declared independence. - Gorbachev opposed using military force to suppress these uprisings. - He proposed a voluntary union granting the republics greater autonomy. - The leaders of the 15 Soviet republics agreed to sign a treaty on 20 August 1991. - The August 1991 Coup Attempt - Vice-President Gennady Yanayev and other hardliners planned a coup to prevent the treaty from being signed. - On 18 August, while Gorbachev was on holiday in Crimea, coup plotters held him at gunpoint and demanded his resignation. - When Gorbachev refused, they placed him under house arrest and sent tanks and soldiers into Moscow. - The public was falsely informed that Gorbachev was ill and a new committee was in charge. - They declared a state of emergency, banned demonstrations, and attempted to suppress opposition through military intimidation. - Yeltsin Leads the Resistance - The coup leaders underestimated the power of the people and failed to arrest Yeltsin. - Seizing the opportunity, Yeltsin rushed to the Russian Parliament (White House) and gathered a massive crowd. - In a historic moment, he climbed onto a tank in open defiance of the coup leaders. - The soldiers refused to fire on him or the protesters. - Yeltsin emerged as the symbol of resistance, and public support for the coup crumbled (fall apart). - The Coup Collapses - Confusion and chaos spread across the USSR for two days. - Hardline communists initially celebrated, removing Gorbachev’s portraits and destroying books on glasnost and perestroika. - Lenin and Stalin’s images reappeared in government buildings. - An Indian newspaper editorial described the situation as the “Dance of Fossils.” - On 21 August, overwhelmed (have a strong emotional effect on) by peaceful resistance, the coup leaders surrendered to Yeltsin. - Four top coup leaders were later sentenced to death, though the USSR collapsed before their sentences were carried out. - Gorbachev returned to Moscow, but his authority was irreparably weakened. - The Final Collapse of the USSR - The coup was the final blow to the Soviet Communist Party, which was now discredited (lost respect). - Gorbachev resigned as General Secretary of the party, and Yeltsin banned it in Russia. - Relations between Gorbachev and Yeltsin soured further. - Yeltsin declared Russia’s independence and encouraged other republics to do the same. - On 1 December 1991, Ukraine declared its independence, sealing the fate of the USSR. - Finally, on 25 December 1991, Gorbachev resigned, marking the official end of the Soviet Union. - With political unrest, economic decline, and growing nationalist movements, the USSR disintegrated, marking the end of communist rule in the Soviet Union. - Polity and economy in the contemporary world - The 20th Century: A Paradox of Progress and Struggle - Economic Disparity - The century witnessed unprecedented prosperity for a few but widespread poverty for the majority. - Despite economic slumps and depressions, the rich grew richer, while the poor suffered more. - Fall of European Imperial Powers - At the start of the century, European empires dominated global politics. - Within 50 years, these empires collapsed, replaced by a world divided between two superpowers. - Rise of the Superpowers - Unlike colonial empires, the new superpowers exerted (apply) influence through nuclear strength and economic leverage. - The USA emerged as the largest economic power after World War I. - USA’s Global Dominance - After World War II, the USA became the strongest political and economic power in the West. - Following the Cold War, it remained the world’s sole superpower, influencing global decision-making. - The Rise of Globalization and America’s Economic Dominance - Early Globalization (Late 19th Century) - Globalization flourished as industrialized nations like the USA, Britain, Germany, and France produced most of the world’s manufactured goods. - Meanwhile, Asia, Africa, and Latin America supplied raw materials, reinforcing economic dependence. - Colonial Economic Control - Imperial powers carved the world into zones of influence: - USA dominated Latin America. - Britain and France controlled Asia and Africa. - Colonizers dictated agricultural production, ensuring cash crops like cotton were grown in regions like Uganda, Sudan, and Mozambique to serve their industries. - Native producers had no control over pricing, which favored buyers from industrialized nations. - America’s Economic Supremacy - By 1900, the USA surpassed Britain, producing twice the amount of coal, iron, and steel. - By World War II, US income levels were: - Twice that of Britain - Seven times that of the USSR - After the war, America left all European economies far behind, cementing its global economic dominance. - America’s Rise to Economic Dominance - World War I and America’s Economic Advantage - The war disrupted global trade, forcing countries to turn to America for essential supplies. - German submarine threats halted merchant shipping, cutting off Europe’s exports. - After the war, a capital crunch in Britain and France and Germany’s devastation solidified America’s role as the leading global supplier. - The biggest economic winner of World War I was the United States. - War Loans and the Great Depression - European powers borrowed heavily from the US to fund their war efforts. - High-interest payments slowed Europe’s economic recovery. - The US reinvested in Germany’s post-war recovery, linking European and American economies. - When the Great Depression struck, it devastated economies worldwide, leaving: - 25 million unemployed in America - Over 50 million jobless across Europe, Africa, and Asia - World War II and America’s Economic Boom - Initially, American companies profited by selling food, essentials, and arms to Britain. - When America entered the war, its economy expanded massively. - Post-war, Europe’s economy was shattered, while America had a cash surplus. - The Marshall Plan was introduced to: - Rebuild Europe as a key market for US goods. - Prevent Western Europe from falling under communist influence. - Steps Toward European Economic Unity - American support encouraged Western Europe to pursue economic integration. - Under the Marshall Plan, the Organisation of European Economic Cooperation (OEEC) was established. - This promoted economic planning and cooperation, discouraging protectionism. - Simultaneously, NATO gave Europe a new perspective on peace and collaboration. - These efforts laid the foundation for European economic unity. - Evolution of European Economic Unity - Robert Schuman and the Idea of Economic Cooperation - French Foreign Minister Robert Schuman (1886-1963) proposed sectoral economic unity. - He convinced European nations to integrate steel and coal production. - In 1951, France, West Germany, Italy, and the Benelux countries (Belgium, Netherlands, Luxembourg) formed the European Coal and Steel Community (ECSC). - The ECSC led to remarkable growth: - Coal production increased by 23% (1955). - Iron and steel production rose by nearly 150%. - Its success encouraged economic integration in other sectors. - Formation of the European Economic Community (EEC) - In 1957, the Treaty of Rome established the European Economic Community (EEC), also called the Common Market. - Objectives: - Free movement of capital and labor. - Elimination of customs duties on member states’ goods. - Balance between wages and social benefits. - Governance: - Council of Foreign Ministers. - High Commission of technocrats. - Expansion and Britain’s Entry - By 1968, all tariff barriers among the six founding nations were removed. - Trade and labor migration increased, inspiring other nations. - European Free Trade Area (EFTA) was formed by Britain, Denmark, Norway, Sweden, Switzerland, Austria, and Portugal. - Britain initially rejected joining the EEC but reconsidered in 1961. - Despite French opposition, in 1973, Britain, Ireland, and Denmark joined the EEC. - Towards a Common European Currency - The EEC began discussing monetary integration, but: - Britain, Norway, and Sweden feared it would weaken their economies. - In 1988, members agreed to create a European Free Trade Zone by 1992. - The 1991 Treaty of Maastricht laid the groundwork for: - A strong central bank. - A unified European currency. - After seven years of parliamentary ratifications, in 2000, the Euro became the common currency (except Britain, which retained the Pound). - Impact of the European Union (EU) - The EEC evolved into the European Union (EU)—the world’s first successful trade bloc (alliance). - Achievements: - Removed trade barriers among member states. - Strengthened collective economic resources. - Inspired regional trade alliances: - NAFTA (North America). - Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC). - ASEAN (Southeast Asia). - The SAARC Dilemma in South Asia - Despite similar aspirations, SAARC (South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation) has failed to integrate South Asia into a common market. - Member nations: India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Sri Lanka, Nepal, Bhutan, Maldives. - Key obstacle: Pakistan’s hostility toward India remains the biggest roadblock. - The World Trade Organisation (WTO) - Origins: The General Agreement on Trade and Tariffs (GATT) - After World War II, major economic powers negotiated trade rules to reduce trade barriers and settle disputes. - These rules were established as the General Agreement on Trade and Tariffs (GATT), headquartered in Geneva, Switzerland. - Several rounds of multilateral trade negotiations were held under GATT. - The Uruguay Round (1986-1993) - The eighth round of GATT, known as the Uruguay Round, started in 1986 in Punta del Este, Uruguay, involving 117 countries. - It lasted till 15 December 1993 and led to the most comprehensive trade liberalization agreement. - The negotiations exposed tensions between: - The North (developed nations) and the South (developing nations). - The world’s biggest trading nations and newly freed economies. - Key Issues Debated in the Uruguay Round - Subsidies - Governments subsidizing industries make their products cheaper, creating unfair competition. - Example: European agriculture was heavily subsidized, leading to objections from the U.S. - After intense debate, poor countries were allowed limited subsidies, but hidden subsidies still persist. - Patents - Developed countries demanded patent protection, preventing national companies from copying manufacturing processes. - Issue: Many developing countries produce and sell cheap generic medicines based on patented drugs. - Concern: If multinational corporations monopolize patents, life-saving drugs would become unaffordable for developing nations. - Quotas - Many poor nations rely on quotas (especially in textiles) to secure foreign exchange earnings. - Multi-Fibre Agreement (MFA) ensured minimum import quotas for poor countries. - However, rich nations opposed quotas, arguing they hindered free trade. - The agreement was extended but scheduled to end by 2005. - Dispute Settlement - Earlier, trade disputes led to expensive, time-consuming legal battles. - The Uruguay Round established a transparent dispute settlement mechanism under the WTO for fast resolution of trade conflicts. - Non-Tariff Barriers - Labour Wages: - Developed countries argued that low wages in the South resulted in lower production costs, making their own industries uncompetitive. - They demanded uniform labour standards, but developing nations resisted, citing economic constraints. - Environmental Concerns: - Developed countries claimed that manufacturing in the South caused environmental degradation. - They proposed banning goods made through environmentally unsafe processes. - Developing nations argued this was a protectionist move by rich countries. - The Uruguay Round ignored environmental concerns, but the debate continues. - Most Favoured Nation (MFN) Status - WTO members agreed to treat each other as Most Favoured Nations (MFN), ensuring non-discrimination in trade policies. - However, many countries were already part of regional trade blocs (e.g., EEC, NAFTA, GCC, ASEAN), granting preferential trade rights to their neighbours. - This conflict between WTO rules and regional agreements remains unresolved. - Formation of the WTO (1995) - GATT was rebranded as the World Trade Organisation (WTO) and officially started on 1 January 1995. - Responsibilities of the WTO: - Administers trade agreements and treaties. - Monitors trade policies and statistics. - Settles trade disputes among member nations. - The Third World and the North-South Divide - Origin of the Term ‘Third World’ - During the 1950s, the term ‘Third World’ was widely used to describe countries that were: - Neither part of the highly industrialized West (First World) - Nor part of the rapidly industrializing Communist Bloc (Second World) - These were mostly newly independent nations, previously isolated from global economic activity. - By 1970, the Third World included most of Africa, Asia, and Latin America. - Economic Disparities - Despite housing over 70% of the world’s population, the Third World consumes less than 30% of global food production. - In contrast, the United States, with less than 10% of the world’s population, consumes 40% of global agricultural production. - Recognizing these disparities, world leaders began addressing the issue in the 1970s. - The Brandt Report and the North-South Divide - In 1980, former West German Chancellor Willi Brandt and former British Prime Minister Edward Heath published the Brandt Report. - The report rejected the First-Second-Third World classification and instead divided the world into: - The North – Developed industrial nations (North America, Europe, former USSR, Japan, Australia, New Zealand). - The South – The underdeveloped and developing nations (Third World). - The report confirmed that the North was getting richer while the South was becoming poorer. - By 1989-90, the Gross National Product (GNP) of the North was 24 times greater than that of the South. - Neo-Colonialism and Economic Exploitation - Many Southern countries remain economically dependent on their former colonial rulers, a phenomenon known as neo-colonialism. - African nations are particularly affected, still relying on one-product economies set up by imperial powers: - Ghana depends on cocoa. - Zambia depends on copper. - A decline in the world price of these commodities causes severe economic crises in these countries. - Perpetual (never ending or changing) Indebtedness of the South - While commodity prices fluctuate (vary), the cost of manufactured goods from the North keeps rising, worsening the South’s trade imbalance. - As a result, Southern nations frequently borrow from the North to afford these imports, increasing their debt burden. - The World Bank estimates that the total loan burden of the South has reached $900 billion. - Some nations are even forced to borrow money just to pay interest on their previous loans, trapping them in a cycle of debt dependency. - Challenges and Industrial Growth in the South - Particular Problems of the South - The Southern world faces multiple challenges, the most pressing being: - Rapid Population Growth: - The population in the South is increasing at a much faster rate than in the North, leading to more mouths to feed each year. - In contrast, the North experiences negligible or even negative population growth. - Conflicts and Instability: - Many Southern nations are plagued by ongoing conflicts, which hinder economic development. - Resources that could be used for human development are instead spent on military expenditures. - Some of the worst conflicts have occurred in Ethiopia, Eritrea, Nicaragua, Guatemala, Lebanon, Congo, Sudan, and Liberia. - Environmental Vulnerability: - Many countries in the South are highly susceptible (likely or liable to be influenced or harmed by something) to natural disasters such as droughts and floods. - These disasters frequently devastate their economies, worsening poverty and instability. - Industrialisation in the South - The industrial landscape of the South is diverse, with some countries experiencing significant economic progress: - Oil-Rich Nations: - Some countries in the South possess vast oil reserves, which have led to substantial economic growth. - The income from oil sales has notably improved their per capita Gross National Product (GNP). - Rapidly Industrialising Nations: - Countries like South Korea, Taiwan, Malaysia, Indonesia, and Singapore underwent rapid industrialisation during the 1960s and 1970s. - This led to a significant rise in living standards for their populations. - Economic Strength of the ‘Tiger’ Economies: - The Southeast Asian ‘Tiger’ economies now have GNP levels comparable to many European nations. - They have attracted substantial foreign investments from the North. - Some Northern companies have even relocated entire production facilities to these countries due to lower costs and business-friendly policies. - Impact of Industrialisation on the Environment - Environmental Degradation - Industrialisation prioritises wealth over the environment. - Leads to overuse of resources, pollution, and ecosystem damage. - Fossil fuels (coal, oil, gas) are being rapidly depleted. - Experts predict oil shortages by the mid-21st century. - Resource Depletion - Metals like tin, lead, copper, zinc, mercury are running out. - Southern economies suffer due to dependence on resource exports. - Northern consumption patterns influence global demand. - Sustainable Development - Earth Summit 1992 introduced Sustainable Development. - Focus on fair wealth distribution and resource management. - Self-discipline and sustainable consumption are key. - Sustainable Consumption - Simple living reduces environmental harm. - “Think global, live local” discourages unnecessary transportation. - Vegetarianism and eco-friendly products are gaining support. - Alternative Energy - Ethanol from sugar is used in vehicles. - Nuclear power, if safe, is a good alternative to coal. - Deforestation and Biodiversity Loss - Half of the world’s forests were lost by 1987. - 80,000 sq. km of forests vanish annually. - Many species are extinct due to habitat destruction. - Climate Change & Pollution - CFCs from fridges/ACs damage the ozone layer. - Global warming melts polar ice caps, causing flood risks. - Agriculture crisis due to land shortage and toxic fertilizers. - Water Scarcity & Future Conflicts - Freshwater sources are drying up. - Deforestation affects the natural water cycle. - Interstate & international water disputes are rising. - Solutions & Challenges - Industries need subsidies to adopt green technology. - Farmers require aid for organic farming. - Wastewater recycling is essential but costly. - Polluting industries must be closed with compensation. - People must shift from greed to need for true sustainability. - Political Currents in the Post-Cold War World - Rise of Democracy - The French Revolution (1789) introduced democracy, equality, and fraternity. - By the 21st century, democracy became the preferred form of government. - Dictatorships, military regimes, and one-party rule are being replaced. - The Atlantic Charter (1942) envisioned a world dominated by democracy. - Expansion of Suffrage - Initially, voting rights were limited to wealthy men. - Over time, all adult males gained suffrage, but women were excluded. - Now, except for a few Arab nations, women have equal voting rights. - Global Democratisation - Most UN member states are now democracies. - Pakistan and China remain exceptions. - Afghanistan (2001) chose democracy after the fall of the Taliban. - Iraq embraced democracy after Saddam Hussein was overthrown. - Economic Impact of Democratisation - Governments have shifted from control to facilitation of economies. - The private sector now plays a leading role. - Cold War and Dictatorships - Superpowers supported dictators in strategic countries. - US-backed coups led to brutal interventions. - Chile (1973): US supported a coup against Salvador Allende. - Pakistan: US supported General Pervez Musharraf. - Growing Spirit of Freedom - Today, leaders and world powers are openly criticised for wrongdoings. - A global spirit of freedom and accountability is emerging. - Terrorism – The 21st Century’s Biggest Scourge - 9/11 – A Defining Moment - On 11 September 2001, terrorists hijacked two passenger planes and crashed them into the World Trade Centre in New York. - Hundreds of innocent people from various nations were killed. - The attack highlighted that wars in the 21st century would not be fought on battlefields but through surprise terrorist attacks. - Terrorism as a Tool of Struggle - Often seen as an expression of frustration by oppressed groups. - Lacking the power for conventional war, some organisations engage in covert (secret) warfare. - The phrase “one man’s terrorist is another man’s freedom fighter” reflects this perspective. - Example: The British labeled Indian revolutionaries like Bhagat Singh, Chandrashekhar Azad, Surya Sen, and Bagha Jatin as terrorists. - The Distinction Between Revolutionaries and Terrorists - Indian revolutionaries targeted only the military-civil establishment, not innocent civilians. - Modern terrorism, starting post-World War II, often targets innocent people. - Example: 1972 Munich Olympics attack, where Palestinian gunmen killed Israeli athletes. - Attacks on civilians, hijackings, and bombings qualify as terrorism, not a freedom struggle. - United Nations and the Fight Against Terrorism - The Challenge of Defining Terrorism - A precise definition of terrorism remains elusive. - Some wrongly blame fundamentalist (one who believes in strict, literal interpretation of religious scripture) Islam for the rise in terrorism. - Islamic fundamentalism grew in reaction to: - Neo-colonial powers imposing foreign lifestyles on Muslim countries. - U.S. support for Israel, leading to widespread animosity (strong hostility / enmity) in the Islamic world. - Radical groups spreading a cult of hate under slogans like “Islam is in danger.” - Terrorist groups like Al-Qaeda and Intifada attract unemployed, semi-literate youth to the gun culture. - United Nations’ Efforts Against Terrorism - Since the 1972 Munich Olympics attack, the UN has been working to combat terrorism. - It has passed 12 international conventions to address terrorism-related issues: - Convention on Offenses Committed on Aircraft (1963) - Convention for the Suppression of Unlawful Seizure of Aircraft (1970) - Convention for the Suppression of Acts Against Civil Aviation Safety (1971) - Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes Against Internationally Protected Persons (1973) - Convention Against the Taking of Hostages (1979) - Convention on the Physical Protection of Nuclear Material (1980) - Protocol on Violence at Airports (1988) - Convention for the Suppression of Unlawful Acts Against Maritime Navigation (1988) - Protocol on the Safety of Fixed Platforms on the Continental Shelf (1988) - Convention on the Detection of Plastic Explosives (1991) - International Convention for the Suppression of Terrorist Bombing (1997) - International Convention for the Suppression of Terrorism Financing (1999) - The Need for a Comprehensive Convention - Despite these efforts, no single UN convention defines terrorism universally. - In 1995, India proposed a Comprehensive Convention on International Terrorism. -
