The treaty aimed to unify global efforts against terrorism, but progress has stalled (stopped). - Opposition comes from: - Human rights groups, fearing misuse against political opponents. - Some West Asian countries, arguing over the distinction (contrast) between legitimate movements and terrorism. - India’s Battle Against International Terrorism - India has been a major victim of cross-border terrorism. - Over one million Indian civilians, mostly from poor communities, have suffered. - Terrorism in India is often linked to external support for secessionist (formwal withdrawal from federation or body) movements, including: - Nagaland (North-East India) - Punjab - Jammu & Kashmir (North-West India) - Oppressed People’s Movements - - The People’s Century and Struggles for Rights - The 20th century is often called the People’s Century due to vast improvements in political, social, and economic conditions. - The fall of feudalism, empires, and rigid social hierarchies empowered people to shape their nations. - However, not all communities achieved equality easily: - Indigenous people in Latin America are still fighting for their basic rights. - The Kurdish people in Iran, Iraq, Jordan, and Turkey continue to suffer government persecution and resort to violent resistance. - India’s freedom struggle under Mahatma Gandhi became an inspiration for global movements, including the Black struggle against apartheid. - Some of these movements remain unfinished and continue to influence and motivate oppressed people worldwide. - The Civil Rights Movement in the USA - Segregation and Discrimination - The abolition of slavery (1863) did not grant equal rights to Black Americans. - While the Federal Government did not officially differentiate between races, southern states enforced discriminatory laws, such as: - Denial of voting rights in local elections. - Segregation, a system forcing Blacks and Whites to live separately, similar to South African apartheid. - Restricted access to schools, buses, restaurants, and job opportunities. - Exclusion from trade unions, keeping Black workers economically disadvantaged. - Despite these injustices, Black Americans served the U.S. in both World Wars with distinction (contrast). - The Rise of the Civil Rights Movement - Inspired by India’s independence struggle, Black Americans sought freedom from racial exploitation. - Martin Luther King Jr., a Baptist minister, emerged as the leader of a non-violent movement. - The movement was launched in December 1956 when: - Rosa Parks, a Black woman, was arrested for refusing to give up her bus seat to a White passenger in Montgomery, Alabama. - Inspired by Gandhi’s methods, King organized a bus boycott, which succeeded in ending segregated seating in Montgomery. - Expansion of the Movement - In 1957, King expanded the movement beyond Montgomery: - Founded the Southern Christian Leadership Council to spread the movement across the southern U.S.. - Used sit-in demonstrations and civil disobedience to challenge segregation. - Leveraged media to amplify Black grievances, gaining domestic and international support. - Thousands of White Americans joined, weakening the segregationist movement. - Violent resistance from White supremacist groups, such as the Ku Klux Klan, resulted in attacks on activists. - In 1963, King led a historic march of 250,000 people to Washington, D.C., where he delivered his famous “I Have a Dream” speech. - In 1967, he was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize, but was assassinated in 1968, much like Gandhi. - Rise of Militant Resistance - Not all Black activists agreed with King’s non-violent approach: - Groups like the Black Panthers and Black Power emerged in response to continued White aggression. - Ku Klux Klan violence and government inaction fueled frustration among Black youth. - Violent race riots erupted: - 1964 in Harlem, New York - 1965 in Los Angeles, where over 35 people were killed - Some Black Americans rejected Christianity, viewing it as a White man’s religion, and converted to Islam. - Cassius Clay, the 1960 Olympic Gold Medal-winning boxer, famously converted to Islam and became Muhammad Ali. - Government Reforms and Legacy - The Civil Rights Movement found support within the Democratic Party (1961–1968). - Presidents John F. Kennedy and Lyndon B. Johnson recognized historical injustices and introduced time-bound reforms to integrate Black Americans into mainstream society. - The movement left an enduring impact, setting a global precedent for non-violent resistance and human rights movements. - Social Reform Movements in 20th Century India - Introduction - India has a history of continuous social reform. - 19th-century reformers: Raja Rammohun Roy, Ishwarchandra Vidyasagar, Swami Dayananda Saraswati, Swami Vivekananda. - Their contributions: women’s rights, education, spiritual awakening, scientific temper. - However, caste-based discrimination remained a major issue. - The 20th century saw movements aimed at uplifting the downtrodden castes. - - Shree Narayana Guru (1854–1928) - Transformed the Ezhava community in Kerala. - Before his movement, Ezhavas were denied entry into temples and upper-caste homes. - Established temples with Ezhava Shivalingams and an Ezhava priesthood. - Promoted education: “Educate to be free, organize to be strong, thrive through industry.” - Simplified marriage rituals (limited to 10 guests). - Preached “One caste, one religion, one God for man.” - Opposed conversion of backward castes to Christianity and Islam. - His teachings continue through Shree Narayana Dharma Paripalana Yogam (SNDP). - Dr. Bhim Rao Ambedkar (1891–1956) - - Led a movement against untouchability. - Founded Bahishkrit Hitakarini Sabha (1924) to fight for the rights of Dalits. - Focused on education, women’s rights, and economic upliftment. - Won a case in Bombay High Court, leading to the ban on untouchability. - Established the Scheduled Castes Federation for political empowerment. - Played a key role as Chairman of the Drafting Committee of the Indian Constitution. - Ensured constitutional safeguards for the backward castes. - Conclusion - These movements reshaped Indian society. - Focused on education, self-awareness, and legal rights. - Their legacies continue to inspire social justice movements today. - Science and technology, literature, art and culture in the contemporary world - Science, Technology, and Human Development - Importance of Discoveries and Inventions - Discoveries and inventions have shaped human progress since ancient times. - The 19th and 20th centuries witnessed rapid advancements. - Inventions like electricity, antibiotics, telephone, satellites, atomic energy revolutionized life. - These innovations benefited all of humanity, not just specific nations. - Some regions lagged due to economic and political limitations. - Even today, some areas lack basic infrastructure and communication systems. - Scientific and technological progress reflects a nation’s advancement. - Technology and economic growth are interconnected. - Role of Technology in Daily Life - Technology applies scientific principles to everyday activities. - Technological progress is driven by scientific discoveries. - In earlier times, scientific advancements were localized. - In the last 200 years, discoveries spread worldwide much faster. - Energy Sources and Technological Growth - Scientific progress depends on available energy sources. - Early energy sources: firewood, wind, water. - Coal became dominant, powering engines. - Later, petroleum and natural gas improved engine efficiency. - Electricity and magnetism led to new technologies. - Electronics and atomic energy reshaped the 20th century. - Science and Technology: A Two-Way Relationship - Technology advances due to scientific discoveries. - Scientific knowledge also grows through technological innovations. - Examples: - Steam engine by James Watt. - - Telescope by Galileo. - Science and Technology in the 19th Century - The 19th century strengthened the link between science and technology. - Scientific principles were applied to technological inventions. - Examples: - Davy’s Miners Safety Lamp used heat conduction, smoke escapement, and light transmission. - Electric generator and bulb applied principles of electricity, mechanical engineering, and heat conduction. - Conclusion - Science and technology drive global progress. - Their interdependence continues to shape modern advancements. - Technology and the Scientific Revolution - The First Scientific Revolution (19th Century) - Science and technology led to the first scientific revolution in the 19th century. - New materials, energy sources, and technologies transformed industries. - Mass production became possible, leading to industrial growth. - Key industries: - Steel production - Railways - Textiles - Automobiles - Electrical equipment and gadgets - Industrial expansion occurred primarily in Europe and America. - The Second Scientific Revolution (20th Century Onwards) 1900-1910 - Plastic synthesized (Bakelite by Bakeland, 1906). - Chemotherapy introduced (Ehrlich, 1907). - Mass production of motor cars (Ford Model T, 1908). 1910-1920 - Vacuum tube for radio communication (Fleming, 1904, 1912). - Mass production of track vehicles. - Construction of the Panama Canal (50 miles long, 1914). 1920-1930 - Radio broadcasting network established (1922). - Empire State Building constructed (New York, 102 stories, 1930). - First liquid-fuel rocket developed (Goddard, 1926). 1930-1940 - Television invented (Baird, 1936). - Hydroelectric power generation (Boulder Dam, 1 million kW, 1936). - Gas turbine engine developed (Whittle and Chain, 1937). 1940-1950 - Antibiotic drugs introduced (Penicillin, Fleming & Florey, 1942). - First atomic chain reaction (Fermi and others, 1942). - First atomic bomb developed (USA, 1945). - First electronic computer built (1946). - Supersonic aircraft introduced (1947). - Transistors invented (1948). 1950-Present - Atomic power plants for peaceful use (1952). - Space exploration begins (Sputnik, USSR, 1957). - Satellite communication develops (1970s). - Biotechnology emerges (1975). - Personal computers introduced (1980s). - Computer-based communication systems revolutionize technology. - Key Scientific Advances in the 20th Century - Discovery of the electron. - Understanding atomic structure. - Development of vacuum tubes. - Electromagnetic waves and their use in communication. - Harnessing atomic energy for electricity generation. - Advancements in rocketry and communication satellites. - These developments marked the second scientific revolution, transforming the world with unprecedented technological progress. - New Sources of Energy - Non-Renewable Energy Sources - Commercial and domestic energy mainly comes from coal, oil, and natural gas. - These fuels take millions of years to regenerate, making them non-renewable. - Their reserves are depleting, raising global concerns. - There is a growing effort to find renewable and clean energy sources. - Solar Energy - - The sun radiates energy at 10²⁸ watts, but only a small fraction reaches Earth. - Solar energy can be harnessed in two ways: - As heat (Solar cookers, water heaters, solar furnaces). - Converted into electricity (Solar cells). - Solar Cells: - Discovered about 100 years ago with selenium, but it was inefficient. - First practical solar cell (1954) had 1% efficiency. - Modern solar cells (using silicon) now reach 25% efficiency. - They provide clean, self-generating electricity. - Used in artificial satellites and remote areas. - India is a pioneer (one who is first to do something) in solar energy adoption. - Wind Energy - - Used for thousands of years, but efficient technology is still developing. - Faces technological constraints in modern applications. - Hydrogen Energy - Hydrogen is a potential future energy source, but it is highly explosive. - Scientists are developing safe technologies for its use. - Jules Verne (1874) predicted that hydrogen would be a major energy source. - Alcohol as Fuel - Alcohol reacts with oxygen to produce heat. - Some countries use alcohol-petrol mixtures as automobile fuel. - Research continues to improve alcohol-based energy technology. - Atomic Energy - - Nuclear fission and fusion revealed the immense energy potential of atoms. - Nuclear reactors generate electricity using fission reactions. - Countries like France, Germany, Japan, Sweden, and others generate over 30% of their power from nuclear energy. - In India, nuclear power contributes only 3% to total electricity generation. - Transport and Communication - Development of Transport - Science and technology led to efficient transport. - New energy sources and engines revolutionized (changed radically) travel. - Types of Transport (i) Land Transport - Road: Developed with roads and wheeled vehicles. - Rail: Steam engines started the railway era. (ii) Water Transport - Crucial for economic growth. - India has 5,760 miles of navigable waterways. (iii) Air Transport - Fastest-growing mode of transport. - Reduced travel time, connecting the world. - Pipelines - Transport liquids and gases over long distances. - Used for crude oil, gas, water, and petroleum products. - Essential for urban water and gas supply. - Development of Communication - Printing technology improved in the 15th century. - Major breakthrough with Faraday’s electromagnetic induction. - Key Inventions - Telegraph (Morse): Enabled long-distance communication. - Telephone (Bell, 1876): Allowed speech communication. - - Wireless Communication (Marconi, 1901): Used electromagnetic waves. - Radio & TV (1920s-30s): Transformed mass communication. - Satellite Communication (1962): Relayed press, radio, and TV globally. - Technology as a Means of Destruction - Advancement and Misuse - Science and technology have improved human life. - However, greed and power struggles have led to destructive applications. - The 20th century saw rapid development in weapons of mass destruction. - Weapons of Mass Destruction (i) Military Research and Missiles - Many scientists are engaged in military R&D. - Technological advancements have enhanced weapons like: - Tanks, combat aircraft, missiles, and warships. - Cruise missiles: Surface-to-surface, surface-to-air, and air-to-surface. - Ballistic missiles: - Intermediate Range Ballistic Missiles (IRBM). - Inter-Continental Ballistic Missiles (ICBM). - IT developments have enabled guided missile systems. - Changing warheads from conventional to nuclear increases destruction potential. (ii) Nuclear Weapons - Development of nuclear weapons followed discoveries in radioactivity. - World War II ended with the dropping of atomic bombs: - Hiroshima (6 August 1945). - Nagasaki (9 August 1945). - Today, nuclear warheads can: - Carry 200 kilotons (compared to 12 kilotons in Hiroshima/Nagasaki bombs). - Cover distances of 1600 km. - Chemical and Biological Weapons (i) Chemical Weapons - Used in World War I: - Chlorine, phosgene, mustard gas. - Used in later conflicts: - Italy (Ethiopia, 1935-36). - Japan (China, 1937-45). - Hitler’s concentration camps (World War II). (ii) Biological Weapons - Long history of use in warfare. - Geneva Protocol (1925): Banned gases and bacteria in war. - Biological Weapons Convention (1975): Prohibited development and stockpiling. - Despite bans, covert research continues. (iii) Advanced Biological and Chemical Threats - Binary nerve agents are the most advanced chemical weapons. - Biological warfare agents include: - Viruses (Yellow fever). - Bacteria (Typhoid, plague). - Fungi (Coccidioidomycosis). - Even less advanced nations can produce chemical/biological weapons. - Role of Genetic Engineering - Provides new materials for military applications. - Can lead to dangerous developments. - Needs strong international treaties to prevent misuse. - Development in Biological Science - Early Discoveries and Beginnings - Man’s quest to understand life and fight diseases led to advancements in biological sciences. - 1897: E. Bucher, a German chemist, discovered that a cell-free yeast extract can convert sugar into alcohol, marking the start of biochemistry. - Before this, it was believed that life processes only occurred within living cells. - Virus discovery: Researchers identified viruses as the cause of diseases in plants and animals (e.g., yellow fever, polio, mumps, smallpox, influenza, common cold). - The search for medicines to cure these diseases took many decades. - Growth in Microbiology and Biochemistry (20th Century) (i) Key Discoveries - Vitamins were discovered. - The term “gene” was coined to describe the carrier of heredity. - Insulin was isolated. - Penicillin was discovered, offering strong anti-bacterial properties. - Development of sulpha drugs. - Antibiotics were further developed, reducing mortality from infectious diseases. - Intensive work in genetics and heredity also took place during this period. (ii) Breakthroughs in Microbiology - Electron microscopy and X-ray crystallography helped in understanding fundamental building blocks of living organisms. - Proteins, DNA, and RNA were identified as the essence of living beings. - These discoveries gave birth to molecular biology (a blend of biochemistry and physics). - DNA was found to be essential for detecting hereditary characteristics, carrying the genetic code. - In the early 1950s, Crick and Watson discovered the double-helix structure of DNA. - Advances in Genetics and Genetic Engineering - (i) Synthesis and Engineering - 1970: Har Gobind Khurana and his team synthesized a gene for the first time by assembling it from its component chemicals. - - Cohen and Boyer: In the following years, they established genetic engineering by demonstrating that DNA molecules could be: - Cut with restriction enzymes, - Joined with other enzymes, - Reproduced by inserting them into Escherichia Coli chromosomes. (ii) Identification of Diseases - 1980s: Identification of AIDS and isolation of the AIDS virus. - 1990s: Identification of genes related to colon cancer. (iii) Impact of Genetic Engineering - 1980s-1990s: Genetic engineering led to the development of several new proteins, including: - Human insulin. - Interleukin 2. - Blood clot dissolver. - Hepatitis B vaccine. - Significance of Genetic Engineering - Genetic engineering is regarded as one of the most important discoveries of the 20th century. - It offers powerful tools for managing health and life. - Information Technology - Overview of Information Technology - Information Technology (IT) is the science of collecting, storing, processing, and transmitting information. - It is the convergence of computer technology and telecommunications. - IT has revolutionized the way information is handled and disseminated (spread). - Developments in Computer and Telecommunications Technology (i) Advancements in Computer Technology - Mini and microcomputers have been developed for information handling. - These computers are efficient, reliable, and capable of processing information at high speeds. (ii) Advancements in Telecommunications - Developments have led to devices like telex, fax, artificial satellites, and optical fibers for near-instantaneous information transmission. - The first electronic digital computer was built in the USA in 1946; it weighed 30 tons and filled two car garages. - Modern microelectronics and semiconductors now contain the same computing power in a pea-sized silicon chip. (iii) Convergence of Technologies - Telecommunication networks now use digital signals (like in computing), facilitating the convergence of electronics, computing, and telecommunications. - This convergence is revolutionizing Information Technology and has brought dramatic changes in how we live, work, and even think. - IT in Everyday Life (i) Household Applications - Microchips are used in home appliances like washing machines, food processors, and VCRs. - Personal computers are increasingly used in homes for various purposes. (ii) Educational Use - Computers are used for educational purposes in schools. - Students are also learning hardware and software technologies to build professional careers. - Applications of Information Technology - IT is replacing paperwork and improving productivity and customer service. - It is extensively used in industries like transport, banking, architecture, publishing, and healthcare. - It aids scientists in weather forecasting and many other fields. - Global Impact - The applications of IT are vast and have impacted human societies globally in numerous ways. - Culture in the Contemporary World - Advancements in Art and Culture - The pace of progress in art and culture has accelerated over the last two centuries. - Earlier, intellectual expression was limited to the courts and religious institutions, relying on their patronage. - The majority practiced simpler, unsophisticated forms of expression, known as folk art, especially in India. - Technological advancements in print and electronic media have made mass reproduction of artistic creations possible, making literature (poetry, drama, novels) and various arts accessible to the public. - The rise of satellite television and the Internet has added new dimensions to culture, making it a multi-dimensional concept that reflects broader societal changes. - Defining Culture - According to Robert Redfield, culture is “an organised body of conventional understanding manifest in art and artefacts, which persists through tradition and characterizes a human group.” - Culture involves acquired behaviors that are shared and transmitted among members of society. - Mathew Arnold defines culture as “the acquainting of ourselves with the best that has been known and said in the world.” - In the era of globalisation, culture is no longer confined to local features or elite groups but encompasses broader, interconnected global trends. - Dual Cultural Tradition - People today experience dual cultural traditions: - One tradition focuses on local features, often not sanctioned by scriptures and widely accepted by the community. - The other, embodied in literature and refinement of thought, is typically followed by an elite group. - Examples include Stalin’s illness, where the Russian Church was asked to pray for him, and Mao Zedong presiding over Buddhist celebrations during the Cultural Revolution. - Culture’s role is crucial in the evolution of societies. - Globalisation and Cultural Uniformity - Globalisation has impacted all cultures, promoting a form of cultural uniformity worldwide. - Some view globalisation as a subtle form of neo-colonialism, where the economic flow to developed countries continues even after their political independence, replacing political or military control with economic domination. - While this aspect of globalisation persists, newly independent states have responded with self-assertion, promoting democratic processes and global cooperation. - New Trends in the Post-1945 World - The contemporary period, especially since 1945, has seen five major trends: - An increase in the number of independent states. - The acquisition and development of nuclear weapons by various nations. - Increased exposure of states to external and internal subversions. - The influence exerted by superpowers. - A rise in the influence of non-state actors. - The ‘South’ countries face challenges such as aggressive nationalism, population growth, economic backwardness, and political instability. - The ‘North-South’ divide has widened, with the North growing more developed and the South becoming increasingly dependent on foreign aid. - The New International Economic Order (NIEO) - The UN Declaration on NIEO (May 1974) marked a significant milestone, calling for a global partnership based on equity and mutual benefit. - The Brandt Commission Report (1980) emphasized that a New Order of international relations benefits all nations. - Mass media and public opinion now play a critical role in accountability in international diplomacy. - The Global Village and the Impact of Globalisation - With advancements in mass media and computing, the concept of a global village is no longer distant. - Globalisation is pervasive (penetrating / prevalent), as seen in the global popularity of films, sports events, and human-interest stories. - Figures like Nelson Mandela and Mother Teresa are revered globally, embodying a universal humanism that transcends specific cultural or philosophical boundaries. - This new cultural ethos blends Eastern universalism with Western individualism to form a global humanistic perspective. - Contemporary World Literature - European Literature - Social Background - The 50 years before WWI saw remarkable economic growth and scientific discoveries. - This created material wealth and shifted society from an aristocratic, semi-feudal, humanistic, agrarian order to a middle-class, democratic, mechanistic, and urban one. - By 1900, there were 11 metropolises with populations over a million. London, Berlin, and Paris were at the center of a global economic network. - This led to the rise of modern, individualistic thinking, with modernism emerging as an urban art. The city became the cultural hub. - Global Impact and Reaction - Due to imperialism and colonialism, these changes affected distant regions of the world. - World literature began to react to industrialization and material advancement, with Western powers suffering in WWI, WWII, and the Great Depression. - Russia and China underwent revolutions, while nationalism and independence movements rose in Asia, Africa, and America. - Marxism, socialism, and the discovery of a Godless world emerged as significant consequences. - The world grappled with the paradox of material affluence and emotional sterility, as the West’s industrial awakening ironically led to the decline of its colonial aspirations. - The Writer’s Response - The writer, particularly in the West, faced confusion, spiritual bankruptcy, and the disintegration of traditional values. - This search for meaning led to new forms of expression in response to a society in flux. - Poetry - Modern poetry began with Baudelaire’s “unreal city,” where crowds symbolized loneliness. - German expressionist poets like Rilke and George saw the city as a place of madness and disinheritance. - Rimbaud’s language and imagery made him the first true modern poet. - Poets like Yeats, Eliot, Mallarmé, Montale, and Lorca struggled with the tension between tradition and innovation. - Free verse, with uneven lines and no rhyme, emerged as a popular form, starting with Whitman and Ezra Pound and mastered by T.S. Eliot in “The Waste Land.” - Eliot’s “The Four Quartets” exemplified the poem-as-play form. - Novel - The 19th century established the novel as a literary medium of great variety and depth. - Henry James first used the novel as an “art form” in works like “The Americans,” where form and content were inseparable. - Modern novelists, like Joseph Conrad and James Joyce, expanded this fusion of form and content. - Sigmund Freud’s “The Interpretation of Dreams” influenced many, with Joyce using the stream of consciousness method in “Ulysses.” - - Virginia Woolf followed Joyce’s lead, while Proust’s “Remembrance of Things Past” explored consciousness and the past. - Many modern novels portrayed the artist as a character, as seen in the works of Thomas Mann and André Gide. - Novels by Malraux, Sartre, Camus, and Kafka presented symbolic representations of life. - Even traditional novelists like D.H. Lawrence and E.M. Forster employed symbolism in works like “The Rainbow” and “A Passage to India.” - - Drama - Modern drama, shaped by technical and linguistic diversity, was best represented by Henrik Ibsen. - Ibsen’s “The Doll’s House” projected contemporary themes using symbols, alongside Strindberg’s expressionistic dream-plays and Chekhov’s surface dialogue pointing to hidden realities. - Maeterlinck, the Belgian playwright, became the first modernist by using silence extensively through pauses, gestures, and external sound patterns. - Chekhov followed suit with indirect dialogue in “The Cherry Orchard.” - Wedekind, the German playwright, discovered that real-life communication often lacked dialogue altogether. - The 20th century saw a break with traditional language in drama, pushing the boundaries of expression. - Modern Forms of Drama - Surrealism: Apollinaire introduced surrealism, while Antonin Artaud’s Theatre of Cruelty aimed to affect the audience deeply. - Dadaism: Dadaists emphasized nonsensical and shocking representations. - Bertolt Brecht: Brecht introduced the “alienation effect” in plays like Mother Courage, treating the stage as a stage rather than reality. - Absurd Drama: Playwrights like Beckett, Ionesco, Adamov, and Genet formed the Absurd Drama school. Their plays lacked real-life characters, presenting symbolic representations of emotions like lovelessness, loneliness, and hopelessness. - Language was often nonsensical, with monologues, pauses, gestures, and exclamations replacing dialogue. - Plays like Beckett’s Endgame were nihilistic, leaving no comforting resolution for the audience. - While Camus and Sartre portrayed rootless existence with logical dialogues, Beckett took a more imagistic, symbolic approach. - Conclusion - The 20th century literature marked a significant shift, with experimentation across genres. - Some works departed entirely from tradition, while others blended traditional techniques with new forms. - The variety and experimentation ensured a prominent place in literary history. - English Literature - Turn of the 19th Century - The late 19th century marked a period of complex change for Britain. - Rudyard Kipling’s short stories often critiqued the country’s failure to live up to its imperial ideals, rather than the imperial idea itself. - - Thomas Hardy explored the realities of empire-building and war, particularly their effects on ordinary people. - During this time, a tradition of verse emerged that focused on everyday life and used everyday language, finding its quintessential expression in the works of various poets. - Continental Influence (By 1900) - By 1900, the influence of mainland Europe was pervasive in British literature. - This influence can be seen in works such as: - Ford Madox Ford’s The Good Soldier (1915) - Somerset Maugham’s Of Human Bondage (1915) - John Galsworthy’s Villa Rubein (1900) - Continental Influence on Novelists - The aesthetic movement of the 1890s influenced James Joyce, especially in A Portrait of an Artist as a Young Man, which also showed a connection with French Naturalism. - Henrik Ibsen’s realism was another major continental influence, shaping many British novelists and playwrights. - Early 20th Century Political Liberalism - The early 20th century saw a resurgence of political liberalism, reflected in the country’s intellectual life. - Liberalism was associated with human emancipation and the pursuit of higher, finer values of life. - This intellectual movement appeared in works such as: - E. M. Forster’s Where Angels Fear to Tread (1905) - Virginia Woolf’s Mrs Dalloway (1925) and To The Lighthouse (1927) - Aldous Huxley’s Crome Yellow (1921) marked a new stage of liberal intellectual anxieties in response to the modern world. Industrialism and Rediscovery of Human Values - The shift from rural life to industrialism led to a focus on rediscovering human values: - D.H. Lawrence, in works such as Sons and Lovers (1913) and The Rainbow (1915), explored this theme. - - W.B. Yeats also reconsidered local and traditional culture in response to the perceived threat of modernity. - Poetry in the 1930s - The poets of the 1930s, influenced by Ezra Pound and T.S. Eliot, focused on satire and terse colloquialism, moving away from the symbolist side of their work. - The political poets of the 1930s, such as William Empson, George Barker, and Dylan Thomas, were known for their lucid, visionary, and apocalyptic themes. - Development of English Drama - Early 20th-century English drama reflected a complex interaction of indigenous forces and continental influence. - Henrik Ibsen’s social dramas strongly influenced: - George Bernard Shaw’s early plays, like Man and Superman (1905) and Major Barbara (1905) - John Galsworthy’s plays, such as Silver Box (1906), Strife (1909), and Justice (1910) - The most significant development in drama during this period was poetic drama, with a notable contribution from Ireland. - Post-World War II ‘New Poetry’ - After World War II, a new form of poetry emerged, characterized by common sense, a disciplined tone, craftsmanship, and sharp intelligence. - W.H. Auden’s poetry, such as About the House (1965) and City without Walls (1969), showcased interest in historical learning and linguistic virtuosity. - American Literature - Influences from 1900 to 1941 - Three major developments shaped American literature during this period: - Social Realism: Writers began addressing the pressing social issues brought about by the Industrial Revolution. - World War I and the Economic Depression: These events led many authors to become critical of the American way of life. - Psychoanalysis: Sigmund Freud’s theories opened new areas of the personality for writers to explore. - Notable Authors and Works - Ernest Hemingway: A major figure in 20th-century literature, Hemingway won admiration with works such as: - The Sun Also Rises (1926) - A Farewell to Arms (1929) - For Whom the Bell Tolls (1940) - The Old Man and the Sea (1952) - William Faulkner: Known for his stories set in the fictional Mississippi county of Yoknapatawpha, Faulkner utilized a stream of consciousness technique in novels like: - - The Sound and the Fury (1929) - His style and intricate narratives create a compelling, magical atmosphere that captivates readers. - Pearl S. Buck and William Saroyan: Both authors were associated with regionalism, capturing vivid regional details in their works. - Pearl S. Buck became famous for The Good Earth (1931), which dealt with life in China. - William Saroyan wrote about the Armenian-American community, with works like: - The Time of Your Life (1939) - The Human Comedy (1943) - American Poetry - After the death of Walt Whitman in 1892, American poetry declined sharply for about 20 years. However, A Magazine of Verse (published in Chicago by Harriet Monroe in 1912) revived interest in poetry and became a significant outlet for some of the greatest poets of the early 20th century. - Edwin Arlington Robinson and Robert Frost gained recognition through the resurgence in poetry. Frost, in particular, delved deeply into human psychology with works such as: - Mending Wall (1914) - Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening (1923) - Ezra Pound and Amy Lowell were radical poets who led The Imagists, focusing on clear, sharp imagery, everyday speech, and new rhythms. Notable works include: - Amy Lowell’s Patterns (1916) and Lilacs (1925) - Drama - Eugene O’Neill revolutionized American theatre with his exploration of realistic themes and daring stage techniques. His works include: - Beyond the Horizon (1920), a brutally realistic play. - Later works such as The Hairy Ape (1922) and Mourning Becomes Electra (1931) blend symbolism and psychological drama. - Jewish-American Literature - The conflict between traditional Jewish life and modern American society was explored, particularly by Saul Bellow, who examined this theme in works such as: - Herzog (1964) - Humboldt’s Gift (1975) - Black Literature - Harlem Renaissance (1920s): This era saw a flourishing of Black literature, with writers exploring Black American culture for the first time in depth. - Post-World War II Era: In the 1950s, Black writers gained prominence. Key works include: - Ralph Ellison’s Invisible Man (1952), considered a major post-war novel about Black life in America. - James Baldwin’s Go Tell it on the Mountain (1953). - Alex Haley’s Roots (1976), which traced 200 years of his family history from Africa to the United States, sparking popular interest in genealogy across many ethnic groups. - Latin American Literature - Colonial Influence: - During the late 1400s, settlers from southern Europe, particularly Spain and Portugal, began arriving in Latin America. - They brought their languages, religious beliefs, and customs, which had a substantial impact on the region over time. - Modernism and Poetry: - Ruben Dario (1867-1916), a Nicaraguan poet, is credited with giving modernism its form. - Latin American poets searched for the unusual, turning to exotic sources like Greek, Oriental, and Nordic mythology. - In the early 1900s, a group of women poets rose to prominence, with Gabriela Mistral from Chile winning the Nobel Prize for Literature in 1945. - Themes in Literature: - In the mid-1900s, Eduardo Mallea of Argentina expressed themes of isolation and lack of human communication in novels such as Bay of Silence (1940) and All Green Shall Perish (1941). - Early Latin American writers often explored exotic themes, such as the ancient past, distant lands, and childhood fantasies, with a focus on beauty and “art for art’s sake.” - Magical Realism: - In the 1950s, Latin American writers began experimenting with language and structure, incorporating fantasy and fragmented time and space into their works. - This style, known as Magical Realism, blended dreams and magic with everyday reality. - Gabriel García Márquez’s One Hundred Years of Solitude is a famous example of magical realism. - Russian Literature - Early 20th Century: - Soviet literature in the early 20th century was influenced by the attempts of writers and politicians to align with new ideological movements. - Early writers, raised in 19th-century literary traditions, clashed with those promoting new social and political ideas. - Tolstoy and Social Criticism: - Leo Tolstoy (1828-1910) critiqued modern society in works like Resurrection and hinted at the emergence of a new system and human order. - The Rise of Social Realism: - In the 1930s, the Union of Soviet Writers was established, endorsing the theory of social realism, which became prominent in historical literature. - One of the finest works on the Revolution and civil war during this time was Mikhail Sholokhov’s Quiet Flows the Don (1928-1940). - Famous Writers: - Anna Akhmatova and Boris Pasternak were influential writers of this era. - Pasternak’s Doctor Zhivago was first published in the Soviet Union in 1986. - Literary Movements and Political Expression: - With the installation of the Communist Party, literature began to support communist ideologies. - In the 1960s, a group of liberal writers emerged, advocating for freedom and creativity in Soviet life, though this led to increased anxiety about freedom of expression. - Soviet leader Mikhail Gorbachev’s glasnost policy in the late 1980s allowed for freer expression of ideas. - African Literature - Post-World War II and Nationalism: - After World War II, African literature gained prominence with the rise of nationalist movements across the continent. - Kwame Nkrumah, the foremost leader of the nationalist movement in Ghana, called for ‘Freedom Now.’ - Nationalism and anti-colonialism became the primary themes in African literature, with writers composing works in European languages such as English, French, and Portuguese. - Notable Writers: - Léopold Sédar Senghor and Sembène Ousmane brought Africa to the international literary stage. - Ousmane’s novels Xala and Le Mandat gained global recognition, introducing dynamic realism into African novels. - Apartheid and its Impact: - The policy of apartheid in South Africa hindered writers, but they used literature as a means of resistance. - Denis Brutus reflected the harsh conditions of apartheid in his poems, which described imprisonment and exile. - Alex La Guma also gained fame for his poignant stories published in Black Orpheus. - Nigeria’s Literary Contribution: - Nigeria emerged as a leading force in African literature, with writers like Wole Soyinka, Chinua Achebe, Gabriel Okara, and Christopher Okara making significant contributions. - Gabriel Okara, poet and novelist, received the Commonwealth Poetry Award for his collection The Fisherman’s Invocation. He experimented with language by blending English with his local dialect. - Chinua Achebe: - Chinua Achebe played a pivotal role in popularizing African literature globally. - His important works include Things Fall Apart, A Man of the People, Girls at War and Other Stories (1972), and the famous poetry collection Beware Soul and Brothers. - Other African Writers: - Ngũgĩ wa Thiong’o, a revolutionary writer from Kenya, made his mark with his novel Weep Not Child (1964). - Meja Mwangi focused on the struggles of the poor and downtrodden in his works. - Post-Independence Shift: - After the independence of African nations, there was a thematic shift in African literature. - The focus moved from colonial experiences and cultural conflicts to exploring human discontent and aspirations. - Australian Literature - Late 19th Century and Early 20th Century: - The last 20 years of the 19th century saw a rise in nationalism and a move towards the federation of separate states. - In the first 30 years of the 20th century, literary works focused on the plight of ordinary people and the need for social reform. - Prominent writers like Katherine Susannah Prichard, Louis Stone, and Edward Dyson wrote about city life and the struggles of workers. - The life of the Aboriginal people and their relationship with the white man was also portrayed by writers like Aeneas Gunn, Xavier Herbert, and Katherine Susannah Prichard. - Poetry in the Early 20th Century: - The success of poets was limited, but their works showed a diversity of aims and styles. - Jotun Shaw Neilson wrote fine, delicate lyrics, while Victor Daley created romantic poems and sharp satires. - Post-World War II Literature: - After World War II, literary magazines proliferated, and the number of readers grew. - From the 1950s onward, Australian writers became increasingly speculative and introspective. - The most influential mid-20th-century novelist was Patrick White, whose major novels were distinctly Australian, but whose treatment of themes transcended nationalistic boundaries. - Short stories continued to be popular, though they maintained a strong rural bias. - Autobiographical Writing and Poetry: - Of particular interest were the autobiographical writings of Hal Porter, who explored the changes in Australian society from the 1930s to the 1960s. - A tradition of descriptive poetry remained, but mid-20th-century poets dominated the literary scene. - Indian Literature - 20th Century Preoccupations - The two major concerns for Indian writers in the 20th century were national awakening and Western cultural achievements. - Western civilization, though associated with colonialism, influenced Indian writers with its ideals of humanism, freedom, and individualism. - Continuity of Tradition - Literature reflected changing societal moods, while also showing a remarkable unity despite India’s racial, religious, and linguistic diversity. - Regional traditions had local peculiarities, but remained integral to the national literary culture. - Language Influences - Persian had a significant influence on Indian literature in the past. - English continues to be an influential language in Indian literature, even after the colonial era. - Major Writers - Writers like Sri Aurobindo, Rabindranath Tagore, Premchand, Subramania Bharati, and others presented a life view rooted in Indian ethos, with some showing secular humanism influenced by the West. - Tagore, the first Asian Nobel laureate, was a major figure, contributing extensively to literature with his works like Gitanjali and Gora. - Notable Poets - Three influential poets of the early 20th century: Manmohan Ghose, Aurobindo Ghose, and Sarojini Naidu. - - Their works focused on social, religious, and political themes, making a significant societal impact. - Rabindranath Tagore - - Tagore, the first Asian Nobel laureate (1913), broke new ground in literature, especially in meter, diction, and thought. - His works, written in Bengali, reflect both national awakening and international humanism. - Notable works include Galpa Guchchha (short stories), Gora, and Ghare Baire (novels). - Subramania Bharati - A great Tamil patriot poet, Bharati revolutionized the poetic tradition in Tamil. - His poetry was deeply influenced by his political activism. - Indian Fiction - The late 19th and early 20th century saw the rise of the Indian novel, influenced by Indo-European contact. - Notable early novelists included Bankim Chandra Chatterjee, Mirza Qalich Beg, and Hari Narayan Apte. - Novels in several regional languages emerged during this period, such as Malayalam, Oriya, Sindhi, and Kannada. - Notable Novelists - Bankim Chandra Chatterjee: Known for his historical novels Anand Math and Debi Chaudhurani, which included nationalistic themes. - Icharam Suryaram Desai: Wrote Hind ane Britania, one of the earliest Indian novels with political overtones. - Vaikom Muhammad Basheer: Known for Balyakala Sakhi, a tragic tale of love. - Premchand: A significant Hindi-Urdu novelist whose works like Godan and Sevasadan reflected social issues. - - Modern Novels and Rural Life - Phanishwarnath Renu’s Maila Anchal (Hindi) and Bibhuti Bhushan Bandhyopadhyay’s Pather Panchali (Bengali) are key novels about rural life. - Sarat Chandra Chatterjee focused on middle-class conflicts in his works. - Yashpal’s Jhootha Such and Khushwant Singh’s Train to Pakistan explored the partition of India. - Mahasweta Devi and Mulk Raj Anand depicted tribal life and social-political upheavals. - - Short Story Genre - The 20th century saw the short story as a distinct genre, with major contributors like Rabindranath Tagore and Premchand. - Tagore’s short stories, such as Post-Master and Kabuliwala, explored rural life and human emotions. - Writers like Mulk Raj Anand, Saadat Hasan Manto, and Rajendra Singh Bedi brought realism to the short story form. - Ismat Chughtai’s Terhi Lakir explored social codes through the story of a Muslim girl. - Poetry - Indian poetry has a powerful and ancient tradition. In the 20th century, it adapted to nationalism, the independence struggle, and the communication revolution. - The two main phases of transition in Indian poetry were: - Romanticism in the early 20th century. - Nationalism, marked by patriotism. - Romanticism was exemplified by Tagore, whose poems had mysticism and romantic subjectivity, representing the entire culture. - Key poets included: - Toru Dutt, Manmohan Ghose, Sri Aurobindo, Sarojini Naidu, Rabindranath Tagore, Swami Vivekananda - Others: Mohammad Iqbal, Jai Shankar Prasad, Sumitra Nandan Pant, Mahadevi Verma, Ramdhari Singh Dinkar, Nirala, Subramania Bharati, and others. - - The Progressive Writers’ Movement in 1936 shifted towards realism, maintaining social protest themes. - New poets included Nagarjun, Shiv Mangal Singh Suman, Majaaz, Faiz, and others. - The modern movement became more personal and subjective, with poets like Kamala Das, Amrita Pritam, Raghubir Sahay, and Nissim Ezekiel. - - Theatre - In the 19th century, Indian theatre evolved by blending Western drama with classical folk traditions. - The national movement influenced theatre, focusing on historical and mythological themes and the middle class’s issues. - Key dramatists: Sachindranath Sengupta, Manmatha Ray, Parvati Prasad Barua, Laxminarayan Mishra. - The Indian People’s Theatre Association (IPTA) was formed in 1943 to promote New Drama and folk theatre. - One-act plays gained popularity in the mid-20th century, with writers like Bivendra Krishna Bhadra, Shaukat Thanvi, and Saadat Hasan Manto. - Modern experimentation in theatre, like in poetry, reached wider audiences. Key dramatists include Badal Sarkar, Vijay Tendulkar, Girish Karnad, Mohan Rakesh, and others. - Literature in China - The Chinese literary revolution began after the establishment of the republic in 1911, marking the end of a monarchical institution that had lasted for more than three millennia. - Ai Qing (Revolutionary Poet): - In 1936, Ai Qing, at the age of 26, revolutionized Chinese poetry. - He continued writing until 1957 when he was exiled for twenty years. - After political changes, he was rehabilitated and resumed writing poetry. - Lu Hsun (Writer): - Lu Hsun (1881-1936) was another significant figure who introduced a new trend in realistic fiction. - His notable works include Madman’s Diary and The Story of Ai Q. - His short satirical essays also played a crucial role in public awakening. - National Struggle (1937-1945): - During the period of Japanese aggression, all writers—whether leftist, rightist, or nonpartisan—participated in the national struggle. - Literature was seen as an effective tool to boost the morale of the people and help win the war against Japan. - Leftist writers played the dominant role in literary theory and production. - Ideological Remoulding (1950s): - In the 1950s, China underwent a series of ideological changes that deeply impacted the literary circles. - Some authors were creating new works, while others focused on revising traditional masterpieces. - The entire literary heritage of China was re-evaluated and reinterpreted to align with communist ideologies. - Cultural Revolution (1966-69): - During the Cultural Revolution, creative activities in literature came to a halt. - Revitalization (Early 1970s): - In the early 1970s, signs of renewed literary productivity emerged. - Japanese Literature - Western Influence on Japanese Literature: - The influence of Western ideas has significantly transformed Japanese literature, with the most notable outcome being the development of the modern novel. - Mori Ogai (1862-1922) and Natsume Soseki (1846-1916): - Both writers are key figures in imparting artistic maturity to Japanese fiction. - Soseki’s novels focused on the absurdities of life and the hollowness of middle-class society. - Influence on Poetry and Drama (1900s): - Western literature also influenced Japanese poetry and drama during this period. - Some poets continued to write in traditional forms like Tanka and Haiku, with Masaoka Shiki (1867-1902) being a prominent Haiku writer. - Longer poems and free verse became more prevalent in Japanese poetry. - Literature in the Showa Period (1926): - Literature became more associated with the working class. - Despite the Western influences, the Japanese literary tradition remained robust. - Revival of Romanticism: - At the beginning of the 20th century, Romanticism saw a revival in Japanese poetry. - Post-World War II Writers: - Prominent writers in the post-World War II era include Kafu Nagai (1879-1959) and Yasunari Kawabata (1899-1972). - Contemporary World Art - Post-Renaissance Art (17th-18th Century Europe): - Art during this period was dominated by Baroque and classical artists. - The themes of their works primarily focused on Christianity and the depiction of nature. - Impact of the French Revolution: - The French Revolution led to significant changes in socio-cultural activities. - France became the center of modern art, with Paris emerging as the focal point for most of its cultural activities, which dominated the world of art in the 19th and 20th centuries. - Artistic Movements Before Modernism: - The world saw various artistic expressions, including Classicism, Romanticism, Idealism, and Naturalism. - Modernism emerged in the 1860s, primarily through the works of Édouard Manet. - Advancement in Science and Technology: - The rapid progress in science and technology added new dimensions to art during this period. - Intellectual Churning in Europe (Late 19th Century): - Intellectuals such as philosophers, scientists, poets, psychologists, politicians, and economists became engrossed in evolving new theories across various subjects. - Henri Bergson, a French philosopher, reinforced Nietzsche’s theory by asserting that man was the master of his own destiny. This emphasized the superiority of intuition over intelligence, gaining support from scientists. - Influence of Freudian Theory: - Sigmund Freud’s theory of the subconscious influenced 19th-century artists. - The new generation of intellectuals began to believe that reality could not be depicted accurately, leading to a focus on feelings and perceptions instead of apparent truth and nature. - Modern Art in Europe - Birth of Modern Art and Impressionism: - A new artistic movement emerged in Europe, giving birth to Impressionism and its subsequent movements at the turn of the 19th century. - The term ‘modern art’ typically refers to the period from the 1860s to the 1960s, which is considered the era of modernism. - Characteristics of Modern Art: - The period in modern art describes the dominant styles and ideologies of art produced in that era, with modernism as its guiding philosophy. - Artists such as Monet, Degas, Picasso, Sisley, and Renoir led the new movement of post-impressionism, followed by Cézanne, Rodin, Kandinsky, and others. - Subsequent Art Movements: - Movements like Expressionism, Fauvism, Cubism, Futurism, Abstractionism, Dadaism, and Surrealism followed in search of forms and expressions that replaced the aesthetic sensibility of European Classicism. - This period marked a rejection of previous theories and embraced experimentation not only with formal expression but also with materials and techniques of art. - Post-Modernism and the Second Half of the 20th Century: - Experimentation continued into the second half of the 20th century, leading to the post-modernism movement in art. - Modernism reached the American continent between the two World Wars and infiltrated post-Revolutionary Russia, though it eventually succumbed to communism there. - The period between the two World Wars, marked by the German Holocaust and the formation of the USSR, somewhat shattered the European art scene. - Revival and Transformation Post-1950s: - By the 1950s-60s, European art regained its creative expression with a changed perception. - The anti-establishment sentiment continued to influence art, and progressive modernism took center stage, with New York becoming the epicenter for the art community. - Art during this period focused on defining the future and challenging present values, leading to the emergence of conceptual art. - Major Art Movements (1900-2000): - Several significant art movements took place during this period, shaping the trajectory of modern and post-modern art. - Cubism - Origins and Development: - Cubism was developed by Pablo Picasso and Georges Braque around 1908. - Though the movement was short-lived, it had a significant influence on later movements such as Orphism, Purism, Precisionism, Futurism, Constructivism, and Expressionism. - Synthetic Cubism: - The second phase of Cubism, starting after 1912, was known as Synthetic Cubism. - This phase emphasized the combination or synthesis of forms in the artwork. - Artists often incorporated materials like newspapers and tobacco wrappers, merging them with painted areas, a technique known as collage. - Fauvism - Introduction to Fauvism: - Henri Matisse (1869-1954), a fiercely independent painter, led the Fauvist movement. - The movement, which also included Maurice de Vilaminck (1876-1895) and Andre Derain (1880-1954), focused on the expressive use of color. - Artistic Philosophy: - Fauvist artists used color not only to describe subjects but to enhance their luminosity, breaking traditional color boundaries. - Surrealism - Origins and Ideology: - Surrealism is closely linked to some forms of abstract art, sharing similar origins but differing in interpretation. - Led by Andre Breton, Surrealism emerged after World War I, when artists, disillusioned by war, sought to destroy traditional art concepts. - Breton, however, aimed to give art a new meaning through abstraction. - Divisions Within Surrealism: - Two main groups emerged among surrealists, based on interpretations of the works of Sigmund Freud and Carl Jung: - Automation Group: Focused on subconscious expression. - Veristic Surrealists: Led by Salvador Dalí and Pablo Picasso, with a focus on realistic and symbolic elements. - Post-Modernism - Definition and Scope: - Post-Modernism is a broad term encompassing various disciplines, including art, architecture, music, film, literature, sociology, communication, fashion, and technology. - It emerged as a concept in academic circles around the mid-1980s, though its exact beginning remains unclear. - Philosophy and Characteristics: - Post-modernism rejects the boundaries between high and low forms of art, emphasizing parody and irony. - While Modernism believed art could provide unity and meaning in society, Post-modernism denies this notion, viewing the world as inherently meaningless and embracing experimentation with absurdity. - Theoretical Perspectives: - According to art historian Fredric Jameson, both Modernism and Post-Modernism are cultural formations that correspond to different stages of capitalism. - Modernism emerged during the market capitalism phase (mid-18th century), aligning with technological advances and realism in art. - Post-modernism reflects the era of consumer capitalism and multinational corporations, associated with advanced technologies like microprocessors. - Paradox of Post-Modernism: - Post-modernism is paradoxical in nature. Some view it as anti-modern, while others see it as a revision of modernism. - It rejects the supremacy of reason, the notion of truth, and the belief in the perfection of man. - Modern Indian Art - Bengal School and Renaissance - Bengal School’s significance in modern Indian art. - Rise of nationalism in the 19th century influenced art and literature. - Contribution of three Tagores: Gaganendra Nath, Rabindra Nath, and Abanindra Nath. - Establishment of Vishwa Bharti University by Rabindra Nath Tagore. - Art Schools and Institutions - Art schools in Madras (Chennai), Calcutta (Kolkata), and Bombay (Mumbai) established around 1850. - Bombay School became J.J. School of Art. - New universities and fine arts departments in Vishwa Bharati, M.S. University of Baroda, Benaras Hindu University, Ravindra Bharati, Chandigarh, and Delhi. - Notable Artists of the 20th Century - Nanda Lal Bose, Jamini Roy, Ramkinker Baij (Bengal). - Amrita Sher-Gill and Raja Ravi Verma contributed with Indian themes and Western techniques. - Progressive Artists Group - Post-independence, a group of artists formed the Progressive Artists Group: F. N. Souza, M.F. Hussain, J.H. Raza, and K.H. Ara. - Movement against the Bengal School and traditional values. - Delhi’s Contribution to Art - Establishment of institutions like All India Fine Arts and Crafts Society, Delhi College of Arts, Lalit Kala Academy, National Museum, and National Gallery of Arts. - Artists like B.C. Sanyal, Krishan Khanna, Dhanraj Bhagat, and Manjeet Bawa contributed to the art scene in Delhi. - Artists of the 1980s Onwards - A new group of artists emerged in the 1980s with J. Swaminathan as their leader. - Notable artists: Anjoli Ela Menon, Manjeet Bawa, Satish Gujaral, Meera Mukherjee, Dhiraj Choudhary. - Handicrafts and Handlooms Revival - Post-independence focus on India’s handicrafts and handlooms. - Figures like Kamladevi Chattopadhyay and Neelima Barua revived Indian handicrafts. - Government agencies and festivals promoted handicraft products. - State-Specific Art Forms - Handloom products: Naga shawls, Assam silk, Madhubani painting, Zari work, Chikan embroidery, and more. - Metal crafts: Brass works (U.P.), Bell metal work (M.P. and Orissa), Filigree (Orissa), and Bidri (Hyderabad). - Tribal arts: Terracotta and pottery. - Contemporary Art of America - Post-1960s Art Evolution - After the 1960s, American art shifted its identity, moving away from European influences. - New vernacular imagery emerged, drawn from popular sources and nostalgia, contrasting with the previous idealism of Action Painting. - Migration of artists, scientists, and intellectuals enriched American culture, contributing to both its economy and intellectual growth, helping the U.S. become a superpower by the late 20th century. - Major Artists - Key artists in the American art scene include Roy Lichtenstein, Jim Dine, Tom Wesselmann, and Richard Estes. - Op Art Movement - Briefly dominated the art scene with imagery based on the scientific theory of optics. - Post-Op Art Movements - Following Op Art, Perceptual, Minimalist, and Post-Minimalist painters took over the American art scene. - Folk Art Movement - The American Primitives, artists embracing a non-academic, freer style, contributed to the diversity of American art. - Their regeneration of traditional art forms added color and life to the pluralist landscape of American art. - Mass Communication - Cinema - Cinema began in the late 19th century with the Lumière Brothers’ moving picture in Paris, December 1895. - Commercial cinema started in Europe and America, and in India in 1913 with Raja Harish Chandra by Dada Saheb Phalke. - The silent film era ended in 1931 with Alam Ara, India’s first talkie. - Cinema became a major global medium of expression and is considered an art form with various national and international festivals. - It evolved from printing technology, quickly growing into a large business. - France dominated early cinema, later spreading to England and America. - Prominent directors: Sergei Eisenstein, Charles Chaplin, Akira Kurosawa, Satyajit Ray, Ingmar Bergman, Fellini, Hitchcock, Spielberg. - Newspapers - Despite technological advances, newspapers remain widely popular. - Categories include dailies (morning, afternoon, evening) and weeklies. India has national and regional editions. - Print media shapes social, political, and economic views. - Investigative journalism influences public opinion, such as the Watergate scandal. - Major international agencies: Associated Press, Reuters, AFP, Kyodo, Tass, Xinhua. - In India: United News of India, Press Trust of India. - English journalism grew with increased literacy, while regional editions serve semi-urban and rural areas. - Magazines provide extensive coverage and promote consumer awareness. - Radio and Television - Radio became a key medium in the 1920s, with the invention of the transistor making it affordable. - BBC and Voice of America were key broadcasters; All India Radio became the national agency. - In the 1990s, FM radio became popular. - Television, invented in the 20th century, saw advancements with satellite technology in the 1980s, enabling global broadcasts. - Television became both an entertainment and information source. - Cable TV offered multiple channels with global content like CNN, Discovery, and National Geographic. - Doordarshan remained India’s sole channel until the 1980s, after which private channels emerged. - The last century’s advancements in mass communication brought nations closer, yet created challenges in their use. - The 21st century brings new global challenges, emphasizing peace and coexistence. - Glossary - Ancien Regime: - The political and social system in France before the Revolution of 1789. - Anglican: - A member of the Church of England or of any Church in communion with it. - Apartheid: - Racial segregation; A policy practised by law for the complete separation of the whites, the coloured and the blacks in the Union of South Africa until February 1991. - Appeasement: - A policy formulated to pacify or satisfy a potentially troublesome adversary. - Archipelago: - A group or chain of islands. - Aristocrats: - The upper class persons of the European society. - Astrolabe: - An astronomical instrument used for measuring the altitude of the sun or stars to determine a ship’s latitude. - Atom bomb: - Bomb which derives its destructive power from the rapid release of nuclear energy by fission of heavy atomic nuclei, causing damage through heat, blast and radioactivity. - Authoritarianism: - Favouring or enforcing strict obedience to authority, especially that of the State at the expense of personal freedom. - Axis Powers: - The military alliance formed by Germany, Italy and Japan during the World War II. - Balance of Power: - A condition in which competing groups or nations have approximately equal strength. This condition is supposed to keep any one group or nation from dominating the other. It is also supposed to keep the competing groups or nations from going to war. - Bilateral: - The process of acting or reaching a decision or espousing a principle by two sides on the basis of mutual understanding or agreement. - Bipolar World: - Two superpowers dominating world politics. - Blitzkrieg: - A German word meaning lightning warfare in which there is an overwhelming all-out attack, especially a swift ground attack using armoured units and air support. - Blood and Iron policy: - Bismarck's policy for the unification of Germany adopted by the Prussian monarchy ignoring the Prussian Parliament. - Bolsheviks : - Members of the Russian revolutionary Marxist Party headed by Lenin. - Bourgeoisie: - The middle class. The word comes from a French word meaning ‘city dwellers’. - Buffer State: - A smal] country located between two or more larger countries. The existence of a buffer state is supposed to prevent the larger countries from fighting one another. - Capitalism: - An economic system in which individuals use wealth other than land to make a profit which they then invest to make more profit. Under capitalism the means of production are owned by individuals or corporations, and there is little government control over business. - Cartel: - A cooperative arrangement between countries intended to protect and promote mutual economic interest. - Cease-fire: - A temporary suspension of fighting. - Central Powers: - The name given to Germany, Australia, Turkey and Bulgaria, who fought as allies during the World War II. - Coalition Government: - A temporary alliance of political parties forming a government for combined action on a mutually agreed prograrnme. - Cold War: - The contest for power between the communist block headed by the erstwhile Soviet Union (Warsaw Pact) and the capitalist block headed by the United States (NATO) that began after the World War II. - Colonialism: - The policy or practice of acquiring full or partial political and territorial control over one country by another. - Comintern: - The Third International; a communist organisation. - Commercial Revolution: - Changes in the business life of Europe, brought about by increased trade with colonies overseas. It resulted in the introduction of new business methods, an increase in prices, and the growth of modern capitalism. - Communism: - A form of socialism, which in theory calls for the withering away of the state but in practice has resulted in the dictatorship of the communist party. - Compass: Instrument for showing directions, consisting of a needle or compass cord that points to the North Magnetic Pole. - Conscription: - Compulsory enlistment by governments for service in the armed forces. - Conservative Party: - A political party promoting free enterprise and private ownership; a major British party. - Convention: - An agreement between or among states covering particular matters, especially one less formal than a treaty. - Counter Reformation: - The efforts in the mid-16th century by the Roman Catholic Church to reform the abuses of the Catholic Church from within. The process was stimulated by the Protestant movements. - Coup: - A sudden, decisive act in politics, usually bringing about a change of government unlawfully or by force. - Covenant: - An agreement which brings about a relationship of commtitment between God and his people. - Cultural Revolution: - A political upheaval in China from 1966 to 1968, intended to bring about a return to revolutionary Maoist principles. - Decolonisation: - Withdrawal by a colonial power from its colony leaving it independent. - Democracy: - A form of government in which the power to rule rests with the people. - Depression: - The financial and industrial slump of 1929 and subsequent years which began in the USA and then affected the world economy. - Developed Countries: - Countries sufficiently advanced economically and socially. - Developing Countries: - Poor agricultural countries trying to become more advanced economically and socially. - Dictatorship: - Government by a ruler with total power over a country. - Diplomacy: - Skill of managing international relations. - Dominion: - Self-governing territories of the British Commonwealth. - Dyarchy: - Government by two independent authorities. - EEC: - European Economic Community, an economic association of Western European Countries set up by the Treaty of Rome (1957). - Enlightenment: - A European intellectual movement of the late 17th and 18th centuries emphasising on reason and individualism rather than on tradition. - Entente: - A friendly understanding or informal alliance between states or factions. - Factionalism: - Relating to political dissent by a small organised group within a larger one. - Fascism: - The principles or methods of a government or a political party favouring rule by a dictator, with strong control of industry and labour by the central government, great restrictions upon freedom of individuals, and extreme nationalism and militarism. - Federation: - The action of forming States into a single group with some kind of central organisation. - Feudal System: - The dominant social system in mediaeval Europe, in which the nobility held lands from the crown in exchange for military service, and vassals were in turn tenants of the nobles, while the peasants (serfs) were obliged to live on their lord's land and give him homage, labour and a share of the produce, notionally in exchange for military protection. - The Feudal system was common in Western Europe from about A.D. 900 to 1400. - Free enterprise: - An economic system based on the belief that businesses will thrive when they are allowed to operate with minimum of government interference or assistance. - GATT: - General Agreement on Trade and Tariff, an international treaty to promote trade and economic development by reducing tariffs and other restrictions. - Generation: - People born and living at about the same time. - Genocide: - The systematic extermination by a government of a racial, religious or national group living within the country. - Geocentric: - Pre-Copernican astronomical view, representing the earth as the centre of the universe. - Geopolitics: - Politics, especially international relations, as influenced by geographical factors. - Girondist: - A member of the moderate Republican Party in France. The Party was in power during 1791-93. - Glasnost: - A bold policy of openness instituted by Mikhail Gorbachev, leader of the Soviet Union in the 1980s, to promote economic growth and simulate discussion. - Globalisation: Integration of the world into a world village in all matters and for all practical purposes. - Hegemonism: - Dominance, especially by one state or social group over others. - Heliocentric: - Astronomical view of Copernicus that the sun is the centre of the solar system. - Historiography: - The art of writing history. - Holocaust: - A great destruction of life. It is a word used by the Jews to refer to Hitler's extermination of about 6 million European Jews in concentration camps during the World War II. - Humanism: - A Renaissance cultural movement which turned away from mediaeval scholasticism and revived interest in ancient Greek and Roman thought. - Humanitarianism: - Concerned with or seeking to promote human welfare. - Imperialism: - Policy of extending the rule or authority of one country over other countries, especially colonies. The term also connotes domination over another country’s economic, political and military structures without taking governmental control. - Iron-curtain: - An expression, first used by Winston Churchill in 1946. It refers to the difficulties imposed by the Soviet Union on the movement of ideas. goods and the people between the communist and the non- communist nations in Europe. - Liberalism: - The 19th century European doctrine of the middle class encouraging greater political participation and economic opportunity for members of their class. - Mandate: - A commission from the League of Nations to a‘member state to administer a territory. - Manor: - A unit of land, originally a feudal lordship, consisting of a lord’s demeshe and lands rented to tenants. - Marshall Plan: - A programme of financial aid and other initiatives, sponsored by the US after the World War II. - Massacre: Indiscriminate and brutal killing of people. - Menshevik: - Members of the Russian Revolutionary Marxist party. The name means the minority party. - Militarism: - The belief or desire of a government or people that a country should maintain a strong military capability and be prepared to use it aggressively to defend or promote national interests. - Missile: - A weapon that is self-propelled or directed at a target by remote control. - Modernisation: - Adapting to modern needs or habits, typically by installing modern equipment or adopting modern ideas or methods. - Monopoly: - The company or group having exclusive control over a commodity or service. - Movement: - A group of people working together to advance their shared political or social ideas. - Mujahideen: - Guerilla fighters in Islamic countries, especially those who are reéligiously motivated. - Multilateral: - Decision or policy agreed upon or participated in by three or more parties, especially the governments of different countries. - Multi-national companies: - Business firms of the industrial countries which operate in a number of other countries. - Multiracial State: - A State made up of people of many races. - Nationalism: - The sense of unity felt by people who share a common history, language and culture. - NATO: - North Atlantic Treaty Organistion. A military alliance formed in 1949, composed of the United States, Canada and the nations of Western Europe. - Nazis: - The National Socialist German Workers Party. - Neo-colonialism: - The use of economic, political, cultural or other pressures to control or influence other countries, especially former dependencies. - No-fly Zone: - An area over which aircraft are forbidden to fly, especially during a conflict. - OPEC: - Organisation of Petroleum Exporting Countries, primarily in the Middle East and North Africa, which jointly formulate oil- related policies. - Pact: - A formal agreement between individuals or parties. - Pagan: - A person holding religious beliefs other than those of the main world religions. - Pan-Arabism: - Belief that if the Arabs would unite, they could overcome the political domination of Britain and France and the cultural and economic domination of the West. - Peaceful co-existence: - A policy of economic competition rather than military confrontation between the Soviet Union and the United States. - Perestroika: - Restructuring policy developed along with Glasnost by the Soviet leader Mikhail Gorbachev in the middle of 1980s. - Pharmacology: - The branch of medicine concerned with the uses, effects and modes of action of drugs. - PLO: - Palestinian Liberation Organisation, an umbrella organisation for various Palestinian and Arab guerrilla groups. - Policy of Containment: - The action or policy of preventing the expansion of a hostile country or its influence. - Policy of Encirclement: - Policy of forming a cordon of allies around a country, pursued in international politics. - Proletariat: - The lowest class in economic and social status, including all agricultural, industrial and unskilled workers. - Protectorate: - A weak country under the protection and partial control of a strong country. - Protocol: - The original draft of a diplomatic document, especially the terms of a treaty agreed to in conference and signed by the parties. - Puritan: - A member of a group of English protestants of the late 16th and 17th centuries, who regarded the reformation of the English church under Queen Elizabeth I as incomplete and sought to simplify and regulate forms of worship. - Red Army: - The army of the Soviet Union, formed after the Revolution of 1917. The name was officially dropped in 1946. - Referendum: - A general vote by the electorate on a single political question which has been referred to them for a direct decision. - Refugees: - Persons who have been forced to leave their country for another in order to escape war, persecution or natural disasters. - Reich: - The former German state. The term is most often used to refer to the Third Reich. - Renaissance: - The revival of art and literature in Europe under the influence of classical models during 14th to 16th centuries. - Reparation: - Payments made by a defeated nation for the devastation caused in the territory of a victorious power during a war. - Republic: - A state in which the supreme power is held by the people and their elected representatives; and which has an elected or nominated president rather than a monarch. - Romanesque: - The style of architecture that was prevalent in Rome during the Renaissance period. - Sans Culottes: - A lower class Parisian republican during the French Revolution. - Satellite States: - Country nominally independent but actually controlled by a more powerful country, especially a country under the control of the Soviet Union. - Self-determination: - The right of people with a common background to form their own nation and to elect their own government. - Socialism: - An economic system in which the government owns the means of production. - Spheres of Influence: - Regions marked out by foreign powers, where their econornic and strategic interests are involved. - Spiritualism: - Spiritual practice, which believes in the existence of a supreme spirit as distinct from matter and aspires to have communion with it. - Statistics: - A fact sheet of data, obtained from the study of large quantity of numerical data. - Status quo: - Easting state of affairs; especially regarding equation of power in international relations. - Superpower: - A very powerful and influential nation. - Tank: - A heavy armoured fighting vehicle carrying guns and moving on a continuous articulated metal track. - Terrorist: - A person, who uses violence and tactics of intimidation in the pursuit of political goals. Developing countries of Asia, Africa and Latin America. - Third World: - A form of government in which the state claims absolute authority over the people. - Totalitarianism: - A form of government in which the state claims absolute authority over the poeple. - Triple Alliance: - Military alliance of Germany, Austria and Italy before the World War I. - Truman Doctrine: - The Principle that the US should give support to countries or p]eoples threatened by the Soviet forces OF communist insurrection. First expressed in 1947 by the US President H. Truman. - U-boat - A German submarine first used in the World War I. - Underdeveloped countries: - Economically and socially backward countries. - Unilateral: - The process of acting or reaching a decision or espousing a principle all by oneself. - Westernization: - The process of adopting or being influenced by the systerns of the West. - Whigs: - Members of the British pro-reform constitutional party that sought the supremacy of Parliament. They were eventually succeeded in the 19th century by the Liberal Party. - WTO: - World Trade Organisation, An inter-national organisation founded in 1995 to promote international trade and economic development by reducing tariffs and other restrictions. - Yalta Conference: - The meeting held in Russia in February 1945 at which F. D. Roosevelt, W. Churchill and J. Stalin committed the US, Great Britain and the Soviet Union to help European nations after the World War II. - Zionism: - A movement for the re-establishment and development and the protection of a Jewish nation.
